#### A.3 Project/Programme Executive Summary [max.300 words]

1. The Kyrgyz Republic's Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDCs) under the Paris Agreement commits the country to reducing, by 2060, its greenhouse gas emissions by 35–37 percent through international assistance. By investing in rangeland and forestry management, strengthening national and local institutions, introducing market-driven incentives for sustainable land use, and promoting climate-resilient livelihoods, the Kyrgyz Republic will achieve these reductions. These reductions represent 1.76 percent of the country's total emissions, and 22.6 percent of the agricultural share of emissions.

2. The project will achieve these sequestration results while capitalizing important co-benefits from adaptation and disaster risk reduction through: supporting government’s on-going efforts to harmonize relevant policies and legal frameworks; strengthening local institutions for sustainable land use management, including the development of locally integrated rangeland and forestry resource planning built around forest, rangeland and livestock management practices (Component 1); increasing the resilience of rural households by providing them with access to a range of household income earning activities (Component 2); facilitating market opportunities that provide the financial incentives, funded by the Russian Kyrgyz Republic and KfW, for resource users to adopt and implement sustainable land use practices in order to reduce their national carbon footprint. The project will be implemented due to their high level of exposure and sensitivity to climate change stressors, mitigation potential, human poverty and dependency on natural resources.

3. The 49.99 million US$ investment will achieve carbon sequestration at a cost of US$ 2.5 per tCO2eq, while also indirectly strengthening the resilience and improving the livelihoods of 90,000 households (8.9 percent of total county population) through improved access to markets for forest products, increased income from sustainable land use dependency communities on direct uses of resources (i.e., wood and pasture) and improving their livelihoods through benefits derived from ecosystem services.

4. The harmonized legislative and legal frameworks, enhanced institutional systems and local procedures will enable the natural capital and carbon sequestration investments through forest and rangeland management, and increase the effectiveness of additional domestic expenditures in reaching the country’s INDC targets.

### Financing

The project will be funded by a GCF grant for the amount of US$ 29.99 million, or 60 percent of the total FCR. The GCF grant will finance:
(i) 100 percent of the evidence-based strengthening of NRM governance Component (US $ 5.2 million);
(ii) 91 percent of the green investments for forest and rangeland rehabilitation Component (US$ 22.48 million); and
(iii) 73 percent of the project management Component (US$ 1.42 million). An estimated 92.7 percent of the GCF budget (US$ 24.82 million) will be devoted to mitigation result area interventions (i.e., reduced emissions from forest land use and development, improved livelihoods for vulnerable people, and increased resilience of communities and regions), while 3.1 percent or US$ 0.38 billion to increased resilience of most vulnerable people, communities and regions; and about 4.3 percent or US$ 3.9 million to the project management component.

The GCF grant mechanism is an innovative catalytic element of the co-financing components which assists by enabling a more efficient use of resources in the project. The GCF grant will finance the following outputs:
(i) Output 1.1: Strengthened institutions and systems for NRM governance;
(ii) Output 2.1: Strengthened institutional and system frameworks for forest management of land use change and development.
The project will create an integrated policy and legal framework through technical, legal and institutional approaches to advance public-private partnership in integrated natural resources management (e.g., Output 1.1 and Output 2.1). Under the public-private partnership, the GCF grant and co-financings will provide investment environment and opportunities for

### RKFQ co-financing amounts US$ 15,0 million, or 30 percent of the total C&F-KOF funds. The co-finance will cover (7) climate change mitigation-sensitive value chain development Component (US$ 15,0 million). Such co-finance is critical to the project's theory of change, as they contribute to the required (a) economic diversification from the prevailing reliance on forest products and livestock production; (b) increased income generation for smallholder farmers in rangelands (i.e. by developing non timber forest products value chains); and (c) transition towards a modern economy less dependent on natural resource extraction. The project will also support the establishment of a national carbon stock accounting system (Annex 3) and the project has the potential to enhance carbon sequestration as a long-term mitigation strategy, while supporting the development of forest products value chains that are committed to similar mitigation objectives.

10. RKFQ will provide its contributions as wholesale credits to local banks which will on lend to entrepreneurs beneficiaries (‘End-borrowers’). Wholesale loans to the banks will be provided at about 3 percent p.a. in US$ and 6 percent p.a. in ARIS, with a maximum term of 5 years (with an option for extension by the bank for a further period of term for a total of approx. 3-4 years). RKFQ already has partner arrangements and experience in working with 13 local commercial banks in the country. The project will also provide direct credit to the End-borrowers at 5 percent p.a. in US$ and 10 percent p.a. in ARIS.

11. The final selection of End-borrowers will be carried out by KOF under the oversight of FAO, reviewed by the Project Steering Committee and finally confirmed by AE during the project implementation. Contractual agreements with selected beneficiaries will be executed directly between the banks and the beneficiaries (with a guarantee from RKFQ co-financing contract). RKFQ manages and oversees the loan fund, while FAO will oversee the implementation of activities related to the establishment of the national carbon stock accounting system. The project will also provide technical grants in the project activities sequentially as a co-funding of the grant component. The timeline of RKFQ financing is expected to be 3 years (2015-2017) with an option for extension by the bank for another year.

12. The project intervention areas, this will result in important contributions to enhanced resilience of target communities.

As shown in table B.1.2 above, component 3 represents the bulk of the investment for carbon sequestration. Among the investment in component 2, the majority is for afforestation / reforestation / forest enrichment, with a lower share on rangeland rehabilitation. While the largest share of carbon sequestration is from rangeland, the costs for forestry are higher and thus the contribution to the total project cost is smaller than that of Component 3 which has more direct benefits.

The EAFR (Chapter 7 of the Feasibility Study in Annexes 2, and detailed in Annex 3) demonstrates that without the project, the forest sector would not be able to achieve its targets for carbon sequestration and other environmental benefits. Similarly, there is limited scope for private sector investment (i.e., from the financial market) as the returns are too long and too low to justify investments in this sector. The EAFR also demonstrates that the project will have a positive impact on the environment.

Nevertheless, Component 1 efforts on the enabling environment (policy and regulatory framework) will generate accompanying benefits for the forest sector. Investment and Component 2 will support in diversifying from the prevailing unsustainable livestock practices.

## B.3 Financial Markets Overview (if applicable)

20. In order to support the sustainability of carbon sequestration investment in forests and rangeland, with USD 11.6 million in co-financing as senior loans from the RKDF, the project will activate special credit lines for project-relevant value chains and projects. The loans will be provided to entrepreneurs at a fixed interest rate of 5 percent per annum (p.a.) on eligible value chains. The loans will be provided to entrepreneurs at 5 percent p.a. in USD and at 10 percent in local currency, with the option for borrowers to repay the loan in local currency. End-borrowers will pay interest only during the first four years of the loan term (at a fixed rate of 5% per annum) and will be subject to interest rates (Credit Information Bureau - see Chapter 3 of the Feasibility Study, rural finance section). Wholesale loans will be provided at a floating interest rate linked to the LIBOR rate. The project will also provide a project concept to support investment towards reducing unsustainable use of forests and rangelands. The RKDF loans will specifically target small and medium sized enterprises that need larger loans as compared to those typically offered in the market by commercial banks.

# C.1. Strategic Context

## Climate Scenario

The Kyrgyz Republic is a low-income but landlocked country (LIFDC) with a population of nearly 6 million, of which two-thirds are rural dwellers. Agriculture is one of the key economic sectors in the Kyrgyz Republic, with 48% of labour force dedicated to this sector and almost 15% of GDP coming from it.

Kyrgyzstan is a landlocked country with many tall mountains, glaciers, and high-altitude lakes. Ninety-three percent of the territory lies above an elevation of 200 meters (660 feet), three-quarters of that under permanent snow or glaciers. At lower altitudes, the dominant climate type is continental semi-arid, while at higher elevations it is subpolar. The average temperature difference between summer and winter seasons: With increasing altitude, average temperatures decrease while humidity increases such as in the mountains.

The Third National Communication to the UNFCCC and additional literature show that small but statistically significant changes have been observed in climate variables over the past 50 years. For example, mean annual air temperature has increased over the period 1960-2010 has been increasing at a rate of 0.02°C/yr, reaching a rise of 0.07°C/year since 1980. The average precipitation in Kyrgyzstan is about 450 mm per year. However, the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events have increased over time.

## Precipitation

Precipitation projections suggest an increase in accumulated rainfall of between 12% (west) and 18% (northeast) under RCP8.5 compared to historical values. The projected change is within the range of 30% (west) and 26% (north). Annual precipitation has increased by about 4 mm/year since 1970, with a rate of increase of 0.08 mm/year. Monthly rainfall projections for the period 2000-2050 indicate an increase in monthly rainfall over most parts of Kyrgyzstan.

## Temperature

The average temperature has increased by about 0.3°C since 1970, with a rate of increase of 0.06°C/year. The projected change in annual ground temperature is 0.34°C.


As reported in the feasibility study (Chapter 1), the interactions between changing weather patterns, the impact of climate change on land use and land cover activities, has led to an increase in damage from extreme weather events and a growing threat to the future provisioning of ecosystem services. The number of days with snow cover above 500 meters has decreased by about 24% since 1970. The number of weather related disasters has increased by six times and that about 70 percent of these events have occurred in the last decade (UNISDR, 2013). The average duration of snow cover is decreasing at a rate of 5 days/year. The frequency of extreme weather related emergencies was above average; causing 1.8 billion KGS in damages (approximately US$23 million). Over the past two decades, there has been an increase in the number and intensity of forest fires, degradation and deforestation of vegetation and along waterways from wildfires overgrowing. The growing volume of rainfall in the spring and summer months has led to increased soil erosion and loss of fertile topsoil. The decrease in cover vegetation, has resulted in the increased frequency and intensity of flash floods and landslides (UNISDR 2013).

As the climate continues to change, the changes and seasonal redistribution of rainfall and rising temperatures will continue to have a significant impact on land use and land cover activities. In addition, as the warming trend is not allowed by physical features, forest biomes will shift upwards in elevation, although into an ever-decreasing surface area. The specific composition of grassland communities will also change in response to temperature increase and the decrease

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund Proposal (Page 8 of 9)

### Introduction to the Project/Programme

The detailed project description outlines a proposal for addressing climate change impacts on ecosystems and land use management in Kyrgyzstan. It highlights the increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, such as droughts and floods, which are attributed to climate change. The project aims to enhance resilience by improving water resource management, reducing greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture, and promoting sustainable land use practices.

### Key Objectives

1. **Enhance Water Resource Management**: Implement strategies to manage water resources more effectively in the face of changing precipitation patterns.
2. **Reduce Greenhouse Gas Emissions**: Focus on reducing emissions from livestock farming and agricultural activities through improved practices and technologies.
3. **Promote Sustainable Land Use Practices**: Encourage sustainable land use management that reduces deforestation, soil erosion, and other environmental degradation.

### Background

- **Climate Change Impact**: Kyrgyzstan is highly vulnerable to climate change due to its mountainous terrain and dependence on agriculture for livelihoods.
- **Kyrgyzstan's Climate Risk Index**: Ranked 81st out of 195 countries in terms of vulnerability to climate change, with a score of 3.60.

### Project Scope

The project will cover the following areas:

- **Water Resource Management**: Strengthening water infrastructure and improving irrigation systems.
- **Agricultural Sector**: Promoting sustainable agricultural practices that reduce emissions from livestock farming and improve soil health.
- **Forest Management**: Protecting and restoring forests to enhance carbon sequestration.

### Implementation Strategy

The project will be implemented through a combination of policy reforms, capacity building for local communities, and the adoption of new technologies. It aims to achieve significant reductions in greenhouse gas emissions while enhancing overall resilience to climate change impacts.

### Conclusion

This proposal seeks to address the urgent need for action on climate change by focusing on key sectors where interventions can have a substantial impact. By reducing emissions from agriculture and promoting sustainable land use practices, Kyrgyzstan can contribute significantly to global efforts to mitigate climate change and adapt to its effects.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal (Page 9 of 9)

### Country Economic Background

#### Key Points:
- The economy of Kyrgyzstan highly depends on services (56%), industry (29%), and agriculture (15%) with a GNI per capita of US$3,100 in 2018.
- Over 70% of the population lives in rural settlements.

#### Debt Sustainability Analysis:
- The IMF's 2017 assessment of Kyrgyz Republic indicates moderate risk of debt distress.
- High public debt and a high debt service ratio are major risks to debt sustainability, leading to real GDP growth and deterioration of fiscal balance.

#### Poverty and Inequality:
- Poverty level is high with 25.4% of the population living below minimum subsistence level in 2016.
- The highest poverty rates are found among rural households (38%) compared to urban areas (27%).

#### Land Use and Sustainability:
- Scarce arable land combined with underdeveloped irrigation, limited off-farm employment opportunities, distance from markets, and poor infrastructure hinder economic development in rural areas.

#### Livestock and Traditional Practices:
- Livestock rearing is a long-standing tradition of Kyrgyz people.
- Before being fully settled by the Soviets in the mid-20th century, nomadic herders grazed their livestock on vast tracts of land.
- Sustainable transhumant grazing was lost during the Soviet times when herders were prohibited to use more than three plots of land and were forced into sedentary lifestyles.

#### Climate Change Impact:
- A detailed reference of climate change impact is found in Chapter 1 of the Feasibility Study (Page 20).

#### Forest Degradation:
- Among the key drivers of forest degradation are overexploitation and over grazing, resulting in livestock and water management of natural resources exacerbated by negative impacts of climate change.
- Forest's level of degradation is estimated at 35% with a total area of 1.4 million hectares.

#### Rangeland Management:
- Traditional practices have been slowly recovering since the Soviets' prohibition on nomadic herding.
- Modern livestock farming today ranges along the rangelands not only to graze livestock but also for other purposes such as tourism and recreation.
- Pastoral-based grazing has a core of Kyrgyz traditional pastoral practices, which have been incorporated in recent pastures.

### 41. In the last ten years, animal inventories have increased by 41 percent with cattle and sheep – jointly representing 80 percent of the total stocks - spiking by 37 and 68 percent respectively. The main drivers of growth are low annual productivity and a high level of literacy of rural residents who perceive livestock as both a source of cash income and a means of savings accumulation.

### 42. Animal husbandry has been traditionally the main source of livelihood for rural population with farming households generating 95% of all red meat in the country. Livestock is especially important in remote mountainous areas, where crop production is not possible due to poor soil quality and lack of water resources. Livestock is also used as a means of transport and food during the dry season. In the last ten years, animal inventories have increased by 41 percent with cattle and sheep – jointly representing 80% of total stocks - spiking by 37 and 68% respectively.

### The main drivers of growth are low annual productivity, non-diversified economies and low financial literacy (as well as traditional attitudes) of rural residents who perceive livestock as both a source of cash income and a means of savings accumulation. In the last ten years, animal inventories have increased by 41 percent with cattle and sheep – jointly representing 80% of total stocks - spiking by 37 and 68% respectively.

### 43. Almost all livestock is grazed in pastures year-round. Daily grazing occurs in pastures near villages during the fallow season, while animals are moved to higher altitudes for grazing during the rainy season. This practice results in large seasonal variations in animal body weights which indicate that animal feeding is geared towards annual survival rather than long-term productivity.

### 45. Degraded pastures and inefficient production systems have resulted in animal breeding non-profitably. Animal owners are trapped in intermediaries' network, relations of mistrust and difficulties to reach up the formal market weaken the economic potential of livestock. The majority of herders are not able to access markets for their products because they graze pastures inefficiently (this is the case of the vast majority of herders, except for the most advanced PULS that apply good animal husbandry practices). This situation has been exacerbated by the current global economic crisis and high market and aggressive competition on prices by animal traders (intermediaries), the latter already akin to at price growth. The lack of access to markets is also due to the fact that the majority of herders are not able to meet the quality standards set by the formal market, which results in low margins of intermediaries.

### 46. Farmers' knowledge and experience are key factors for improving their livelihoods. However, limited local knowledge on how to improve their production practices and how to factor in climate change into their natural resource management strategies is a major challenge. The lack of technical assistance from the private sector and the fact that donor support and limited assistance had been delivered to local administrators to support rural economy in the described period has also contributed to this situation.

### 47. Considering that only before off-farmers can afford technical assistance from private extension services in a business context, it is important for them to have access to such services as well. However, the lack of such services makes their activity and non-sustainable in time.

### 48. Documented delays with difficulty in implementation are jeopardizing the positive impact of investment's operations designed to address issues related to climate change and degradation of natural resources. Failure to address these issues will have a negative impact on the increased degradation of natural resources further compromising the net carbon sequestration profile of the County.

### 49. In recent years, climate change and accompanied destruction of ecosystems have deteriorated natural resource management strategies in the County. The lack of technical assistance from private extension services has made it difficult for farmers to use their resources appropriately and efficiently - cannot cope with this situation. In this context, climate change is a major challenge that needs to be addressed by all stakeholders. It is important to consider climate change as a main element in its framework which is a multi-layered approach including the following three elements: 1. Promote top-down activities from national and county governments to ensure that all relevant stakeholders participate and cooperate horizontally; 2. Promote bottom-up activities from local communities and connect them with the global network.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Alignment to Relevant National Policies and Strategies

50. As reported in the feasibility study (Chapter 1), to cope with the described scenario without compromising its economic and social development objectives, the country has developed a series of policy frameworks that find their place within the national climate change strategy (NCCS). The NCCS is based on three pillars: adaptation, mitigation, and reporting and described from where a cross cutting approach is desirable to ensure maximization of climate resilience. The National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) was developed in 2015 for the Forests and Biodiversity sector and the draft Conclus of Forestry Development 2040 aim to reduce poverty of the forest communities by 10 percent, increase forest cover from 7 to 8.5 percent.

51. Kyrgyzstan’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) is currently being developed in close collaboration with the Government and the SAEPF in development of the National Policy for the Adoption to Climate Change (NPACC). NACC is a partner in the implementation of the NCCS, and the NACC has been involved in the preparation of the NCCAP and the CS-FOR proposal. However, while there are several coordination mechanisms available in the country, there is a lack of institutional capacity for effective coordination between different stakeholders.

52. Although Kyrgyzstan’s IRDC is among the few reporting cost estimates related to adaptation and mitigation, the document provides limited information in regards of strategies and approaches established or envisaged to ensure climate resilience. The NCCS is based on three pillars: adaptation, mitigation, and reporting and described from where a cross cutting approach is desirable to ensure maximization of climate resilience.

53. To support the implementation of the NCCS, the following scenarios were developed (Figure C-4): Scenario 1: low population growth / high economic growth; Scenario 2: average population growth / moderate economic growth; and Scenario 3: high population growth / low economic growth. The Kyrgyz Republic’s contribution to mitigation will be to reduce GHG emissions in the range of 11.49–13.75 percent below BAU in 2030, compared with a BAU scenario that would see total emissions increase by 68.5 percent above current levels.

54. In terms of mitigation targets monitoring, the NDC confirms that the domestic MRV system will be developed and implemented as part of the national climate change strategy (NCCS). The NDC also states that it is expected to be supported by the national communications on climate change and biennial update reports. The NDC is not yet developed and there are no plans for its development in 2015, but it will be submitted as part of the country’s costs related to the three identified scenarios assessing total financial needs in more than US$ 1.8 billion.

55. Climate change mitigation and adaptation are also addressed in a series of key policy frameworks and strategies, including the National Forest Strategy (NFS) for 2013–2020; the National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan (NBSAP) for 2014-2025; the draft Conclus of Forestry Development 2040 aim to reduce poverty of the forest communities by 10 percent, increase forest cover from 7 to 8.5 percent.

56. Within the framework of the Pilot Programme for Climate Resilience (PPCR), the Government has started developing the Country Forest Climate Change Strategy (CFCC), including a Climate Finance Centre (CFC) in 2013. It is expected that the CFCC and CFC will become fully operational by 2015, with the aim of supporting the country’s efforts to reduce GHG emissions and increase climate resilience.

57. The NDC also states that it is expected to be supported by the national communications on climate change and biennial update reports. The proposed project will address these gaps by providing a more decentralized approach that is determined commitments and reducing both exposure and vulnerability of communities and national economy.

64. Particularly, for the CS-FOR project, given the presented content and relevant policy frameworks, data and analysis will be used to identify priority areas in terms of forest cover and land use change. The selection process will target areas according to the following criteria: (a) mitigation potential in terms of forest and pasture rehabilitation; (b) type and extent of degradation; (c) vulnerability of ecosystems and communities to climate change; (d) high dependency of communities on natural resources, including forests and pastures; (e) willingness of local communities and resource users to participate in the national engineering process. The NDA and the FAO identified the four contiguous districts of Ak- Tash, Kosh-Agach, Osh, and Sary-Chelek.

65. Within the four districts selected, the project has identified priority areas (hotspots) where, according to the criteria above, there is a high potential for forest rehabilitation and restoration, as well as other ecosystem services such as those provided by pastures and forests (i.e., protection, livelihood, water) benefiting communities in terms of food security. The selected areas are characterized by: (a) presence of land cover of at least 1,000 hectares; and (d) agreement of communities for reducing pressure on identified areas.

66. Leshaikos are Forest enterprises in charge of the total management of State Forest Fund territory. They are also responsible for the protection and conservation of forest resources as well as the sustainable use of forest land, buffer zones and land for future afforestation. The land classified under the latter is often used as grazing areas and is subject to a rotational grazing system. This system has been in place since 1920s and is still in effect today. It consists of dividing the area into several plots that are rotated on an annual basis, with each plot being grazed for one year before the rotation of the area. ### Project Areas and Forests

87. In the Project Areas there are five State Forest Enterprises, one Forestry Unit, and two National Natural Parks which together cover an area of approximately 205,000 ha. The project will address forest management in the pre-determined expansion area, including forest / rangeland forestry.

### Objectives

87a. The project aims to improve the capacity of Kyrgyzstan’s State Forest Enterprises and Forestry Units for sustainable forest management through understanding and knowledge of the local forest resources (trees, nuts, fruits and shrubs for grazing), and their role in climate change mitigation and adaptation.

87b. The project will also address the needs and strategies identified by the Kyrgyz Republic in the INDC and in the NAP roadmap of 2017 with an approach that maximizes mitigation while capitalizing important co-benefits from adaptation and disaster risk reduction (DRR). This includes the implementation of the National Strategy for Disaster Risk Reduction, Environmental and Climate Change Policy (Kyrgyz Republic 2013-2017 National Sustainable Development Strategy), and the Kyrgyz Republic’s NAP roadmap. The project will also be aligned with the UNFCCC COP21 Paris Agreement and the Program and Action Plan for Adaptation to Climate Change for the Agricultural Sector (2016-2020).

89a. Coordination of ongoing relevant projects and strategies.

### Frameworks of the Sustainable and Climate Sensitive Land Use for Economic Development in Central Asia project in Kyrgyzstan.

The GCF facilitated establishing the Coordination and Consultative Council at the national level, comprised of representatives from government agencies, civil society organizations, and private sector. The Council reviews policy issues related to the forestry sector. The project has initiated piloting of new management approaches in six pilot sites: two forest landscapes (Kok-Tash and Kosh-Agach), one mountainous forest landscape (Chon-Kemin), and three land-use systems (Wetlands, Walnut Forests and Pastures in Southern Kyrgyzstan) (2015-2018) is another GCF project focusing on six pilot Landscapes.

### Coordination and synergies are established with the SAEPP-implemented Integrated Forest Ecosystem Management Project (2017-2021), with World Bank and Global Environment Facility (GEF) funding for US$ 16.1 million, which aims to improve forest governance and increase investment in public goods enabling activities for (a) sustainable forest management, (b) climate change adaptation, and (c) biodiversity conservation.

CS-FOR and FEM will also support the GCF-funded Forest Ecosystem Management Project facility* managed by the World Bank and implemented by the SAEPP in the country, for all aspects related to forest governance and increasing investment in public goods enabling activities. CS-FOR will also work with the facility* coordinating with the relevant ongoing GCF-funded Livelihoods and Market Development Project and the two World Bank funded projects (FEM and Forest Ecosystem Management Project) as well as other relevant projects.

### Specific complementarities are around the common aim to improve productivity of range-dependent communities.

CS-FOR adds value by adopting an integrated approach to rangeland rehabilitation (focusing also on State Forest Funds areas) that limits the pressure on rangelands thus increasing the sustainability of investments and ensuring long-term benefits for rural communities. CS-FOR will focus its operations at the national level, while FEM will operate at local level, where CS-FOR ensures complementarity in investment and harmonization of approaches.

### 1. Climate Change Adaptation

CS-FOR will contribute to climate change adaptation by focusing on the most vulnerable populations exposed to climate change through a GCF Funding Proposal (Funding Proposal No. 2018-034) that aims at increasing resilience of rural communities in Kyrgyzstan through Diversification of Climatic Sensitive Livelihoods. This project – expected to be discussed at GCF B20 will contribute to the Sustainable Management of Mountain Forest and Land Resources under Climate Change Conditions (Sustainable Management of Mountain Forest and Land Resources under Climate Change Conditions) that was pioneered also by the GIZ and SAEPP Regional Project on Ecosystem-based Adaptation to Climate Change in Kyrgyzstan. CS-FOR will also support the development of a national strategy for climate change adaptation to climate change. In addition, CS-FOR will also interact with the activities of the ongoing GEF-UNDP Project.

### 2. On value chains and agribusiness support

CS-FOR will collaborate with the GCF-funded Access to Markets Project (Funding Proposal No. 2018-035) that aims at increasing access to markets for forest products, providing technical support to private sector enterprises working in CS-FOR target areas and their suppliers. The common aim will be to increase the competitiveness of these enterprises by improving their market knowledge and access to new markets for their products, with improved and more equitable returns. CS-FOR will add value by focusing on simultaneously increasing the productivity of forest resources and enhancing the resilience of rural communities through carbon sequestration from forests and rangelands. CS-FOR will coordinate also with the ongoing and future support from EBRD and GIZ to promote a Green Growth and Sustainable Development in Kyrgyzstan: Economic Transition approach aimed at increasing the volume of financing with focus on environmental benefits.

### Building on existing towards the implementation

*Funding Proposal No. 2018-035: Access to Markets Project (AM) which is the ensuing entity created by CS-FOR through its implementation.

73. Within the framework of the GEF4FA project ("Sustainable management of mountainous forest and land resources under climate change conditions"), reforestation activities were carried out in 6 pilot Leskhores with broad participation of local youth, women and men. The main objective was to increase the area covered by trees and shrubs, including grasses, reduce the area of degraded lands, improving and maintaining soil fertility, allowing communities to diversify their livelihoods.

74. One of the most important lessons learned through the implementation of this GEF sponsored project is the interest of local communities in identifying innovative opportunities for forest resources management, allowing to create new forest-based enterprises and jobs, as well as the importance of involving women and youth in such activities, helping to solve social and economic problems in rural areas of the country.

75. In line with the above lessons learned, the project proposes to develop a national criteria and indicator system for sustainable forest management through the capacity-building project "Accountability Systems for Sustainable Forest Management" (LNEEC / FAO Project). The objective is to provide a set of tools that will allow countries to monitor the sustainable management of the state forest fund, specially protected natural areas and hunting grounds. This tool will be based on the following criteria:

76. The objective of the work carried out within the framework of the LNEEC / FAO project "Accountability Systems for Sustainable Forest Management" is to develop a set of tools that will allow countries to monitor the sustainable management of their state forest fund, specially protected natural areas and hunting grounds through the use of criteria characterizing the performance of the main functions of state forest management and the results of the forest resources management. The project will also provide an evaluation methodology.

77. During the design, and in anticipation of the project's activities, FAO has also complemented the analysis of the forest coverage with satellite images and produced a detailed Atlas (Annex 6). Before the FAO Forest Assessment, about 60 percent of the state-administered forests were monitored, leaving a significant information gap. Moreover, inventories focused more on timber resources than on other ecosystem services provided by forests such as water regulation, soil conservation and socio-economically.

The Forest Assessment aimed to help the Government design and carry out a comprehensive forest inventory that would provide an accurate picture of the country's forest resources in terms of quantity and quality. The assessment was carried out in two phases, in partnership with the State Agency for Environmental Protection and Forestry and with additional funding from the European Union. The Forest Assessment was launched by the President of Kyrgyzstan. The project brought together major stakeholders involved in forest management: civil society, NGOs, forest services, scientists, line ministries and international partners, to agree on a sector-integrated approach to assess the country's forest resources and develop an action plan for their sustainable use.

The Forest Assessment provided a comprehensive picture of the country's forests. It also helped to better understanding of the relationship between plant biodiversity and carbon storage. The stakeholders also reached consensus on the need to establish a national forest monitoring system.

### C2. Project / Programme Objective against Baseline

#### Project baseline

78. The project baseline for this proposal was established using nationally and locally collected data, including:

(i) Literature review (1); (ii) questionnaire-based household survey (project sites 600, control sites 300); (iii) community focus group discussions; (iv) key informant interviews.

The project baseline includes both target and control areas selected through the national engagement process used in preparation of this proposal. The baseline data were collected during the period from January to June 2014. The baseline data show the progressive impacts achieved through prior implementation, and the ability to document changes in conditions between baseline and project end.

79. The intervention areas of the project are described in detail in Chapter I of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2). Analysis of the data collected during the baseline survey shows that the target communities have a high vulnerability to climate change impacts, as well as other factors such as poverty, lack of access to markets and limited land tenure security. The main parameters and related hazards are presented in the Project Atlas (Annex 6). In carrying out the analysis, the design team used the following criteria for selecting areas: (i) high vulnerability; (ii) low capacity for adaptation assessments to identify the project's core target areas. Target areas were selected according to the following criteria:

a. Exposure of ecosystems and communities to natural (rapid onset) hazards triggered by climate change;

b. Vulnerability of ecosystems and communities to slow (latent) climate change;

| **Significant change in the resilience of target communities and activities** | **The project will contribute to significant changes in the resilience of target communities and activities.** | **The country will not only achieve its climate goals but also enhance the resilience of target communities and activities through improved forest management practices, increased carbon sequestration, and sustainable land use.** |
| **Limited local capacity for forest management and conservation efforts leading to degradation of forests and loss of biodiversity** | **The project aims to strengthen local capacity for forest management and conservation by providing training, resources, and support for sustainable forest management practices.** | **The country will have a more capable workforce with the skills needed to manage and conserve its forests effectively, leading to improved forest health and biodiversity.** |
| **Limited access to markets for forest products and limited economic opportunities in target communities** | **The project will create new market opportunities by developing green value chains under community ownership and promoting sustainable forest product production.** | **The country will see increased economic opportunities as the local population gains access to new markets, leading to improved livelihoods and reduced poverty rates.** |
| **Limited knowledge of climate change impacts on forests and limited capacity for adaptation efforts in target communities** | **The project will provide education and training on climate change impacts and adaptation strategies, empowering communities with the necessary skills to adapt to changing conditions.** | **The country will have a more informed and prepared population capable of adapting to climate change impacts, leading to reduced vulnerability and enhanced resilience.** |

90. The chart shows that the current level of forest degradation under BAU scenario generates an estimated net loss of 1.5 m tCO2eq in 20 years; through the combined effect of the various interventions, the project will be able to avoid the removal of 38.4 m tCO2eq (of which 9.6 m tCO2eq from forest degradation and 28.8 m tCO2eq from deforestation), for a net effect of 19.8 m tCO2eq sequestered. Such expected avoided emissions are equivalent to the removal of 34,000 cars emitting 50 tons of CO2 per year or 67,000 trees.

91. One of the main and most innovative elements of the project is the development of green value chains under community ownership in order to create economic opportunities with limited risk, in order to decrease pressure on and degradation of forests in the project intervention areas; this will result in important contributions to enhanced resilience of target communities.

# C3. Project / Programme Description

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 20 OF 99

### Project objective and project indicators

1. In line with the national policies and regulatory framework in the fields of climate change and natural resources management, the project aims to contribute to the development of a low-carbon economy and climate-resilient economy, while capitalizing important co-benefits from adaptation and disaster risk reduction.
2. The project objective is to increase carbon sequestration through supporting climate investments in forests and other land-based ecosystems, including forest restoration and sustainable management practices, as well as ecosystem-based sustainable management of natural resources and green growth investments.

### Context

1. Government policies have been in place for decades to protect the environment but there has always been a problem of free riders.
2. As a countermeasure to such a situation, traditional natural resource management aimed at protecting forests and other land-based ecosystems was introduced (e.g., by establishing a forest reserve system and adopting a usage method with less environmental impact), and also provided a framework to bear the cost of maintenance and development.

### In context

1. It is crucial to consider that at country level there is no unified vision and policy on management of lands and ecosystems, this leaves the management of intrinsically linked ecosystem resources such as rangeland and forestland in a fragmented manner.
2. The 2015 Forest Law Reform Act (FLRA) was enacted by the Government of Nepal to address this issue and started an effective process of reform, putting pastoralists at the center.
3. While such reform has achieved substantial results, it is still not sufficient for the sustainable management of rangeland and forestland resources in Nepal.

### Natural resource management framework

1. The FLRA provides a new framework for natural resource management especially with regard to forest land and forest resources, often adjacent to rangelands.
2. On the other hand, the Forest Act (2074) is the main legal instrument governing forests and forest enterprises, and aims to maximize forest potential and extension more than potential co-benefits with other resources.
3. While the FLRA provides a new framework for natural resource management especially with regard to forest land and forest resources, often adjacent to rangelands.

### Joint Management

1. The 2015 Forest Law Reform Act (FLRA) was enacted by the Government of Nepal to address this issue and started an effective process of reform, putting pastoralists at the center.
2. While such reform has achieved substantial results, it is still not sufficient for the sustainable management of rangeland and forestland resources in Nepal.

### Rangeland Forest Law

1. The 2015 Forest Law Reform Act (FLRA) was enacted by the Government of Nepal to address this issue and started an effective process of reform, putting pastoralists at the center.
2. While such reform has achieved substantial results, it is still not sufficient for the sustainable management of rangeland and forestland resources in Nepal.

### Project components

1. The project will facilitate the development of a new legal framework for natural resource management that incorporates lessons learned from a number of initiatives (clinics), government and non-government organizations.
2. The project will also support the Government to develop a national strategy on climate change adaptation, which will be based on the evidence of best practices in rangeland forest management.

### Project activities

1. In addition to the above, the project will facilitate the development of a new legal framework for natural resource management that incorporates lessons learned from a number of initiatives (clinics), government and non-government organizations.
2. The project will also support the Government to develop a national strategy on climate change adaptation, which will be based on the evidence of best practices in rangeland forest management.

### Components

The project's investments and activities will be executed through three technical components (such as a "Component"):

1. **Evidence-Based Strengthening of Natural Resources Management (NRM) Governance:**
2. **Green Investments for Forest and Rangeland Rehabilitation:**
3. **Climate-sensitive Value Chains Development:**

### Component 1: Evidence-Based Strengthening of Natural Resources Management Governance

#### 102. Building the long-term capacity of the Government to provide Kyrgyzstan with an enabling environment that supports investment for carbon sequestration through forest and rangeland management while providing evidence-based governance mechanisms for NRM is a key objective of this project. This will be achieved by building on the existing legal framework, which provides a strong basis in Kyrgyzstan to preserve forest-rangeland ecosystem's natural resources so as to improve governance in their management and use.

#### The current enabling environment stimulates innovative practices and investments that conserve and regenerate these resources. The current enabling environment is also characterized by a lack of coordination between the different stakeholders involved, including local communities, private sector actors, and government agencies. This results in fragmented land-use planning and management arrangements for forests and rangelands on State Forest Fund (SFF) lands are managed differently than the 9 million ha of municipal public forest lands.

#### The management, use, and protection arrangements for the resources within the SFF are regulated by the Forest Code of Kyrgyzstan. However, there is a lack of coordination between the different stakeholders involved in the management of communal rangelands and forests on SFF lands. This results in fragmented land-use planning and management arrangements.

#### The current enabling environment also lacks legal frameworks for private sector participation in forest and rangeland management and by different agencies, and lack of legal framework for private-public partnerships in management of natural resources. As a result, the existing regulatory framework fails to support effective and sustainable arrangements for the conservation and regeneration of forests and rangelands on SFF lands.

#### For example, the current enabling environment does not provide sufficient incentives or investment to reduce the impact of climate-related stresses on natural resources (see Chapter 1, Feasibility Study). Several factors contribute to this lack of investment in forest and rangeland management. These include a lack of legal frameworks for private sector participation in forest and rangeland management.

#### As an example, the current enabling environment is not supportive of the FEL's current baseline situation that is the policy and governance mechanisms in NRM are segmented and limits further inclusion of climate risk in the management schemes in forestry and rangeland management. This results in fragmented land-use planning and management arrangements for forests and rangelands on SFF lands.

#### The added value proposed by CS-FOR-RIS is the use of evidence for the identification of resources, and associated risks, that are most likely to be affected by climate change and which have the highest potential at carbon sequestration potential. The GCF additionally in the proposal has therefore focused on establishing an integrated approach to NRM governance.

#### 104. The participation of private sector is essential to realize the theory of change and paradigm shift of the project. In this sense the policy and legal framework will enable private sector engagement by enhancing the inclusive policy dialogue and stakeholder engagement process, which was initiated during the project formulation stage by involving private sector actors in the development of the project concept note. This will be achieved through a participatory approach to knowledge management and facilitated by the experts, aiming at institutionalizing policy influence and governance mechanisms for NRM.

#### The project will also support the establishment of an integrated approach to NRM governance (Component 1) and climate-sensitive value chain development (Component 3).

### Component 2: Green Investments for Forest and Rangeland Rehabilitation

#### 105. The project will provide financial resources in the form of grants, loans, or a combination thereof together with the State Agency for Environmental Protection and Forestry to put together the Forest Assessment, as a tool containing an inventory of the forest resources on SFF lands. This will be achieved through a participatory approach involving stakeholders from forest management, civil society, NGOs, forest sciences, line ministries and international partners.

#### The project will also support the establishment of an integrated approach to NRM governance (Component 1) and climate-sensitive value chain development (Component 3).

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Output 1.1: Evidence-based natural resources management governance is strengthened across stakeholders

### Objectives:
- By operating with participatory, inclusive and evidence-based approaches, this output constitutes a basis to establish an enabling environment and support the harmonization of the regulatory framework related to forest and land use planning.
- The output will also provide capacity development on climate risks and natural resource assessments. It will support within SAPPF (with evidence-based tools and methodologies for Planning, Monitoring and Evaluation), and it will facilitate the integration of climate change considerations into national and sub-national forest and land-use planning processes (see Activity 1.2.1). The output is dedicated to strengthening the existing national systems for monitoring and evaluation of forest and land use plans, including the development of tools for scenario analysis and decision making (e.g., via Earth Map, Collect Earth, and other tools developed by FAO specifically for climate change adaptation) (see Activity 1.3.1).
- Activities 1.2.1 and 1.3.1 are instrumental to the communities’ integrated climate-resilient plans for Activity 1.3.1.

### The first group of activities implemented within this output will support:
- The development of a set of knowledge products, tools and methodologies (KPTM) on forest and land use planning, management and tenure.
- Conduct sectoral policies and regulations; conduct social assessments on impacts of existing legislation on biodiversity conservation and sustainable forest management.
- Lack of legal framework on tenure and management of communal/forest lands, contractual tenure arrangements, and lack of private-public partnerships in the management of natural resources. Other gaps relate to legislative and policy frameworks for land use planning and management, including the need to strengthen the consultations with the local government bodies, district administrations, forest institutions, PUSA and other community groups.
- Capacity development and technical assistance on forest and land-use planning, management and tenure elements for integrated and participatory natural resources management and use.

### Activities:
1.1.1 Organize national workshops and meetings to mobilize and sensitize the stakeholders (see Activity 1.2.1).
1.1.2 Organize local international conferences/meetings to sensitize the stakeholders.
1.1.3 Training sessions/workshops on forest and land tenure arrangements, policy making, and management of natural resources.

### Output 1.1 is a completion of the following activities (also an “Activity”):
- Activity 1.2.1: Organize national workshops and meetings to mobilize and sensitize the stakeholders.
- Activity 1.3.1: Develop tools for scenario analysis and decision making.

### Detailed Project / Programme Description

#### GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT FUNDING PROPOSAL | Page 23 of 99

**1.1.4 Propose recommendations for enforcement of sustainable management and use of forest-based resources through community participation process**

The Activity will support the development of (a) legislation on tenure arrangements for forest-rangeland ecosystem and include aspects such as: (i) recognition of customary rights, (ii) land-use planning, (iii) land titling, and (iv) land management; and (b) technical, legal and institutional approaches to advance public-private partnership in promotion of integrated natural resource management. The Activity will also support the development of a framework for integrating existing forest-rangeland ecosystem related legislation required for integrated management. The theoretical basis is that tenure arrangements are key to sustainable use of forest-based resources through introduction of a tenure system, which would provide security and certainty for investment by creating a property interest in land. This will be achieved through: (i) the establishment of a National Land Use Committee; (ii) creation of beneficiaries’ local communities and beneficiary institutions at local level (communities, mutuelles, user groups); (iii) development of a National Forest Policy (NFP), which will include the following: i) Responsible Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests – VGGT; and its technical guides; ii) User Groups’ Rights to Access and Use Forest Resources; iii) Community Forestry; iv) Strengthening of forest governance; v) Improving management of forest tenure; and vi) Improving governance of pastoral lands); (iv) elaboration of legislation on land use planning, including the development of a National Land Use Plan (NLUP). The project will also elaborate standards of sustainable use of pastures and forests, methods and tools for monitoring and evaluation.

**1.1.5 Identify approaches for national stakeholder involvement process and organize National Stakeholders Platform Policy Dialogue for the management and use of municipal forest and facilitate development of a National Forest Policy**

The Activity will establish a system of documenting findings and evidences to channel to the CSF-NGO National Platform, which will be responsible for coordinating the activities with other stakeholders. The Activity will engage agencies engaged in the NIM and associated legislation, and will serve as Steering Committee for CSF-NGO National Platform. The project will also support the development of a gender-sensitive approach on gender issues, and its Terms of References will include gender aspects. The project will also support inclusion of gender perspectives in all activities. The project will ensure that mechanisms, tools and approaches are put in place to support the dissemination of NIM and climate policies/laws/information across the country.

The activity will support development of standards, methodologies and information management tools to ensure that all stakeholders have access to relevant data and information. ### 1.1 The three groups of activities implemented under this Output 1.1 aim to create and improve skills and capacity in the following areas:

#### a. Mobilize communities, establish CLMGs and engage in formulating NRCMRCPs.
This Activity will implement the following sub-activities:
a.1 Establishment of Community Landscape Management Groups (CLMGs). Through social mobilization activities, the project will establish CLMGs at the village level to advance participatory management of natural resources. Specific members of the CLMGs will be selected as part of a community-based approach to NRM. At the sub-district/local government level, for example, the representatives of district administration, local self-government bodies (e.g., chiefs, village heads), pastoral and agro-pastoral communities, livestock keepers' unions, Pastoralists Users Unions (PUsUs), Water Users’ Associations (WUA), other civil society and community organizations and private sector actors will be invited to participate in the meetings. The CLMGs would also include active forest and pasture resources users and local entrepreneurs, as well as representatives of the district administration and local self-government bodies.
The CLMGs will be formed in four target areas. Community representatives will be selected at the general village meetings; CLMGs will then be established by the project team with the assistance of the district administration. The project will invite representatives of the State Registration Offices and district tax bodies to participate in meetings. The information on the composition of each CLMG, including their legal status, will be reviewed at the mid-term stage.

#### b. Mobilize communities for NRM climate change mitigation and climate resilient plans (NRCMRCP) elaboration.
This Activity will develop methodologies, guidelines and materials on the elaboration of NRCMRCPs in line with the national framework. The methods will relate to climate change mitigation and adaptation, but also to improve livelihoods. The methods will be developed for different types of landscapes and ecosystems, including pastoral lands, resource use regimes, including agreed schedules and migratory routes of the livestock grazing on the SFF and other areas, as well as forest resources. They will also include guidelines on how to manage natural resources in a way that preserves fragile resources and allow regeneration, and on improvements in the pasture and forests. CLMGs will be trained by the project team on these methods and materials and will disseminate them within their communities.
The project will provide training for community members on the preparation of NRCMRCPs. This Activity will develop trainings methodologies, guidelines and materials to support the elaboration of NRCMRCPs in line with the national framework. The methods will relate to climate change mitigation and adaptation, but also to improve livelihoods. The project will provide training for community members on the preparation of NRCMRCPs.

#### c. Design of NRCMRCPs in all communities in the target districts.

Component 2: Green Investments for Forest and Rangeland Rehabilitation

112. **National Context:** Forest ecosystems hold the largest shares of terrestrial carbon, and trees and perennial grasses play a major role in sequestering CO₂ from the atmosphere into long-term biomass in trees and shrubs. The climate rationale for forests is that they are one of the most effective means to mitigate greenhouse gas emissions and help ecosystems perform their carbon cycle functions. This capability has been weakened in the Kyrgyzstan forests and soils due to a lack of forest management, which has led to a loss of ecosystem services. There is a need to communicate the principles and benefits of their sustainable management into broader user groups than their traditional custodians. Equally important are the social and economic dimensions of forest ecosystems, which can lead to policy contradictions and only partial solutions that do not lead to sustainable development.

113. **Private sector’s involvement in forestry investment:** The key returns of forestry investment are essentially uncertain, as they depend on a number of factors (e.g., climate change, market conditions) over long time horizons, which are largely unfavorable for the private sector. The financial return of pure forestry investment are too low even if it is a 10-year horizon. However, there can be returns from forest products and services that can be generated by the project. The approach described in this document will encourage investments in forests (but not necessarily in rangelands) to generate returns for private sector participation in forest investment. Even if the financial return of forestry investment is low, it may still be possible to generate returns through other means (e.g., carbon credits, biodiversity offsets). The level of concessionality for such investments has been set according to the potential returns of the forestry investment and the willingness of the private sector to invest in forest management. The level of concessionality described) Ventures such as Juniper, Spruce, Poplar, and Maped tree species give particularly low returns (the related concessionality is 95%). In contrast, coniferous tree species such as Picea obovata, Pinus sylvestris, and Abies alba are necessary for about 90 percent of the investment. On the other side, walnut and pistachio reforestation, even with the highest concessionality (65%), is not economically viable in the long term. The level of concessionality described) is necessary for forest management to be financially sustainable over a 20-year horizon. Concessionality is set at 95% for coniferous tree species and 85% for walnut forests, bare policy for pistachio), and the concessionality is set at 65% percent. Such level, when accompanied by the financial return of forestry investment (e.g., carbon credits, biodiversity offsets) can be economically viable. The project will provide an actual contribution even when the major benefit of the investment is a public good as mitigation. As per the enabling environment, the cost of reforestation is about $0.26 USD/CO₂eq (very low compared to average costs in Kyrgyzstan). However, the returns from forest products and services that can be generated by the project are very low. The returns are barely positive. For Pistachio Forests (non-commercial varieties, afforestation activities), the IRR for a grant-aided investment is 10% (very low compared to average costs in Kyrgyzstan). The level of concessionality described) is slightly more viable, with a 20-year IRR at 19% and NPV of $0.5 million USD.) All forest investment envisaged by the project will be subject to an enabling environment assessment as per the enabling environment. The level that is relevant of possible private investors – that could access to credits lines generated by RKFQ. See Financial Study (Annex 2, Chapter 4, Appendix 3), which summarizes the significance of private sector involvement in the project.

114. **Private sector’s participation in rangelands development:** Rangelands play a critical role in the project's objectives and are an important component of the enabling environment for forest investment. # Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Capacity development activities

### 1. Conduct training to 50 communities and institutions on technical legal matters on forest protection and sustainable management of forests and other natural resources (FONR) under a PPP establishment.

All trainings will address gender issues as appropriate in the thematic topic. More specifically, the following components are included:

a. Carry out community mobilization and training for 50 agri ayus (municipalities) and their communities on forest protection and sustainable management of forests and other natural resources at the fire and team management sites at Lesakhoe level. Training of trainers (TOTs) will be provided on technical and institutional aspects of forest protection and sustainable management of FONR for 50 agri ayus institutions at local level including Lesakhoes, NGOs, CSOs, forest and pasture experts, PULAs, WUA’s and other community resource user groups.

b. Provide training to 100 staff members from the Department of Forestry (DoF) on information sharing and organization of CLMAGs and focus on change management in the forest sector. Local level trainings will be provided for 50 agri ayus institutions at fire and team management sites, forest investments. Training sessions will also be provided to women on leadership, decision-making and participation in forest protection and in supporting women’s further engagement in PULAs, WUA’s and other community resource user groups.

c. Provide technical and legal assistance on Public-Private-Partnerships for forest management, to ensure the establishment of a PPP framework for sustainable management of FONR in the area of SFF forest land (in five-year renewable leases) for tree planting on low-productive land.

d. Provide training to 100 staff members from the Department of Forestry (DoF) and local level staff as well as municipal administration and forest users for sustainable management of forests and integrated natural resources management, including the use of community-based forest management and georeferenced to facilitate the M&E process in progress on planting, safeguards, carbon sequestration, etc.

e. Provide technical and legal assistance on Public-Private-Partnerships for forest management, to ensure the establishment of a PPP framework for sustainable management of FONR in the area of SFF forest land (in five-year renewable leases) for tree planting on low-productive land.

### 2. Provide technical assistance to the Pasture Department on climate-sensitive pasture management, assessment and monitoring of pasture resources, and the implementation of INRCMCRP.

Provide support for technical assistance, training and study tours to the pasture department, including via national consultants (GIS and pasture specialists) based in the pasture department with specific task to lead the development of a climate change adaptation strategy for pastoral communities. Enhance awareness and capacity development, along with the technical support of the gender and social dimensions of climate change, as well as the integration of gender equality into the planning and INRCMCRP implementation.

### 3. Conduct training on pasture rotation and evidence-based range management RAE to local cadres as well as training of trainers on INRCMCRP management and implementation, and training sessions for the local communities to implement INRCMCRP on rangeland management. Within this Activity, the project will:

a. develop local cadres capacities on pasture rotation and evidence-based range management RAE. Through a system of TOTs, provide technical assistance in the form of training on the ecological wisdom and benefits of rotational grazing and erosion control, and the need for policies and regulations to support sustainable land use.

b. Strengthen CLMAG’s capacity to implement INRCMCRP on rangeland management. This activity will include TOT organized by NGOs and provided through the mobilization of national and international

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | Page 27 of 99

### Expertise: Capabilities of beneficiaries at local level listed in FC-3 (Livestock, Lushashes, PULs, etc.) to implement INRMCPhS in pasture management will be strengthened through a set of initial trainings and capacity building activities. The project will also support the development of an integrated livestock value chain within the framework of the VGGTs). This project will support the participation of stakeholders in local and district level decision-making processes, including the establishment of District Livestock Management Committees and the 6 Lushashes in the Project Area. Under this activity, capacity development will focus on the following:

- **Livestock owners** and their immediate supervisory organizations (Pasture Committees, local self-governing organizations, and district level) will be coached on implementing on-ground measures to improve livestock productivity and profitability.
- **Sheep farmers** who can use livestock grazing on pastures will be trained in improved grazing management practices. This will include the development of a breeding plan for sheep that is adapted to the local context (e.g., low mortality rate, high reproduction rates).
- **Poultry farmers** will receive training and support to improve their production systems.
- **Women** who are responsible for livestock herding and the cycle of resource degradation driven by this incentive. The project will provide them with training on improved grazing management practices that can lead to higher productivity per head, and how that can be achieved. It will also promote change in pasture use patterns and land tenure arrangements to ensure more sustainable grazing systems.
- **Training** for livestock owners on the integration of tree planting into their pastures, including the use of artificial insemination (AI) to achieve cross-breed livestock that yield more meat and milk per head.

### 17. In line with FAD’s comparative advantages and with the objective to support forest investments that will directly respond to the needs of communities in forest areas, this project will focus on activities that will contribute to the sustainability of the CO2 sequestration potential; however, direct climate-related activities outside of the INRMCPhS framework are not envisaged.

#### 17. Investment activities under Component 2: The economic behavior in rural areas responds to economic and market incentives. The project, mainly under Component 2, will stimulate the required incentives by:
- Facilitating the development of a forest investment portfolio that is attractive for private sector investors.
- Providing concessional investment and technical assistance that demonstrate success in rangeland management and on-the-ground results.
- Encouraging the development of diverse value chains sensitive to changing climate patterns and all aiming at ensuring the sustainability of the carbon sequestration potential.

#### 18. Such activities represent the bulk of investments for carbon sequestration through restoration and improvement of forests and woodlands in forest areas. The project will invest approximately $20 million, which is equivalent to about 9.8 M Euros (break down by investment type is provided in E1.2 and E5.6). The investment will be directed towards the development of a portfolio of investments that are aligned with the INRMCPhS criteria under the INRMCPhS framework.

#### 120. The component includes two main branches of investment, comprising the following two activities:

- **21.4 Forest restoration and improvement in forests based on INRMCPhS developed, and escalate afforestation/reforestation and forest enrichment work by Lushashes with technical assistance from the project**.
- **Component 2: More specifically, within this Activity, in accordance with the Logic Model, the project will support the development of a portfolio of investments that are aligned with the INRMCPhS criteria. The required concessional forestry investment (where the investments are budgeted as equipment and professional services) will be directed towards the development of a portfolio of investments that are aligned with the behavior change of involved institutions and communities in rural areas**.

#### 21.4 Forest restoration and improvement in forests based on INRMCPhS developed, and escalate afforestation/reforestation and forest enrichment work by Lushashes with technical assistance from the project:

- **Support investments include: (a) at least 3,000 hectares of new forests successfully established and managed under the INRMCPhS framework; (b) at least 15,000 ha of existing forests enriched;** (c) At least 56,000 ha of existing forests (slightly above half of the existing forests in four districts) will be converted to commercial varieties, which makes their financial returns very low to stimulate private sector investment. Non-commercial trees will be used in forest areas owned by the state only where forests are established.

## Activities

### Afforestation (A)

- **Area:** At least 10,500 ha
- **Duration:** Over five years (per project)
- **Location:** On public land where urgently needed due to high levels of degradation of forest, or failed or abandoned agricultural lands, and areas with potential for livestock grazing.
- **Project Scope:**
  - The project will use fencing and refreshes pastures of highlands/grassland between and around the proposed afforestation sites.
  - Households sign long-term leases (5 years renewable) to plantations new plantations with a commitment to maintain them for at least five years, after which they can be sold or leased out.
- **Benefits:**
  - Carbon sequestration: Afforestation and planting islands increases sequestration of CO2 in forest biomass.
  - Forest carbon storage: The planted trees will increase the total amount of carbon stored in the lower levels (forest floor) reflect only around 15% of soil's nutrient and mitigate its impact of reduction in water infiltration.

### Co-benefits for CC mitigation:
- Net forest cover (new planting area minus deforested areas) increases by about 20,000 ha.
- Forest carbon storage: The planted trees will increase the total amount of carbon stored in the lower levels (forest floor) reflect only around 15% of soil's nutrient and mitigate its impact of reduction in water infiltration.

### Forest enrichment, covering at least 3,000 ha
- **Duration:** Planned throughout five years (yr.2-6)
- **Activities:**
  - Restoration of moderately degraded growing forests through enrichment planting.
  - Improved forest management for another 54,800 ha of growing forests.

### Benefits:
- Forest carbon storage: The planted trees will increase the total amount of carbon stored in the lower levels (forest floor) reflect only around 15% of soil's nutrient and mitigate its impact of reduction in water infiltration.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal | Page 30 of 99

### Investment: The activity will focus on:

- **Rotational grazing:** The main method for achieving higher pasture production and, therefore greater carbon sequestration over 644,595 ha of grazing land. In rotational grazing (pasture rotation), the essential principle is to allow grassland to recover from grazing pressure by allowing it to grow into a dense growth of pasture vegetation to appraise its potential. By allowing pastures to approach maximum growth, the amount of carbon sequestered in above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass (as 3 tonnes DWW/ha under pasture rotations). The total stock for perennials grasses is at least 21 t ha^-1 or total grassland productivity is at least 4.5 t DM/ha/year. Dividing the grazing period into two phases will require coordination among members of the PUG and the supervising authority. Grazing periods will be staggered to allow for pasture recovery in between.

- **Water management:** The activity will provide water points for livestock, which will be necessary to make management changes more sustainable. Improvements to be considered in the activity include the construction of dams or rotation as a condition for receiving a particular project benefit, such as water point development.

### Component 1: Livestock Investment

- **Component 1:** The role of Component 1 is to strengthen the sustainability of the investment in carbon sequestration carried out in Component 2 by creating economic opportunities with limited risk. In order to decrease pressure on and increase the resilience of natural resources, the project will provide a financial incentive for farmers to invest under Component 3 is referring to the provision of concessional investment through activation of special credit lines (e.g., KfW) or other forms of financing that are available in the country. The component will also contribute to the competitiveness of the market product. Project activities in Component 1 will facilitate access to the external credit line provided by the World Bank, which is expected to be used for the purchase of livestock inputs and the rehabilitation of degraded natural resources in the project intervention area.

- **Livestock Investment:** Referring to the centrality of livestock to ensure forests and rangelands are managed sustainably. Livestock investment will complement the activities carried out by other components, particularly Component 2. The activity will provide the establishment of 1 ha seed-multiplication fields near PUG villages, separate from the main grazing area. This will allow for a more sustainable increase in livestock production and improved breeds (large ruminant releases less methane per kg of body weight than smaller animals). In addition to this, the project will also establish a breeding herd of 100 cows and 200 sheep of intermixture producers or barren cows and weak or injured or low-productive small rams. By selecting these animals for reproduction, it is expected that the number of productive female cattle and male small rams at 1.5 years, livestock production is expected to rise by at least 10%.
