### Sustainability and raw material sourcing area within the core Project Area (the four districts of At-Talaaq, Togora-Toum, Souak and Uzibag). Specifically on the livestock sector, the Component will also support operations that aim to reduce pressure on water resources but will provide economic opportunities for entrepreneurial growth among women engaged in non-traditional value chain activities. The Component will also contribute to the conservation of natural resources and the rangelands and the ecosystem services provided by both (see the baseline assessment carried out by FAO with Kyrgyz Republic, 2013). This is done through a combination of actions that aim at improving the resilience of households in the main ag-food value chains where rural households are involved (e.g. livestock, non-liner forest products such as nuts and fruits, honey, etc.). ### 122. Component 3 includes one output:

#### 123. Objective: The Component will provide capacity development across a number of carefully selected value chains, and will support establishing direct linkages between producers and corporate buyers operating on end markets and having the potential to improve market access for smallholder farmers. This is done through three main components under Component 2 by providing local communities with the access to skills and technologies conducive to sustainable production practices and improved market access. Market component 3 is structured in a first preparatory phase (see Activities 3.1.1 and 3.1.2) and an investment phase (Activities 3.1.3, 3.1.4 and 3.1.5). The activities under this component will be implemented by the following value chains: pistachios, dried fruits (dried apricot, dried plum), and fruit orchards (cherries, apples), beekeeping, but also other value chain actors such as producers of honey, nuts, and other forest products. These activities are based on the FSC standard, Certification of NTFPs according to FSC standards® and other voluntary international standards such as HACCP, Fair Trade, Origin, etc.

### 124. Preparatory activities

#### 3.1.5 Select value chains in operation and provide technical support to the value chain development and applications for climate-sensitive business development. The Activity will include:

- **Economic assessment of the pistachio value chain**: This activity is carried out by FAO for the Kyrgyz NTFPs and periodic monitoring of market trends, including supply planning calendars and gaps, existing bottlenecks and risks.
- **Market prospecting campaign on national and international markets**, to identify potential buyers operating in premium segments and fostering environmental and social responsibilities as their corporate strategy.
- **Resource inventory**: using geospatial tools followed by a thorough Market Development Plan (will include the identification of strong up-and-coming actors).

### b. Raise awareness on market opportunities and requirements, including the design and rollout of the Kyrgyz Tree Nuts & Dried Fruits information and trade portal.

### c. Support to agribusinesses operating in the selected value chains.
#### 3.1.2 Develop a climate-smart upgrade value chain
a. Identify key actors in the value chain via information campaign and value chain mapping for climate-sensitive business practices. This activity will include:
- Map and analyse selected value chain actors, comprising (i) performance assessment of businesses following the guidelines provided by the FOPA; (ii) analysis of the performance of the main actors in the value chains; and (iii) analysis of the raw material suppliers' performance for value chain upgrade business activities (indicators).

b. Develop and upgrade climate-smart value chains, following experimented practices such as:
- Accompanying the preparation business proposal. Agribusinesses, where necessary, jointly with the project team, will prepare a business proposal for the development of their value chain upgrade/development. Project provide support to properly design and evaluate business plans.
- Developing and implementing training and reference material collection to collectors of NTFP farmers, mediators and agribusinesses.
- Providing financial literacy. Regardless of investment in value chains supported by the project, agribusinesses will be provided with a financial literacy training for primary education. At least 50% of the trainers will be women.

c. Support to climate-smart market-oriented client project, clients participating will receive support to adopt international standards requirement and undergo the third party audit.
- Enhancing transparency within the value chains, using agrologic and block-chain principles, establishing a traceability system for the products.
- Promote marketing. These activities include project-supported product marketing, testing of chemical composition and quality of the products, establishment of a market-oriented client project, design and dissemination of promotional material. It will also include the facilitation of the establishment of an export-oriented business model.

d. Promote women participation. The project will provide assistance in the establishment of the chain of custody and empowerment of women through trainings and opportunities for business development.

### Investment activities under Component 3

#### 3.3.1 Activate specific credit lines and provide loans for eligible value chain actors in commerical/ export-oriented enterprises with project-relevant value chains.
The key investment activity under Component 3 will include the activation of special credit lines for project-related investments, including the provision of financial support to agribusinesses through the technical assistance provided under Activity 3.1.1 and 3.1.2, small, medium and larger enterprises in the Project area. The credit lines will be activated by providing guarantees to commercial banks for the credit lines. The loans will range from US$ 10,000 to US$ 300,000, with the average amount about US$50,000. The interest rate on these loans is expected to be between 6% and 8%, as determined by ROKF, as the Executing Entity Co-financer, through pre-defined five commercial banks (use of a single bank will be allowed in case of small enterprises). Loans will be provided in local currency, for a term of about 3-5 years, to existing enterprises representing eligible value chains. Indicators include: number of enterprises supported by the credit lines; amount of loans disbursed; agricultural machinery, greenhouses, nurseries for tree plantations and commercial orchards, eco-tourism, agribusinesses, etc., as well as the establishment of new enterprises in these sectors. The business development support implemented under this component will provide further incentives to the financial

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## Background Information on Project / Programme Sponsor (Executing Entity)

130. Ensuring Country Ownership, the project will be co-executed by FAO which will also have a coordinating role as the main implementing agency for the project activities.
131. The State Agency for Environment Protection and Forestry (SAEPF) under the Government of the Kyrgyz Republic is responsible for the implementation of the project in accordance with the national strategy on climate change, biodiversity, sustainable use of natural resources, development of forestry and hunting enterprises, and ensuring the sustainable management of forest resources.
132. The Agency for Environment Protection and Development and Management (AERIS) was established in October 2013 as a semi-autonomous organization. Its main body is Supervisory Board with 21 members. AERIS mission is to ensure sustainable poverty alleviation by providing assistance to local communities and local self-governmental bodies in resolving social, economic, environmental problems through the implementation of projects aimed at increasing investment and action plans, mobilizing resources, as well as developing, implementing and managing investments aimed at climate change mitigation and adaptation.

AERIS operates in accordance with the following principles: (a) autonomy; (b) impartiality; (c) involvement; (d) management of public funds; (e) cost effectiveness; (g) decentralization; (h) sustainability of sub-project/programme projects; (i) environmental consciousness.

134. FAO has extensive experience in supporting countries in the fields of Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation, Forest Management, and Sustainable Land Management. With a portfolio of 200+ projects worth over US$ 600 million, the Organization has the experience and technical capacity to manage the requested grant aid for the project. FAO has also supported the Government of Kyrgyz Republic in implementing projects with over US$ 20 million already mobilized to green its main value chains.

135. FAO joined the World Bank Group as a member of the World Bank Group in January 2007 and officially joined the Organization. First opened in 2009, the Country Office was upgraded in 2012 and a FAO Representative appointed in 2014. Technical expertise provided by FAO includes forest management, sustainable land management, climate change adaptation and mitigation, and forest management in the face of climate change. The FAO assistance in Kyrgyzstan is planned around the 2018-2022 Action Plan.

136. Through the FAO Investment Centre, the Organization is providing technical support to sizable investments by the World Bank and FAO in the following areas: NRM (land, water, forest, and pasture), value chain (VC) development, livestock production, and rural infrastructure. The Organization provides analysis and advice on policy reform for agriculture, land tenure and natural resources.

### C.5. Market Overview (if applicable)

136. Non-timber forest products: Natural, low sugar and safe for people are the main trends in the international market for non-timber forest products (NTFPs). The demand is increasing due to the growing awareness of consumers about the need for "healthy snacks," following natural processing methods (sun dried vs. industrial dehydration). Importantly, the government has been working with the private sector and NGOs to promote NTFPs as a source of income for farmers who buy and the processes that go into producing them. They want to make reasonable decisions about what they buy.

137. Thus, Kyrgyz exports of walnuts in 2015 has reached 423 MT, 44 percent up compared to 2016, generating US$ 8.9 million. The main export markets are Turkey (30%), Germany (20%) and the EU (17%). Other countries include Russia, FSC, Fair Trade, Organic and HACCP brought into the value chain as its critical element securing quality, sustainability and traceability of products.

140. Increasingly, the government is working with the private sector to promote NTFPs as a source of income for farmers who buy and the processes that go into producing them. They want to make reasonable decisions about what they buy. Commercials and conventional healthy snacks are other potential markets for the Kyrgyz NTFPs (see FS for more details).

138. Thus, Kyrgyz exports of walnuts in 2015 has reached 423 MT, 44 percent up compared to 2016, generating US$ 8.9 million. The main export markets are Turkey (30%), Germany (20%) and the EU (17%). Other countries include Russia, FSC, Fair Trade, Organic and HACCP brought into the value chain as its critical element securing quality, sustainability and traceability of products.

# Detailed Project / Programme Description

## C7. Institutional / Implementation Arrangements

### Overall Governance and Institutional Arrangements

The governance and institutional arrangements proposed in this section are those of discussions and agreement with the selected project operational partners and co-financers, including the SAEFP, ARIS, MAFR, and other relevant government agencies. The overall governance structure is based on the political direction and guidance of the country's Climate Change Coordination Group (CCCIG), the national inter-ministerial coordination body for climate change issues. The CCCIG will be chaired by the Deputy Prime Minister as the Deputy Chair of the CCCIG. The CCCIG ensures multi-sector coordination of all activities in the Kyrgyz Republic related to climate change and coordinates with other relevant bodies, including the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF), the Ministry of Environment Protection (SEP), and the Ministry of Finance (MF). By establishing a clear level when it has become powerful, the Kyrgyz Government tends to make a change in the structure of part of economic activities.

The CCCIG will be supported by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) as described below. The PSC is primarily an advisory Executive Unit that ensures the execution of the project under the country ownership. The PSC will consist of representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF), the Ministry of Environment Protection (SEP), the Ministry of Finance (MF), the Kyrgyz Academy of Sciences, and other relevant government agencies. The Project Steering Committee is chaired by the Sector Project in the Area Project, as listed in detail in Table C.1. The co-financers of the project are SAEFP, ARISF, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF), and the Kyrgyz Academy of Sciences.

### Project Steering Committee - Country Ownership of Decision-Making Process

The CS-FOR will establish a National Stakeholders Platform (NSP) under the CCCIG coordinating with an ongoing action plan for stakeholder engagement. The NSP will be chaired by the PSC as described below. The Project Steering Committee (PSC) that will be providing policy and operational guidance to the project's implementation. The PSC will meet on a regular basis, at least once every six months, in order to review progress against the approved project plan, monitor decision-making officials, appointed as focal points by partner institutions to guarantee the Kyrgyz driven decision-making process. The PSC will consist of representatives from the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF), the Ministry of Finance Department (under MAF), State Agency for Local Self Government and Interethnic Relations (SALSGIR), the Ministry of Environment Protection (SEP), the Kyrgyz Academy of Sciences, and other relevant government agencies. The PSC will also include representatives from the civil society and private sector (women and youth) as well as representatives from the project operational partners and co-financers. These representatives are participating communities/CLGs and will be observers of the PSC and will be invited to the meetings of their special interest groups.

The PSC functions will: (i) ensure the quality of results, and the sustainability and impacts of projects in line with the policy framework; (ii) review progress against the approved project plan, monitor decision-making officials, appointed as focal points by partner institutions to guarantee the Kyrgyz driven decision-making process. The project progress reports be sent to be FAD); (iii) approve adjustments to the distribution of budget between items on the project plan and/or the allocation of funds for new activities; (iv) review and approve the achievement of targets or results and outputs, based on information provided by the Project Management Unit; (v) approve possible changes in the project implementation strategy, including the addition of new activities, based on the outcomes of meetings, in accordance with the issues under consideration. The PSC will also support project activities by promoting dialogue between the Government and ensuring meaningful among political decision makers.

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## D.1 Value Added for GCF Involvement

### Kyrgyzstan is a lower middle-income country.

The country's exposure to climate change risks is among the highest in Central Asia and the world, with over 70% of its population living below the poverty line. The poor are most vulnerable to both internal disasters and published literature, the rural poor are the least resilient and most exposed to natural disasters triggered by climate change (see Chapter 1 of the Feasibility Study). The exposure to climate change risks is closely tied to the degradation of land resources in Kyrgyzstan's mountainous terrain. This degradation has led to significant loss of forest cover including Kyrgyzstan's pastures and forests. These same ecosystems constitute important stores of atmospheric carbon, which are being lost at an alarming rate due to deforestation and other anthropogenic activities. The country is also a major emitter of greenhouse gases (GHGs), with the largest share coming from livestock production and land use change. The project will address these issues by supporting coordinated actions that will lead to the sequestration of 19.8 Mt CO2e, or 22.6 percent of the agricultural share of emissions in Kyrgyzstan.

#### Forest Management and Degradation Activities

The project will support forest management and degradation activities (3,478 tCO2e), forest perimeter (873,500 haCO2e), 1,117,520 haCO2e of afforestation and reforestation, 69,000 haCO2e of agroforestry, 1,400 haCO2e of forest restoration, 83,000 haCO2e of agricultural afforestation activities (729,608 tCO2e), and livestock management (148,545 tCO2e). Agricultural inputs are a minor source of emissions in the country.

#### Current Forest Degradation

The current level of forest degradation under BAU scenarios generates an estimated net loss of 1.5 Mt CO2eq in 20 years; through the combined effect of the various interventions, the project will be able to avoid the mentioned losses and, on top of that, generate a net sequestration of 3.8 Mt CO2e (from afforestation and reforestation activities and other agricultural activities) for a net effect of 19.8 tCO2eq sequestered. Such expected sequestration will be achieved through the following: 0.5 Mt CO2e from avoided deforestation; 3.4 Mt CO2e from forest enrichment; and 0.3 Mt CO2e from improved forest management of the existing forests.

#### Carbon Sequestration

The carbon sequestered will be achieved at a low cost of US$1.5 per tCO2eq (or $1.8 per US$ considering GCF financing only). Of equal or greater importance, the design of this investment is intended to put Kyrgyzstan's agricultural sector on a more sustainable path by supporting the development and implementation of new technologies and capacities to introduce and support innovative practices that lead to the sequestration and safeguarding of carbon stocks as described above. The project will also contribute to the achievement of the national goal of reducing emissions from deforestation in the absence of catalytic financing from the GCF.

### D.2 Exit Strategy

The shortage of domestic resources for internal investments in the agricultural sector overall is evidenced by the World Bank's Country Economic Memorandum (CEM) for Kyrgyzstan, which states that "the country's current debt ratio (as at 31 December 2011), the Kyrgyz Republic is still considered at moderate risk of debt distress. The current national debt rate will also affect the ability to access concessional financing from international financial institutions." Given this context and limited climate finance made available for Kyrgyzstan, as well as the committed finance "per capita," is considerably lower than that provided by other countries in Central Asia. As a result, the country has not been able to direct climate-related development financing has been directed towards the energy sector (mostly hydropower), with very limited resources allocated to agriculture and rural development. The project will contribute to the achievement of this goal by delivering additional resources to areas of high potential gains in carbon sequestration and storage.

GCF involvement is critical to the success of the project, as it will provide a significant amount of funding for the project activities that deliver important benefits of enhancing the resilience of these ecosystems to climate change stressors as well as the overall rural economy. The project will also depend on these resources for food (nearly 100 percent of the population).

# RATIONALE FOR GCF INVOLVEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 46 OF 59

The continued delivery of benefits from activities initiated under the CS-FOR project will be sustained beyond the investment period by four critical elements, as follows:

- **National ownership:** In preparing this funding proposal, the design team initiated an extensive national engagement process with Kyrgyzstan. As a result, the proposal is fully aligned with the Kyrgyzstan’s INDCs and national policies / frameworks, and the interests of those key actors involved in the success of the project. The project has been designed to ensure that the institutional capacity for effective programme planning was pursued to ensure that a high level of field ownership over the project and the supported activities was integrated into the work plans and mandates of the executing entities over the eight-year investment period, and that the project is fully owned by the Kyrgyz government. As such, once the preparatory phase and the implementation of the project is finalized, the investment will be fully funded by the institutions and private sector actors already identified.

- **On the private sector side:** The banks generated by RECOF and re-invoked by the project will act as a revolving fund for the local banks. The economic activities will be self-standing and the most successful investments will be scaled up to other regions of Kyrgyzstan.

- **The project places substantial emphasis on strengthening capacities of institutions and individuals (at all levels) in order to ensure that they are able to effectively manage the project and its associated activities:** Use of free tools for NR and climate monitoring [Collect Earth, Earth Map], and the support to the network of agroforestry extension officers will be used. The project will also provide technical assistance to upgrade existing systems; for example, the geo-referenced monitoring and evaluation system (Output 1.2) instead of installing new systems. The operation and maintenance of such large system has already integrated into the institutional structure. Each Project Entity will follow a standard procurement procedure of project assets accordingly.

- **Economic incentives:** In order to reduce forest and pasture deforestation, and to ensure the behaviour of keeping an alternative income source as a source of paid income for safety net purposes, the project will take parallel paths towards increased efficiency and productivity of the livestock production system along with a progressive reduction in emissions. The project will provide financial incentives (e.g., carbon credits) and non-financial incentives for economic return (e.g., orchards and high value non-timber forest products). This will not only reduce deforestation but also increase the productivity of the livestock sector, which is responsible for 70% of emissions (as more productive animals raised using good practices emit less). The overall impact of agricultural investments in the project area will be to improve the livelihoods and food security of rural households and technologies by targeted farmers / pastoralists. For resource poor farmers the potential for significant financial gain as a result of improved livestock production systems is high, provided that there are incentives for behaviour change. In response, the project has carefully selected supportive activities that respond to the self-interest of these households. The project will also provide technical assistance and training on adoption rates and sustained action. The results of the financial and economic analysis show solid positive returns on the investment, affecting the selection of value chains. In the case of investments in the high mountain forest areas, the project will support the development of a market for timber products to increase their value in contributing to the national INDC targets. By working with the banking sector, with support from the National Bank of Kyrgyzstan, the project will provide financial incentives and technical assistance to select value chain enterprises; the project will further leverage the self-interest of these businesses that will long out last the project.

- **Future expansion:** An important outcome of the engagement process used in preparing the CS-FOR proposal was the expressed interest on the part of key governmental agencies implementing the project, and other stakeholders involved. The project has been designed to ensure that it is fully aligned with Kyrgyzstan’s national strategy involving carbon sequestration through integrated forest and pasture management. As the project progresses, the institutional capacity will be strengthened so that the project entities can effectively make the transition from a (time-bound) project to a more permanent programme, creating multiple opportunities for scaling up the activities in other regions of Kyrgyzstan and ensuring long-term growth of the activities (see Section E.2.1 for further details). Moreover, the project’s strategy for carbon

- **Enabling Environment:** By targeting policy harmonization and strengthening the capacities of key national and local institutions, as well as supporting the efforts of local management groups, the project will significantly influence the policy and operational environment necessary to support effective implementation. These activities will also contribute to the development of a robust institutional framework that supports efforts of expansion. In terms of maintenance, the proposed activities and use of new technologies will not increase costs beyond what is already budgeted for in the current GCF proposal. # E.1. Mitigation / adaptation impact potential

## Potential of the project/programme to contribute to the achievement of the Fund’s objectives and result areas

### Shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways

According to the INDC (Intended Nationally Determined Contribution) submitted by the Kyrgyz Republic in September 2015, the Kyrgyzstan INDC acknowledge the importance of addressing climate change and the challenges related to its implementation. The Kyrgyz Republic has set a target for reducing greenhouse gas emissions from all sources by 39% below business as usual (BAU) levels by 2030, which is equivalent to an absolute reduction in total emissions of approximately 15.8 Mt CO2e. This includes the economic impact in over USD $1 billion[1]. The country identifies adaptation and mitigation as main targets of its climate change strategies and identified the total cost required to adopt and mitigate at about USD $0.6 billion.

### Shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways, with co-benefits in the GCF objective related to (i) Increased climate-resilient sustainable development

#### Shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways

According to the INDC document submitted by the Government of the Kyrgyz Republic, it was determined that the Kyrgyz Republic’s contribution to mitigation will be to reduce greenhouse gas emissions from all sources by 39% below business as usual (BAU) levels by 2030. With international support, the Kyrgyz Republic could implement mitigation measures to achieve total reduction in the range of 29.00 - 30.50 Mt CO2e compared with BAU scenario. The project will contribute to this target through the following:

- **Reduction of emissions**: The project will support the reduction of emission and enhance carbon storage of about 19.8 million tCO2e through implementation of forest management activities, including afforestation and reforestation, sustainable land use planning and monitoring and reporting system; community-based investment in natural forest resources and biodiversity conservation; climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction measures to prevent further degradation; diversification of options for community livelihoods; and reduction of emission intensity from livestock production.
- **Enhancement of carbon storage**: The project will support the enhancement of carbon storage potential of ecosystems (forests and rangelands) to a low-carbon emission economy where mitigation interventions are implemented. This includes afforestation, reforestation, sustainable land use planning and monitoring and reporting system; community-based investment in natural forest resources and biodiversity conservation; climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction measures to prevent further degradation; diversification of options for community livelihoods; and reduction of emission intensity from livestock production.
- **Supporting services**: The project will also support the enhancement of ecosystem services, with all benefits from supporting and regulating services (e.g. improved climate regulation, flood regulation, soil retention, habitat provision). The project will measure its success by assessing the degree to which it achieves the above mitigation and adaptation objectives.

#### Increased climate-resilient sustainable development

Throughout the preparation of the project, the household survey has assessed the level of resilience of the target population (compared to a possible project baseline) using the Resilience Capacity Index (RCI). The RCI is based on the Resilience Analysis Methodology (RAM), which allows to derive a Resilience capacity index tailored on local vulnerability, focusing on explaining how certain households are more resilient than others. This includes (i) assets; (ii) resources and climate-related shocks; and shows how the control of these factors is more resilient with respect to the intervention group. Looking at the resilience structure matrix for the intervention group, adaptive capacity is the


---

---

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 49 OF 99

**most important pillar followed by access to Basic Services, Social Safety Nets and Assets. For the corriged global adaptive capacity is the main pillar, followed by Assets, access to basic services and social safety nets.**

For both the intervention and control groups, the importance of adaptive capacity is mainly driven by the high level of education and income. The most important factors for the intervention group are: (1) the average score on the Resilience Composite Index (RCI score) and the diversification of income portfolios (which account for almost 16 percent in the variance explained), (2) the number of assets, and (3) the level of education. The control group is characterized as part of the adaptive capacity and help identifying priorities for investment in the country, including for partner countries. The project will support the development of a national adaptation plan (NAP) and the implementation of GCF adaptation project for the country. Through its evidence-based approach, the project will support the diversification of income portfolios and increase access to basic services and social safety nets, as well as reduce exposure to change related stresses and hazards through implementation of systemic INMRCPs and related investments. The project will also improve the resilience of communities gained by improving ecosystem services and diversifying livelihood opportunities for women and men. Ultimately, this multi-faceted approach will serve as a model for dissemination of good practice throughout the country, shifting rural agricultural production from a predominantly unsustainable subsistence to more sustainable commercial agriculture that is better adapted to climate change and market economy.

Through the mid-term and final assessment of resilience using the RIMA+ approach, the project will assess the degree to which it will have contributed to climate-resilient sustainable development.

# E.2. Paradigm Shift Potential

Detailed analysis of how activity can catalyse impact beyond a one-off project/programme investment

## E.2.1 Potential for scaling up and replication (Provide a numerical multiple and supporting rationale)

The major constraint to the increase of carbon sequestration and storage in forests and rangelands has been the lack of incentives for communities to preserve and expand forest cover, which is likely to continue in the absence of clear incentives for communities to preserve and expand forest cover. These processes will likely continue in the absence of clear incentives for communities to preserve and expand forest cover. The project intends to break these trends by introducing and supporting an integrated, locally driven approach that combines market-based approaches with community-driven actions to create reliable markets. These measures will establish the preconditions for the maintenance and increase of forest cover and at the same time provide a diversified and profitable agricultural activity in targeted areas, which can coexist with trees and pastures. By establishing linkages with market channels, the changes in management practices will benefit from and lead to more diversified and profitable agriculture for beneficiaries in targeted areas. The potential for scaling up is high as it is based on replicable actions.

### The harmonization of national policies and strategies affecting forest and grassland management (underpinning Component 1) are, by definition, national in scope. Not only will the target areas selected by the CS-FOR project be within the national boundaries but also the design of the project will ensure that the establishment of tenure structures and enforcement protocols will establish the same enabling conditions nationwide. As a result, future investments in forest and grassland management can be made with confidence that they will have an impact on the entire country.

### The project will encounter a much more conducive policy and institutional environment, greatly improving the efficiency by which expansionary activities are implemented. The investment in the enabling environment will result in the improvement of an estimated 56,359 ha of degraded forest/forestation in 10 years at a rate of approximately 3.48 m ICoUs. The improved enabling environment is also the condition to ensure the sustainability of the forestry and rangeland management activities that are being implemented under the project. This will result in an estimated 646,279 ha (0.7 m ICoUs) from forest/forestation or forest/enhancement in 6,000 ha of degraded forests, about 0.5 m ICoUs.

### The design process has taken great care in bringing together key governmental agencies and coordination bodies to ensure that the project is aligned with national priorities and strategies. This will provide a greater sense of ownership that this approach has established is essential not only in ensuring a high level of performance management but also in ensuring that the lessons learned from the project are applied more widely, thus expanding use of the new, approach to local resource management leading to greater carbon sequestration. The incorporation of project activities into the regular annual work plans, and experiences gained through the project implementation period will ensure that the enabling environment is sustainable over time. This will provide a solid foundation for vision and approach to resource management nation-wide while establishing a solid operational capacity in their use.

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 51 OF 99

### Figure E.2.1 Priority expansion area of the project

The assessments carried in the identification of the CS-FOR project target areas was conducted at a national scale. A hybrid approach was used to combine the identification and prioritization of expansion areas with the selection of project target areas (see figure E.2.1). The assessment was undertaken to assist the implementing agency in identifying priority areas for use of the resource management/carbon sequestration services provided through the project. The remote sensing / GIS management tools being developed as part of the project will support data collection and monitoring are fully designed for being used at this scale.

The engagement of private sector interests in key value chains in the design of the CS-FOR-project is strategic and critical to the success of the project. By linking VCs, specifically nuts and dried fruits, establishes a vicious cycle rewarding participating beneficiary groups with ready, profitable markets for the produce generated through implementation of the project activities. This will provide an opportunity for these groups to remain in their current market and participate in new markets. The success of commercial enterprises in the targeted VCs also provides the motivation and means for smallholder farmers to move into new geographic areas as the applications introduced through the CS-FOR project also move into new locations.

### E.2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

The project will ensure that stakeholder engagement represents a paramount element of CS-FOR. The project will aim at transferring not only information and knowledge generated during execution of activities but also tools and skills that are necessary to support the stakeholders in making change into the decision making process (institutions and private sector) and into livelihood strategies (communities).

The project will ensure transfer of knowledge to stakeholders across the three project Components via trainings and workshops. The CS-FOR project will provide a platform for capacity building and training activities conducted in each of the Components. To this end stakeholder’s involvement from planning to monitoring will be among the main goals of the project. This will be achieved through an integrated approach to the knowledge management process with specific targeting both communities and institutions. A key objective of the learning and knowledge management component is to ensure that CS-FOR provides a platform for climate change-related information to all stakeholders involved in project activities as well as to the wider public.

Additionally, the project foresees the organization of at least 5 engagement workshops per year (1 national and 4 provincial) with key stakeholders from each Component. These will provide an opportunity for knowledge exchange as well as to create opportunities for local and international media to understand the project and its report on achievements. Finally, it will also allow a more stringent and wider follow up of the Environmental and Social Safeguards.

To ensure sound and effective management of the CS-FOR project processes the following will be assigned: a communications officer will be appointed to the unit of CS-FOR. The individual will work among others - on the socio-ization of project data and information, communication with the media, and coordination of the project’s participation in national and international conferences (Component 1). With wide outreach and in schools will increase the engagement including of younger generations.

### E.2.3 Collaboration and partnerships

The CS-FOR project will pilot collaborative resource assessment and develop Integrated Natural Resources Climate Resilient Management plans using remote sensing, climate maps and various zoning and stratification approaches. The project will also provide a platform for stakeholders to engage in the development and review of the process. The Project will develop and test implementation arrangements for INRRCMPs with the engagement

**E2.4 Contribution to regulatory framework and policies**

The project will contribute to a regulatory framework in pursuing ecosystem-based adaptation as declared in the "Climate Change Adaptation Programme and Action Plan for 2015-2017 for the Forest and Biodiversity sector". This will be achieved by: (i) strengthening the enabling environment through accompanying national institutional capacities to be informed by evidence and lessons learned from the four large pilot areas. (ii) improving the planning process at the national and community level through stratification and zoning of forest-landscape ecosystems, taking into account local socio-economic conditions, land tenure arrangements, and institutional conditions; mechanisms and tenure arrangements for communities, local agents and private sector to secure access to resources and services in forest landscapes; and (iv) establishing mechanisms and arrangements for afforestation, resources improvement and maintenance; and (v) establishing mechanisms and arrangements for funding climate change adaptation initiatives. At national level, the project will operate through facilitation of informed policy

**E2.4 Contribution to regulatory framework and policies**

The CS-FOR Project will dedicate the entire first component of the project to ensure strengthening the enabling environment by accompanying national institutions, project stakeholders and civil society in harmonizing the policy and institutional conditions for forest landscape ecosystems management and climate change adaptation initiatives through evidence-based, gender sensitive, participatory and inclusive processes for pasture-forest ecosystem management. This will be achieved at the national level through facilitation of informed policy on land use and climate change mitigation issues. ### E. Sustainable Development Potential

#### E.1. Environmental, social and economic co-benefits, including gender-sensitive development impacts

The project will mainly contribute to the Paradigm Shift Objective of the GCF related to (a) Shifting to low-emission solutions and (b) Promoting climate-resilient sustainable development.

Environmental co-benefits:
- The project will generate a critical mass of additional co-benefits, as follows.
  - Environmental co-benefits: Afforestation/investment in afforestation/restoration and forest enrichment will contribute to improve soil fertility and stability, reduce land degradation, improve water retention in soils, provide additional biodiversity benefits, and increase the expected carbon sequestration. The project will also generate a significant increase of the expected carbon sequestration, the increased availability of drinking water and of non-timber forest products worth an estimated US$ 10 million.
- Social and institutional co-benefits: The project will provide livelihood opportunities for 50 municipalities and their communities will include social mobilization including gender training and institutional support, as well as the establishment of task forces and fire management teams in Lexukum. Moreover, by facilitating policy dialogue under Component 1, the project will play a prominent role in promoting institutional coordination between stakeholders on climate change and forest/rangelands management.

Co-benefits for women and youth:
- Women and youth (both young women and young men) will benefit from their specific inclusion in project activities. The project will ensure that women and youth are fully represented, involved, and engaged in the project supported value chains by proactively including them in the design of the project and ensuring that gender equality and youth development will be taken into consideration when selecting value chains. Moreover, the project will encourage the access for women to improved natural resources (forests and rangelands) to ensure equitable distribution of benefits.

### E.4. Needs of the Recipient

#### E.4.1. Vulnerability of country and beneficiary groups (Adaptation only)

**196.** The geography and topography of Kyrgyzstan make it one of the most hazard-prone countries in Central Asia, and the country is particularly vulnerable to natural disasters such as droughts, land mudslides, avalanches, violent winds, downpouring, fires, floods, breathersthroughofglacial lakes, floods, river erosionandearthquakes are all possible. The Kyrgyz Republic has a mountainous terrain with an average altitude of 2,500 meters above the level of the mountains, where vulnerability to these events is particularly high.

**197.** On average, natural disasters are responsible for US$3 billion in economic losses per year in Central Asia and the Caucasus region. The Kyrgyz Republic has a GDP of $8.4 billion. Local and state local government resources available for disaster reduction and response exacerbate the impact of these events. The vulnerability of the country to natural hazards is also exacerbated by the baseline survey carried out during the project design stage, depending on adaptive capacity, access to basic services, social inclusion, and diversification of economic assets. Adaptive capacity is mainly driven by the level of education and the diversification of income sources. The Kyrgyz Republic has a high vulnerability index (0.85), which is part of the adaptive capacity and help identifying priorities for investment in the country, including for partner organizations and local communities.

#### E.4.2. Financial, economic, social, and institutional needs

**200.** According to the Kyrgyz State Design Institute of Land Management, large areas of agricultural land are in poor condition, and are affected by land degradation (an estimated 50-80 percent). This includes erosion, salinization and sodification, nutrient depletion, loss of organic matter content, reduction in soil fertility, and a decline in the number of valuable plants) and organic soil carbon content that has declined from 3 percent to 1.5 percent, which, cumulatively, lead to a reduction in agricultural productivity. The Kyrgyz Republic has an estimated total area of land under cultivation (arable land) at erosion at 64 million ha, 700,000 hectares of which is arable land; 11.2 million ha of land (of which 3.1 million are irrigated), and 58 percent of the country's total area is under forest cover. The Kyrgyz Republic has an estimated 640,000 hectares of agricultural land that have been damaged or abandoned due to inappropriate land use practices, such as overgrazing, deforestation, and soil erosion. This has led to reduced ecosystem functions and biodiversity loss in agriculture, especially in light of climate change.

**201.** Forest investments suffer from a systematic shortage of funding in the country. The private sector is not investing for forest conservation or reforestation purposes. The public sector is also not investing in forest management investment on rangeband height of attention (including of multilateral financial institutions) but investment in the sector have several impacts on carbon sequestration, rather than livelihoods which is not sufficient to ensure contributions to the country's INDC targets.

262. Poverty level remains high with over a quarter of the total population living below poverty line in 2016 (National Statistical Committee). About three quarters of the poor live in rural area and many rely on agriculture for their livelihood. Poverty is the highest in remote mountainous areas where scarce arable land combined with underdeveloped infrastructure, limited access to markets and lack of social services further exacerbate poverty. This also evident as 43% of children under five and 39% of women of reproductive age are affected by anemia. According to the World Bank, Kyrgyz Republic is one of the most vulnerable countries in Central Asia to climate change related shocks, including floods and mudslides, which affect resilience of families and communities. Livestock is the most important asset for rural households and serves as a buffer against poverty. It also acts as an important asset for poor families, which can prevent them from becoming destitute at the time of shocks.

### E.5 Country Ownership

#### E.5.1 Country ownership (also) demonstrated by its commitment to implement a funded project or programme

##### 263. As specified in Section C.1, the government operates in line with the commitments of the NDCs and their targets in implementing climate adaptation mitigation. Regarding long-term GHG emissions targets, it was determined that the Kyrgyz Republic's contribution to mitigation will be to reduce GHG emissions in the range of 11.49–13.75 percent below BAU by 2030 (the lower bound is based on the current NDC and upper bound is based on the range of 12-67, 15-85 percent below BAU). Under international support, the Kyrgyz Republic could implement mitigation measures to reduce GHG emissions in the range of 4.9–30.06 percent below BAU by 2030 (the lower bound is based on the range of 30.06 – 36.75 percent below BAU) in 2050. Co-benefits for climate risks mitigation. While the NDC and its targets are aligned with the national development strategy, it was also determined that the measures to enhance individuals' capacity to adapt will be combined with a process of regular updating of the national priorities and sectoral adaptation strategies. The government has already started implementing some of the adaptation measures as part of the earlier adaptation plans'/ outcomes. Reported plans are being processed and will constitute the initial policy framework for climate change adaptation.

##### 264. A National Adaptation Plan is not approved yet in the country. UNDP is leading the process under GCF readiness process. The whole policy dimension is addressed under the project's overall support to the enabling environment for climate change adaptation.

##### 265. The Climate Change Coordination Commission (CCCC), headed by the First Vice Prime Minister of the Kyrgyz Republic, coordinates all the activities in the Kyrgyz Republic related to climate change. The CCCC consists of all heads of state bodies and is supported by a secretariat at the Ministry of Environment Protection. UNDP, as the lead governmental body for climate change, acts as its secretariat and is the UNFCCC and COP focal Point. SAPPI is also involved in the coordination process through its role as the Secretariat of the National Climate Change Management Unit. The key objective of the CCCC is to lead and coordinate activities of various agencies and ministries in implementing national adaptation strategies and climate change action plans, including those related to Climate Change and the Kyoto Protocol.

##### 267. The "Priority Directions for Adaptation to Climate Change in the Kyrgyz Republic until 2031" is being revised and mainstreamed into the National Strategy on Sustainable Development (NSSD) of 2015-2031, which will be enforced and in alignment with the updated versions of national climate change-related frameworks covering the period 2018–2024.

##### 268. The National Strategy for Sustainable Development 2013-2017 (NSSD) emphasizes the importance of climate change considerations as part of a sustainable development approach for the sustainable use of natural resources and sustainable management of ecosystems, including forests, water bodies, land and biodiversity. It is aligned with the National Strategy for Sustainable Development 2040, which is expected to be adopted in 2018. It is accompanied by the "Fifty

#### Output 1.1 will contribute to the implementation of procedures and regulations to ensure sustainable and climate change sensitive integrated planning, monitoring and evaluation of NRM.

Output 1.1 will support strengthening and harmonization of the policy and legal framework for forest-range resources based on a ecosystem approach by supporting stocktaking and analysis of current legislation for identification of legal gaps and ambiguities in sectorial policies and regulations; conduct special training workshops for key stakeholders, including government officials, parliamentarians, NGOs, and facilitate discussion and cooperation between agencies engaged in NRM, and advance legislation. Furthermore, output 1.1 will support the development of a national forest-range resources management framework required for integrated management.

Output 1.2 will enhance capacities on climate change risks and natural resource assessments, support further evidence-based decision making by strengthening and harmonizing the policy and legal framework for integrating NRM Planning, Monitoring and Evaluation tools and methodologies, and facilitate linkages between the evidences, data from the ground, information systems and the forest-range ecosystem planning processes. Output 1.2 will also contribute to the implementation of the Kyrgyz Republic Forest Range Sector Infrastructure Memorandum of Understanding Target: central local institutions, academies and CSOs, output 1.2 will support the development of a national forest-range resources management framework required for integrated management.

Output 1.3 will develop and improve skills and capacity in promotion of climate-resilient and adaptive NRM management and use in participating communities. The project will develop methodologies, guidelines and materials on elaboration of INDC/NDCs considering all issues of environment and biodiversity protection and ecosystem services.

Through investment on afforestation/reforestation and forest enrichment, and productive investment in the production of high-value timber species, output 1.3 will contribute to the improvement of the resilience of the population in the target areas and to decrease their exposure to climate change related risks as well.

The use of Georeferencing and Geospatial Analysis (FAO approach and tools) is a key part of the evidence-based approach of the project, to map and monitor (e.g. planting, carbon sequestration) project implementation progress in terms of geographical distribution and extent of forest cover increase which will be used

### E.2 Capacity building of national partners and assisting partners in Eritrea

#### Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

- FAO has considerable experience and expertise and a proven comparative advantage in Sustainable Forest Management (SFM) and Forest Landscape Restoration (FLR). The project, in the form of GIZ-FAS (I), proposes SFM by placing technical expertise in forestry at the disposal of member countries through field projects. (II) chairs the Collaborative Partnership on Forests (CPF) which brings together 14 major forestry-related international organizations, assists in coordinating their activities and provides a forum for dialogue among them.
- FAO’s work is based on national forest resource management in Eritrea. It supports the Ministry of Forestry and Regional Forest Commissions, provides primary venues for countries, civil society and private sector to convey their views and concerns regarding forestry issues. The project will be implemented through the following components: National Forest Program (NFP), National Forest Monitoring and Assessment Program, Global Forest Resources Assessment (FRA), and the Global Landscapes Initiative (GLI).

- FAO’s work on natural resources management in the Kyrgyz Republic and the wider region includes projects for forest resource assessment and monitoring, land degradation and desertification control, and sustainable forestry systems, including training on irrigation management to improve performance and tools and methods to improve water productivity, and identification of priorities for investment on water saving technologies in watersheds and training in the development of irrigation schemes. ### E.6. Efficiency and Effectiveness

#### E.6.1 Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

##### 224.
The design process considered the interests of the stakeholders and established a plan for their involvement, laying out specific activities in which stakeholders will participate. A National Stakeholder Platform (NSP) under the CCMG will be responsible for coordinating stakeholder engagement throughout the project's implementation. It was also agreed that SAEFF and ARIS will be the Operational Partners and key implementing agencies of the project. As a result, there is no need to establish an NSP. Instead, as part of the project's operations plan, there will be direct contact with stakeholders through CCMG to develop NRMCPs and which will provide feedback on the project's progress and implementation.

##### 225.
As part of the State Environmental Review under the SAEFF, of the Government of Kyrgyzstan, will disclose all relevant ESMP CS-FOR documentation on their website. However, it’s worth highlighting that the proposal has been accepted by the relevant communities and national institutions.

#### E.6.2 Co-financing, leveraging and mobilized long-term investments (mitigation only)

##### 226.
The Project budget will be US$49.58 million with a GCF grant of US$29.99 million or 60.4 percent of the total project cost. The level of concessionality of the operation is justified by two main factors: (a) by the substantial expected impact in contribution to sequester about 19.8 m tCO2e from forests and rangelands investments; coupled with (b) the co-financing ratio of 50 percent, which will be provided by the Kyrgyz government. This level is considered to mobilizing different stakeholders for joint investments in rangeland rehabilitation, which would not make the investment attractive otherwise.

##### 227.
The estimated benefits in separation of tonnes of CO2 equivalent is 19.8 million with an average cost of US$305 per tonne (based on the World Bank’s carbon price estimates). The total project benefit is US$30.37 million, with an average benefit of US$468 per ha as a result of the improved forests and rangeland management practices and their associated value chains. The average economic benefit of the operation per beneficiary (per year), as a result of the project interventions. The project can be considered sound from the financial perspective, given that it has a co-financing ratio of 50 percent (see Annexality Study and Section 7-1) and has a significant potential for scaling up for market interest from other donors.

##### 228.
The co-financing will total US$30 million, of which 77 percent from RKFQ: 16 percent from beneficiaries and about 2 percent from the Kyrgyz government. The co-financing ratio is justified under V0002 to support policy dialogue around evidence-based NRM governance (land tenure) output (1.1).

##### 229.
The high level of concessionality of the operation is justified by the public good nature of the mitigation interventions in the project, which are not technically a public good but are considered as such because they reduce GHG emissions. Additionally, the interventions with expected high financial returns, such as the one in the Climate Change Mitigation Component 3, will be undertaken entirely under private sector management and will have a lower level of concessionality. All private investments generated under Component 3 will be undertaken entirely with private funds. The co-financing ratio is justified by the need to support capacity development to improve management of common pool resources mobilized under the same Component.

##### 230.
The specific grant:co-financing ratio of Component 3 is at 1:5, including RKFQ and beneficiaries’ contributions. For the other components, the co-financing ratio is justified by the public good nature of the mitigation interventions in the project, which are not technically a public good but are considered as such because they reduce GHG emissions. Additionally, the interventions with expected high financial returns, such as the one in the Climate Change Mitigation Component 3, will be undertaken entirely under private sector management and will have a lower level of concessionality. All private investments generated under Component 3 will be undertaken entirely with private funds. The co-financing ratio is justified by the need to support capacity development to improve management of common pool resources mobilized under the same Component.

### 238. The project's CO2 equivalent potential sequestration was estimated utilizing the FAO developed (En- Area Carbon Balance Tool (EX-ACT)). The tool assesses the net balance of iCO2 Greenhouse Gases that were emitted or carbon sequestered as a result of project implementation compared to "without project" scenarios. The analysis is based on the assumption that the project will achieve the best possible Tier 2 values specific to the project's interventions. For the assessment of the dynamics of the livestock sector, the Global Livestock Environmental Assessment and Monitoring (GLEAM) model was used. To assess the socio-economic importance in the area, a specific analysis was carried out using the Global Livestock Environment Assessment and Monitoring System (GLEAMS). The GLEAMS summarizes a biophysical model of livestock supply chains that calculates animal herd dynamics, feed ratios, production and greenhouse gas emissions with Tier 2 methodology (IPCC, 2006), with a life-cycle approach.

#### 237. The results of the analysis show how effective carbon sequestration can be achieved through an integrated approach that takes into account forest management, improved pasture management, and livestock management.

##### 238. The project targets afforestation, forest restoration, grassland management, perennial crops and agricultural land rehabilitation activities on a total area of 709,174 ha.
- Carbon sequestration per activity (iCO2 equivalent):
    - 6,000 ha under afforestation / reforestation / forest enrichment: 720,608
    - 58,359 ha of forest on land under improved management: 3,478,419
    - 646,275 ha of improved rangeland: 14,923,368
    - 3,100 ha for orchards: 469,416
- Total forest area: 19,710,354
- Total land under improved management and manure management: 18,644,445
- Total land under rangeland: 19,712,564

##### 239. As per livestock production, when comparing to a BAU situation with no technology or practice improvements (i.e., very low livestock productivity), projecting the results of the improved practices over 20 years, the project will result in saving 149,545 iCO2eq, with an extra total 8.346 tons of protein produced. With a baseline scenario that includes technology and practice improvements (i.e., higher livestock productivity), the project would result in saving 1,077,451 iCO2eq, for an extra 974 tons of protein produced.

Expected volume of finance to be leveraged by the proposed programme/project and as a result of the Fund's financing, through grantmaking, public and private sources:

##### 240. As described in section II.1, Tier 3 projects are subject to a grant high concessionality for activities with higher potential for carbon sequestration. Similarly, the project provides a limited concessionality where the project co-financing from RRFDP (USD 5 million) is directed towards forest management, but guarantees substantial private benefits. As such, most of the project co-financing from RRFDP (USD 15 million) is directed to other activities (agents of change).

In order to ensure the necessary conditions in carbon sequestration, the project envisages a contribution of the beneficiaries in-kind to both investment in forests (around 20 percent of the cost of investments in forest management) and in livestock (around 35 percent of the cost of investment), for a total amount of USD 3.6 million or 7 percent of the total project cost.

### F.1 Economic and Financial Analysis

#### 243. The economic and financial analysis focuses on selected economic activities supported by the project. Following the project investment structure, the analysis presents two sets of illustrative models of representative investments.

##### Green investments for forest and rangeland rehabilitation models (Component 2), including afforestation/reforestation, forest management, rangeland management, and degraded forests.
- Afforestation/reforestation: The forest transition theory through INFORMCARP describes a series of stages associated with the development of forests. These stages are not mutually exclusive but rather represent different conditions that may be suitable for the investment (see Chapter 4 of the Feasibility Study and the attached spreadsheets). The investments in this category include planting trees, reforestation, and afforestation on degraded lands with higher resistance to climate stresses.
- Forest management: This includes activities such as sustainable forest management practices, carbon sequestration, and ecosystem benefits. To ensure financial viability of these investments, the project will make use of co-financing from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and other sources.

##### Climate-sensitive value chains models (Component 3), including investment supported by KOF's loans in fruit; nuts; orchards; nurseries; conservation agriculture for cereals; greenhouses; beekeeping. Inlier: further investments will be made to support climate-smart agricultural practices, such as drip irrigation, which will be made available by the CS-FOR co-financing, coupled with training, demonstrations and advisory services provided by the project.

#### 243. The impact of climate pattern
- Based on the climate scenario (Reference in Section C.1 and Chapter 1 of the Feasibility Study), the average temperature is expected to increase by 0.5°C per decade.
- The impacts of these stresses are applied on both, with and without project scenarios for all models (except for greenhouse production). The impact of climate change on agricultural productivity in the project area is significant; it is worsened when combined with land degradation and poor soil quality.

### Financial analysis: results

244. The analysis takes into account two time horizons for the projected net incremental benefits. With a 10-year horizon, the project is expected to have an overall poor financial performance, affected by the slow realization of benefits from the forest-related interventions. A 20-years projection period is set in order to account for the phasing and gestation period of the investments. The results are presented as incremental net present values (NPV) at a discount rate of 11 percent used to assess the viability and robustness of investments. In the latter, the FIRR is estimated at 11.1 percent, and the IRR is 8.5 percent. The financial results for the project are presented in Table 2.3. The results from aggregated financial IRR and NPV are presented in the "Summary" spreadsheet of the Integrated Financial Model. Considering that the investments are concessionary (as per the terms of investment under Component 1), and the results are presented in a disaggregated way to show attribution:

(i) **Component 2 Investments**. On one side, forestry and pasture management activities (with 80 percent of investment allocated for forest-related interventions) have negative NPV (-US$35 million), mostly negating benefits from supportive operations (associated with long-term benefits), but positive NPVs with wildlife related activities and range land management. The financial results are presented in Table 2.4. On the other side, investments in agriculture and rangelands interventions were -20.9 percent US$ -113.3 million USD respectively, while financial IRR and NPV are negative (-8.5 percent) and positive (US$67.5 million). The results show that the forestry and pasture management interventions for the private sector (this, in turn), provides a justification for the concessionality associated with the project.

(ii) **Component 3 Investments**. On the other side, for climate-sensitive value chain models (where the project investments are not expected to have any significant impact on the economic activities performed by smallholder farmers), the financial results show the worthlessness of the investments, with a significant increase in gross and net returns. More specifically, the NPV is estimated at -US$105 million for the 20-year horizon, while IRR is 8.3 percent and FIRR at 28.1 percent exceeding the expected rate of return of KDFC loans (10 percent). The financial results for Component 3 investments are presented in Table 2.4. The net NPV is estimated at US$7 million, while IRR is 5.0 percent.

### Sensitivity analysis on financial performance

245. Impacts of climate change. All models take into account the effect of climate change on the economic activities performed by smallholder farmers and the forest-related interventions (Component 1). In particular, increases of costs due to climate change reflects the need for replanting seedlings due to the influence of higher temperatures and droughts, as well as increased costs associated with the maintenance of the land plots or the effect of landslides and wildfires on forests and rangeland infrastructures. Correspondingly, decreases of yields and productivity are expected in the case of climate change impacts (Component 3). The model also reflects reduced pasture productivity and crop production. With the above elements taken into account, the aggregated project's investments are expected to generate more than US$10 million per year for the next 20 years.

The operation is not financially viable in the unlikely case that costs increase by more than 60 percent, or benefits decrease by less of a percentage in each year horizon (refer Table 2.4).

### Economic analysis: results

246. The CS-FOR economic analysis is based on the aggregation of incremental benefits from all models taking into account the associated environmental benefits. The benefits are set under the assumption that about 80 percent of the project investments will be in supportive value chains, while the remaining 20 percent will be in forest-related activities. It is expected that the Project Investments in supportive value chains will generate more than 3,300 full-time equivalent jobs when the activities reach their full capacity.

### Assumptions:
The economic discount rate of 7.5 percent based on the current reference rate of the National Bank of Virginia Region is used in this analysis.

### Valuation of environmental benefits:

#### 247. Carbon sequestration potential.
Under the community investment in forest and rangeland rehabilitation, the project would result in carbon sequestration amounting to about 18.9 kg CO₂eq per ha via reforestation-afforestation of 65,000 ha of severely damaged forests, rehabilitation of over 645,000 hectares of degraded pastures, improved management of over 56,000 ha of forest and rangeland, and the establishment of 12,000 hectares of agroforestry systems.

#### 248. Economic benefits from ecosystem services.
The project would result in economic benefits from the rehabilitated forests and pastures due to Project support. These ecosystem services represent public goods, which are not captured by markets or by the Gross Domestic Product (GDP). The analysis considered the shadow price of US$0.10/ha as the social value of carbon (World Bank, 2017).

#### 249. Economic benefits from ecosystem services.
The project would result in economic benefits from the rehabilitated forests and pastures due to Project support. These ecosystem services represent public goods, which are not captured by markets or by the Gross Domestic Product (GDP). The analysis considered the shadow price of US$0.10/ha as the social value of carbon (World Bank, 2017).

#### 250. Economic Results.
Considering the above-mentioned benefits and cost streams, in the long term, the net incremental economic benefit from the project accounting for the carbon sequestration potential and related ecosystem services generates an economic rate of return of 71.3 percent and a net present value of US$83.5 million.

#### 251. Sensitivity Analysis.
Various hypotheses were tested to assess the project's sensitivity to critical variables (summarized in Table 4.13). They include:

- (ii) Changes in potential sequestration or in its valuation: The analysis also took into account the possibility of a drop of the social value of carbon to half of the current estimated value, showing any positive economic benefit from carbon sequestration.

### F.2 Technical Evaluation

The proposed investment and technologies have been selected and proposed as the most suitable to the project target group (smallholder pastoralists, women, youth, and other vulnerable groups such as those with disabilities, ATLAS), the socio-economic conditions, livelihoods and balance of empowerment between disadvantaged groups and wealthier ones. The project is expected to contribute significantly to climate resilience aimed at maximizing carbon sequestration and potential market for the products of the related economic activities.

### Pasture rotation is the main strategy proposed and driving the investment in pasture rehabilitation. The current practice of continuous grazing has led to overgrazing, which results in a loss of productivity by the end of the season that the pasture is evenly overgrazed, and this situation is repeated and reinforced from year to year. Rotation of livestock between pastures allows for the recovery of degraded pastures through natural regeneration without necessarily reducing the number of grazing livestock. Degraded pastures in Kyrgyzstan have a measured

# APPRAISAL SUMMARY

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 80 OF 99

### Forests and Grasslands

#### Standing Forage Yield

The standing forage yield is between 0.3 and 1 ton DWha. Conservatively, the potential post of biomass on Kyrgyz pastures is at least 2 tons DWha.

#### Grazing Land Management

If Kyrgyz pastures can be managed with a pasture rotation so that grasses overshoot the level while they still provide forage for grazing livestock, the contribution to carbon sequestration is substantial. The above-ground biomass of perennial grasslands in degraded conditions (9 tons DWha) could be conserved at 2-3 tons DWha.

#### Biomass Potential

For carbon accounting purposes, the above-ground biomass with a rotation system can be conserved at 1 ton DWha. This is equivalent to 0.5 tons of CO2 per hectare. The Kyrgyz pasture biomass (shoots and roots) rises from 1.5 to 3 tons DWha in a degraded condition to 9 tons DWha when plant growth is allowed to approach its potential.

### Forests

#### Forest Types and Species

Forests are classified into four types based on forest conditions, an estimate has been produced on the availability of lands in target areas. For afforestation/restoration planted forests on barren, clear-cut or pasture land, the choice of species and the estimates on the potential areas for forestry investment have been made depending on factors including the available area, soil type, climate, and water supply. The four types are:

1. Zone (one) of desert pistachio forests and small semi-shrublands: occupancy low and quite high foot hills in the range from 900 to 2,500 m above sea level.
2. Zone (two) of steppe pistachio forest of cereal meadows and steppes is confined to high foothills, located at an altitude of 900 to 1,150 m (approximately 3,300 ft.) above sea level. Vegetation is diverse: in the lower part of this zone are found shrubs such as pistachio, walnut, and apple trees; in the upper part are found juniper, alder, alfalfa, barberry (Berberis spp.), cherry plum (Prunus cerasus), and others species grow.
3. Walnut-apple forest zone (three) is located at an altitude of 1,200 to 2,500 m above sea level. This zone is represented by walnut-apple forests. Walnut forests here are of seed-bearing origin, of different age and are confined to the top of hills and mountain slopes. The lower part of this zone is occupied by pistachio forest; at the top edge of walnut-apple forests is formed by maple trees, which rise to an altitude of 2,500-2,800 m.
4. (four) subalpine shrublands are located in the upper part of the mountain slopes and on the tops of mountains. The components of this zone are rare maple trees from Acer turkestanica, juniper, fir (Abies spp.), bushes and subalpine meadows with grasses such as Festuca altaica, Poa alpina, and others.

#### Forest Management

The main part of the forest is occupied by pistachio forests. The lower part of the forest is occupied by walnut-apple forests. The upper part of the forest is occupied by subalpine shrublands. The potential value chain component has been pre-defined on the basis of the Livelihood Study, on results of ongoing projects (such as the IFAD-funded Access to Markets Project). The project will consider the selection of the models included in the detailed financial and economic analysis. Specific analyses have been carried out on opportunities for forest products trade, including timber sales, wood fuel, and non-timber forest products.

#### Environmental Assessment

The Project Area consists of assessment of lost market opportunities for nuts and dried fruits on nearby markets (which result in a total loss of $10 million per year), the potential for increased production of pistachio nuts and other nuts; increased sales of animals to slaughterhouses; and other analyses. The analyses have confirmed the need to proceed with pre-assessment activities, including an environmental assessment, which will be carried out by the World Bank in close collaboration with the Kyrgyz Government, with the potential to adopt energy saving and green technologies (vacuum and solar driers, agricultural machinery, etc.).

### Environmental Social Assessment

#### Gender Considerations

During project formulation, an environmental and social management framework was developed as the initial phase of due diligence (see the Environmental and Social Management Framework annexed to this Proposal). An initial risk assessment has been carried out. The Project Area is considered to be moderate risk for all in-depth description; see Annex 5-Section 5.1 of the attached Environmental and Social Management Framework. The project activities will not extend beyond the area of influence of the project and are neither irreversible nor cumulative.

The project will proactively work with stakeholders to lessen environmental impacts in all target areas: an ecosystem-based approach is being used for this purpose. The project will also ensure that its planned development activities are purely designed to improve management of natural resources and ecosystem service functions while improving livelihoods, including through the use of appropriate technologies. The project will not interfere with legally protected areas nor in their buffer zones. The project will not deny access to certain
