## GREEN CLIMATE FUND GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL | PAGE OF E7

areas a priori. Restoration activities will be selected and executed in agreement with those communities that are legally entitled to benefits from targeted lands. Specifically regarding pasture rotation and common restriction of access to certain areas of pastures, this is determined by the INRMCCs decided by the CLMGS where all users are represented.

The high conservation value forests (HCVFs) will be protected in accordance with the Kyrgyzstan Forest Code. Restriction of access to certain areas is according to the law (i.e., in SFF or in Parks) and no compensation is envisaged. Main tree species are those matched per Lake Lebedin conditions, which are: Picea obovata, Pinus sylvestris, and Abies cilicica under the Kyrgyzstan Forest Code with SAEEF. The project supports only the inclusion of endemic or non-invasive domesticated tree species from the Kyrgyzstan and the Central Asia region. Priority consideration will be given to those species that are not invasive (e.g., in Europe) and are not listed as endangered or are under threat (International Union for Conservation of Nature). Selected species have been identified among the species present on the project site, and will be planted by local communities. The selection of tree species is based on the ecological and economic conditions of the area.

The selection of species will be done according to ecological and climatic trends and projections of target areas (reference Project A). The project will not invest in forest restoration in forests publicly owned by the State where the main purpose of forests is not a direct economic benefit. In such cases, the project will only support the planting of trees that are native to the area; if the incentives, trees will be planted on only dedicated public land (State Forest Fund and/or Municipal land), and will be monitored for their survival.

### Potential social impacts identified in the assessment

258. Potential social impacts identified in the assessment are related to unequal participation because of gender discrimination or discrimination against other vulnerable groups, including youth. A Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan (GAP) has been developed as part of the project preparation phase. The assessment identifies that certain groups in the rural community are socially disadvantaged, particularly poor families, female-headed households, and young people. The project will ensure that all stakeholders have equal access to information and incorporates special actions to ensure their equal participation in the project. For young women and men, in addition to ensuring their equal participation in the project, the project will provide them with training opportunities for their engagement in business opportunities in the project supported value chains by proactively including them in business-related activities.

259. The ESMS identified the most relevant mitigation actions, including compliance with standards, and training, as appropriate. During project implementation, activities will be identified and FAO's screening procedures will be implemented to ensure that no adverse social impacts are caused or exacerbated during project implementation. Social Management Plans will be developed as necessary and mitigation actions will be monitored throughout the life of the project.

The project will not fund or be involved in sub-projects / investments rated at high risk. Independent party will deal with all sub-project proposals, including those that have been identified by ESS (e.g., for community-based forest management; see ESS moderate) for sub-project proposals using ESS standards and have FAO approval and then forward to RSKF and partners for further evaluation. The project will ensure that all sub-projects are screened against the ESS as ESS Management Plan Specialist will be responsible for all sub-project screening and categorization and will ensure the preparation of a management plan.

260. A stakeholder engagement process was carried out to identify and engage with all stakeholders of the project, from national to local authorities, relevant technical institutions, to NGOs and CSOs, etc. The process (divided into two major steps) aimed at identifying key stakeholders and their roles in the project implementation phase as well as in the initial phase of the proposal. A project level grievance mechanism is being established to ensure all stakeholders the possibility to express their concerns and suggestions on any aspect of the project. At least 50% of the members should be youth organization in each Ayyl Aimak. At least 30% of the members should be women.

261. The project will support the Government's commitment to gender equality, including through the Government's provisions of equal gender, researchers and development practitioners not all Kyrgyzstan faces high gender inequalities. Deep-rooted patriarchal attitudes and conventional beliefs of women's roles and responsibilities in the family and society are still prevalent. Women have limited access to education and training opportunities as well as decision-making power on issues related to their lives. Women face many social, economic, and cultural barriers that affect their ability to participate fully in public life. Women suffer from gender-based violence and lack of access to justice. Women's rights and women's empowerment are not strong. Economic empowerment of women is lagging behind as business opportunities for women are limited. The project will support the Government's commitment to ensure one pasture ticket is allowed per family, women have limited access to decision making in pasture. Also gender-based

**F.4 Financial Management and Procurement**

262. The FAO Representation in Kyrgyzstan will be the Budget Holder (BH) of this project, and will be responsible for implementing all financial management and procurement activities in accordance with the Rules and Regulations of GCF.

263. Financial management in relation to the GCF resources directly managed by FAO will be carried out in accordance with FAO’s rules and procedures.

264. The Operational Partners (OPs) SAEF and ARIS are accountable to FAO for achieving the agreed project results, and shall ensure that all financial records made available by FAO. Financial management and reporting for the funds transferred to SAEF and ARIS will be done by them in accordance with terms, conditions, formats and requirements of GCF.

265. Financial resources received from GCF shall be recorded as liabilities on the books of the OPs. The funds received from GCF shall not be carried out under its own financial regulations, rules and procedures, which shall provide adequate accounting standards for the project. The OPs shall maintain separate accounts in accordance with their respective GAAP, including the amount of any unspent funds and interest accrued.

266. Financial resources transferred to the OPs will be subject to the same terms and conditions as the commitments of GCF and in line with FAO Country Project budget codes. The OPs will prepare the financial records in accordance with terms, conditions, formats and requirements of GCF. The OPs shall provide the necessary information for the preparation of the final and financial reports of the OPs. The subsequent installments can be released only based on the BH confirmation that all expenditures are eligible and all OPAs requirements are fulfilled to the satisfaction of the FAO. The BH will withhold any payment from the OP in case of non-compliance with the respective obligations delineated in the OPAs.

**Procurement**

267. The project shall be implemented through a combination of direct implementation by FAO and procurement on a “Best Value for Money” basis, and in accordance with the Rules and Regulations of GCF. It requires analysis of needs and constraints, including forecast of the reasonable time required to execute the procurement process. Procurement and delivery of goods, works or services (i.e., project follow-up) shall be carried out by FAO. The procurement of supplies, equipment and services (i.e., Manual Sections 502 and 507). Manual Section 502: “Procurement of Goods, Works and Services” shall apply to the procurement of goods, works or services for the implementation of projects and services on behalf of the Organization, in all offices and in all locations, with the exception of the procurement actions described above. Manual Section 507: “Procurement of Supplies, Equipment and Services” shall apply to procure supplies, equipment and services as defined by the rules and principles that govern the use of Letters of Agreement (LoA) by FAO for the timely acquisition of services from eligible suppliers in accordance with the Rules and Regulations of GCF. The procurement process will aim at achieving an optimum combination of expected whole life costs and benefits (“Best Value for Money”).

268. The project shall be implemented through a combination of direct implementation by FAO and procurement on a “Best Value for Money” basis, which will be the basis of requests for procurement actions during implementation. The plan will include a description of the goods, works, or services to be procured, estimated budget and source of funding, schedule of procurement activities and proposed method of procurement. The project shall be implemented in accordance with the Rules and Regulations of GCF. The plan must contain reasonable projections that will be corrected as information becomes available.

# G.1 Risk Assessment Summary

The project is set around an ambitious target of transforming the management of NR towards higher participation and higher productivity livestock grazing systems. The technical and operational risk associated with this choice will be strengthened through substantial support provided by the project to the investment, and through the availability to take advantage of existing knowledge and experience in the field. However, there are risks that may hinder the actual situation on the ground of all investment carried out. Such dynamics are expected to encounter some initial resistance from local communities and other stakeholders who have a vested interest in maintaining their current practices or other interests held in land. Nevertheless, the strength of the project in supporting the paradigm shift relies in its approach to address only one of these risks, which is the need for the provision of NR-related services that go beyond the NR related subsectors (e.g., climate-sensitive value chains) and also in support for the use of evidence and availability of information. A number of detailed risks are associated with the investment; they are described in the following section.

# G.2 Risk Factors and Mitigation Measures

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Insufficient inter-ministerial coordination between SAEFF, MAFF, NACR, NRDA, and others and among local self-government bodies, Lesothos and Phitsanulok Province. | Technical and operational | Low (<5% of project value) | High |

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Risk will be mitigated by the establishment of a dedicated National Platform – acting as Project Steering Committee (see Chapter 5 of the Feasibility Study) to be established and chaired by the Vice Prime Minister to ensure inter-ministerial coordination and cooperation. Embracing an inclusive approach including representatives from all relevant stakeholders, including local communities, will be critical in this regard. The role of the CCCC Platform will have a strong catalytic function to ensure the endorsement of policy and regulatory frameworks required to support the project’s implementation.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Since 1995, livestock numbers have been increasing particularly in Phitsanulok Province. The number of cattle, horses, sheep and goats increased by 17 percent between 2008-2013. This has put pressure on pasture reserves. Control of herd numbers is required to ensure the sustainability of improved pastoral management and the subsequent carbon sequestration benefits. | Technical and operational | Medium (5-12% of project value) | Medium |

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Through negotiated INRCPMs, the project will support the development of capacity development to ensure that PUA’s and their Pasture Committees have the knowledge and authority to shift from a low productive livestock grazing to a high productivity livestock production system. This requires a significantly lower number of animals to reach parity of output. As such, the project will provide technical assistance in terms of training and capacity building on herd management behaviour into pasture rotation systems that support higher livestock production. Through Component 3 in support of climate change adaptation, the project will also provide financial incentives for PUA’s to reduce their livestock numbers.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 71 OF 99

### Selected Risk Factor 3

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Low capacity of local forestry enterprises to work in the joint forest management (JFM) framework, as assessed by FAO and the GCF in the framework of projects where they have been involved. This is due to low human capacities of staff employed in such state enterprises, lack of technical knowledge on best available techniques for reforestation, afforestation and pasture rehabilitation | Technical and operational | Low (<5% of project value) | Medium |

**Mitigation Measure(1):**

The project will deploy substantial capacity building in LaoLekhnoi in the Project Areas to ensure that their staff gain up-to-date technical capacity to carry out sustainable (and with high survival rates) afforestation/reforestation and forest enhancement activities in SFM, but also to guarantee a basis of evidence on successful results of an integrated approach. Moreover, it will provide financial support for forestry investments, with higher guarantees that the investments are followed-up on the years subsequent to the first.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Changes in the Government’s political vision such as the decentralization of resource management with high changeability and low predictability, which could affect the project implementation in the way it involves local communities | Technical and operational | Medium (5-120% of project value) | Medium |

**Mitigation Measure(4):**

In order to mitigate the risk, the project will implement a continuous engagement process as key strategic element of implementation. This follows up on the process already initiated during the project design stage (see ESMP and stakeholder analysis). The project will also ensure that all stakeholders are involved in the planning phase of the project preparation and implementation to ensure buy-in, and supporting communications campaigns to disseminate results of the studies in order to raise public awareness on climate change risks and to ensure wide political support.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

| Description | Risk category | Level of impact | Probability of risk occurring |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Saturation of the agricultural lending market when expected disbursement rates by KfW and partner banks may not be met | Financial | High (>20% of project value) | Medium |

**Mitigation Measure(5):**

As a mitigation strategy, the project and KfW will continue to engage domestic banks interested in the development of the “green” agricultural sector, and with banks (e.g. Ayil Bank, Bat Tushum Bank, Kompanion Bank, and Mol Builek Finance) that have already expressed interest in supporting the project. This will create assurance in their capacity to develop new innovative financial products and solutions within the CS-FOR scope. As an element increasing the credit worthiness of potential clients, the project will ensure complementarity of TA and KfW funding with other sources of financing (e.g., from the National Bank of Kyrgyz Republic). In cooperation with the National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic will explore new opportunities in agricultural lending for product development in cooperation with specialized financial service providers and share these learnings among interested parties.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE 72 OF 99

### Selected Risk Factor 7

#### Description
Forest investment may suffer from a downward spiral of illegal logging, driven by increased demand for wood consumption and urban construction needs.

#### Risk category
Technical and environmental

#### Level of impact
High (>20% of project value)

#### Probability of risk occurring
Low (20%)

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Through an evidence-based approach, the project will be able to ensure significantly higher transparency and faster reporting on areas being logged, providing especially SAEPAF and the Leskhose with the information to ensure appropriate decision-making. The project will also provide training for forest management authorities and communities through the support to climate-sensitive value chains and the associated increase in technical capacity. Component 3 of the project will be implemented by a private sector partner, which has extensive experience in this area.

### Selected Risk Factor 8

#### Description
The mobilization at local level and the dialogue around the livelihoods of forest-dependent communities can be distorted incentives as well as petty corruption leading to misappropriation of funds.

#### Risk category
Social and environmental

#### Level of impact
Medium (5-120% of project value)

#### Probability of risk occurring
Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Building upon the experience of Alikis and other partners in social mobilization, the project will adopt an inclusive process to engage communities and ensure that all voices are heard. The project will also work on increasing transparency and accountability. Moreover, broad and active communication and awareness campaigns will be conducted as part of the project engagement rules as well as implementation borders and benchmarks.

### Selected Risk Factor 9

#### Description
Natural hazards especially mudslides, landslides and flash floods can occur in areas where the investments affecting the project's implementation and investments are located.

#### Risk category
Social and environmental

#### Level of impact
Low (<5% of project value)

#### Probability of risk occurring
Low

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Part of the strategy of the project is to assist with range management and above all forests rehabilitation where the investments can be more beneficial in terms of reducing the exposure to such natural disasters.

Other Potential Risks

The National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic (NBKR), the central bank officially operates a free floating exchange rate with inflation targeting. In the recent years, the country experienced a steady depreciation of the exchange rate with the devaluation reaching 20% in 2015 and 34% in 2016. However, following the election of President Almazbek Atambayev at the end of that year, the stabilization of the Russian rouble in 2016 and 2017 led to an appreciation of the KGS, which averaged around KGS 89 per USD during this period. The project is expected to benefit from the country's ongoing consumption of political and economic stability and with an effective free floating exchange policy with inflation targeting, the purchase power parity of the project budget allocation is expected to remain stable.

### H.1. Logic Framework

Please specify the project framework in accordance with the GCF's Performance Measurement Framework under the Results Management Framework.

#### H.1.1. Paradigm Shift Objectives and Impacts at the Fund Level

**Paradigm objective:** The project will support carbon emission reduction and enhanced carbon storage (carbon sinks), while recognizing that climate change mitigation is a subset of broader development objectives, including poverty alleviation, food security, natural resource planning and monitoring system(s) (ii) community-based investments in natural forest resources management, sustainable land use practices, improved livelihoods, conservation of rangelands and prevention of further deforestation; (iv) diversification of options for community livelihoods.

**Drift-off pathways:** Sustainable development pathways

The country will thus shift from a local economy that is currently negatively impacting on carbon storage potential of ecosystems (forest and rangeland) to a low-carbon emission economy where mitigation strategies are integrated into broader development objectives, including poverty alleviation, food security, natural resource planning and monitoring system(s), community-based investments in natural forest resources management, sustainable land use practices, improved livelihoods, conservation of rangelands and prevention of further deforestation; (iv) diversification of options for community livelihoods.

Indicator: PSM Degree to which the Fund contributes to low-emission sustainable development

Through this project, the Fund will contribute to the achievement of the expected results by increasing climate adaptive and mitigation-oriented productivity through implementation of improved land use practices, including forest management and rangeland conservation, enabling environment for diversification, increase of efficiency and competitiveness reducing the reliance on ecosystem services such as protection (reduced climate vulnerability), biodiversity and livelihoods.

Ultimately, the expression of this project will serve as a driver for dissemination of good practices in low-carbon emission development pathways, including forest management and rangeland conservation subsistence livestock production to a diversified and climate-sensitive value chain business oriented enterprises.

Indicator: PSM Degree to which the Fund contributes to climate-resilient sustainable development

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL | PAGE BD OF 99

### Output 2.1

The activity will mobilize and train 500 key agents (municipalities, local communities, forest users, etc.) on the new forest management and restoration and their communities will include social mobilization activities to ensure that they are aware of the importance of the establishment of new forests and the management teams at all levels. The activity will also provide training for 100 key agents (Landowners, NGOs, CSOs, forest users and experts, etc.) on forest management and restoration in general as well as specific topics such as: (i) sustainable land use planning, (ii) further engagement in PULSAs, WILCs and other community-based forest management initiatives, (iii) participatory forest resource management, (iv) natural resources georeferencing and mapping; (v) the development of a forest inventory for the purpose of forest management, and (vi) technical assistance on land tenure within the framework of the national land use planning process.

The activity will support the pasture development with dedicated technical assistance and will include: (i) training and study tours to identify best practices in pasture management; (ii) capacity building implementation; (iii) capacity development on pasture rotation and grazing systems; (iv) participatory forest resource management of training of trainers, the activity will provide trainings and workshops for local stakeholders (land users, landowners, etc.) on sustainable pasture use and benefits of natural grazing and erosion control. The activity will also strengthen CUMICAP capacities in pasture management. # H.2. Arrangements for Monitoring, Reporting and Evaluation

## 271. A detailed description of the Means of Verification of the indicators is provided in Chapter 6 of the Feasibility Study.

Data will be collected through a combination of field data collection, remote sensing and geospatial analysis. Data will originate from described sources and will be generated in the geo-referenced M&E database. Data will be presented on an annual basis to the GCF Board and will be used for reporting purposes as well as for monitoring and evaluation.

## 272. CS-FOR will apply FAO/NME standard procedures and will be compliant with the GCF performance measurement framework. GCF will manage and coordinate reporting to the GCF according to agreed standards and procedures. The project will ensure that all data collected is of high quality, relevant, reliable and comparable across time periods and towards the explicit goals, outcomes and outputs identified as part of the Theory of Change.

(i) Field data Collection: field data will be collected by the M&E unit via dedicated activities planned with the GCF Board. Data collection will also include a baseline survey to collect information on the project area and from communities following the HI survey methodological approach and specifications. Additionally, the project has planned for a series of monitoring visits to collect data on the progress made in the project.

(ii) Georeferencing: This will ensure a unique relation between project activities and geographical coordinates collected according to a specific procedure (Ref. Georeferencing Procedure). The County Administrative Unit (CAU) will be used as the reference unit for georeferencing. Georeferencing will allow the project to profile the last population census data by the Ministry of the Country and will also provide information on downstream communities.

(iii) Geo-spatial analysis: The M&E unit will monitor activities and processes through a series of remote sensing and photointerpretation analysis that have been made accessible to the country via the FAO’s Earth Map Application. The application will allow stakeholders to access, analyze and interpret satellite imagery and other geospatial data into the planning and decision-making process. The integration of “geo-spatial” elements will allow stakeholders to monitor project progress in terms of land use change, deforestation, forest degradation, etc., as well as the distribution of population and livelihoods that are a non-negligible part of an evidence-based and informed decision- making process. The M&E unit will also plan for additional national and regional data collection activities that will support the understanding of climate change impacts at local level.

275. Having georeferenced investments as well as soft activities (i.e., training, capacity development) will allow the project to address indicators with objective elements of evaluation.

The PMU will be responsible for the M&E unit in charge of monitoring and reporting on the progress of the project. The M&E unit is expected to understand if activities have been executed. If there has been successful execution, the project will be able to demonstrate that it has achieved its objectives linked to the project's theory of change. The use of such an approach will allow the project to monitor and report on the achievement of advanced IT skills. Basic software is available under license (i.e., ArcGIS) for data collection and management. The M&E unit will also be able to use available small phones/tablets, regardless of their operating systems: both Android and iOS are supported by ArcGIS. This will allow for easy georeferencing and data management. Additionally, FAO will provide targeted training on the use of ArcGIS for project partners/stakeholders during the start-up phase of the project.

276. The PMU will be responsible for the M&E unit. The M&E unit will be composed of a team leader (ML), a GIS specialist (MGIS), and a data manager (MDM) (see Figure H 2.1).

277. During execution of the project, the M&E unit will ensure, among the others, support at the following levels:

(i) Monitoring of execution performances: The unit will be responsible for (a) collecting data from identified sources and (b) ensuring that these data are geo-referenced and stored in a central database (i.e., Project Atlas). The M&E unit will ensure correct and efficient filing of collected GPS coordinates. Once coordinates will start populating the Project Atlas, the project will be able to monitor execution performances at different levels: activities, locations, and time. This aspect of monitoring is paramount for the project as it will allow for a better understanding of impacts of project activities. Showing activities in their exact location, visualizing relations with the context, will allow a more accurate evaluation of the project's impact on the ground. The M&E unit will also ensure that data are verified and validated by the PMU and that they are disaggregated by gender among other variables as available in the specific Working Paper (linked at the last page of this Funding Proposal).

(ii) Community monitoring and ground truthing: The project will apply a new approach to monitoring the participation of communities in ecosystem-based NRM. This approach is based on the theory of change that defines community participation in ecosystem-based NRM. The M&E unit will ensure annual consultations with communities, which will be used as an opportunity for communities to provide feedback and geo-referencing comments. Communities will participate directly both in planning, according to the criteria designed for the activity, and in monitoring execution performances. This particular aspect of the M&E strategy will also allow for enhanced and evidence-based sharing of local knowledge with all stakeholders. As with all the other activities, data derived from this exercise will be part of the Project Atlas and available at the PMU's disposal.

(iii) Strategic level: Annual results and related analysis, jointly reviewed by FAO and the PMU, will form the basis for strategic decisions to be taken by the PMU and the PSC in order to support its role and to secure transparency and evidence-based strategy development.

278. Independent Evaluation: Independent interim and final evaluations will be carried out in accordance to the FAO procedures for the evaluation of initiatives funded by voluntary contributions.

### Addendum 1: Additional information on climate rationale for the Project Area

The Kyrgyz Republic has an extreme continental climate, milder and that is mitigated by increased cloudiness and precipitation due to orographic effects. Therefore, Kyrgyzstan comprises a number of regions with specific microclimates that are governed by altitude and proximity to water bodies, plains and deserts.

At lower altitudes, the microclimate is drier, showing the typical characteristics of continental climate with marked seasonal variations in temperature and precipitation. In contrast, at higher altitudes, temperatures are lower and humidity increases so that diurnal variations and differences between the summer and winter seasons are more pronounced.

The reported influence of orography, altitude, distance from drier areas such as the Fergana valley, is particularly evident when examining the precipitation (sum average) and temperature (MIN and MAX average) data for the four districts of Ak-Talas (Naryn region), Chaldakai and Alay valleys (Osh region), and Uzgen and Suzak valleys (Chuy region). Analysis of the climate data shows that precipitation is highest in the western regions of the country, particularly in the southwestern regions of the country. Analyzing precipitation trends, such diversification appears even more pronounced between the Fergana valley and the West Tian Shan mountain range (that acts as a barrier) and slightly increasing in the other regions.

The southwestern districts of the country are therefore the most exposed to precipitation and temperatures changes. The Project Area is located within the Chaldakai and Alay valleys, which are part of the Ak-Talas Atlas and Annex 2, Chapter 1 (pages 29-33). Reportedly, ecosystems and livelihoods will be impacted as well with a decrease in water availability and an increase in temperature. This will have implications for agriculture and forestry and of potential income generation with every disaster recovery process.

The stakeholders that participated in the national engagement framework (Annex 2, Chapter 1) Section XI: Description of Target Areas (page 34) and Annex 5 identify the four contiguous districts of Ak-Talas (Naryn region), Chaldakai and Alay valleys (Osh region), Uzgen and Suzak valleys (Chuy region) and Osh (Osh region) as Project Area.

The climate of the Project Area is characterized by a continental climate with high precipitation in the mountains of the broad Fergana (Uzgen and Suzak) valley, the Chaldakai and Alay valleys and the adjacent mountain ranges to the north (Toguz Toru). Climate in the Project Area is heavily affected by topography and can be divided in four zones:

(i) The valley sub-mountain zone (from 900-1,200 m) with hot summers, snowless and temperate winters, and low precipitation.

(ii) The mountain zone (from 900-1,200 to 2,200-2,300 m) with a temperate climate, which has warm summers and cold, snowy winters.

(iii) The sub-alpine zone (from 2,000-2,200 to 3,500-4,500 m) is cooler in the summer and has relatively cold, snowless winters, with temperatures ranging from well below zero to +15 °C; and

(iv) The alpine belt zone (from 3,500 m and higher) with a polar climate and presence of numerous permanent glaciers.

Rainfall trends and projections in Project Area:

Data (1986-2014): gathered from national meteorological stations and remote sensing analysis show a tendency in rainfall reduction on the west side of the four Rayons, fully involving Uzgen and Suzak, with a

Addendum 2. List of definitions

The project applies the following definitions. Source of definition is provided in brackets.

- **Forest (FAO):** Land with tree crown cover of more than 50 percent, area covering more than 0.5 ha, with minimum height of the trees to be 5 m at maturity and in-situ conditions. The land may consist of a single species or mixed species forest.
- **Deforestation (FAO):** Deforestation is the long-term or permanent conversion of forest to other (non-forested) land use.
- **Afforestation (FAO):** Is the conversion from other land uses into forest, or the increase of the primary forest cover by more than 50 percent.
- **Reforestation (FAO):** Is the re-establishment of forest through planting and/or deliberate seeding on land that was previously forested.
- **Land Degradation (UNCCD):** Reduction or loss of the biological or economic productivity and complexity of rainfed cropland, irrigated cropland, or range, pasture, forest, and woodlands resulting from human activities. Land degradation includes soil erosion caused by wind and/or water; long-term loss of natural vegetation; and biological and ecological properties of soil.
- **Soil Erosion (UNCCD):** Long-term loss of natural vegetation.

Concerning the identified classes of degradation (extremely, moderately, non-degraded), the project uses the Land Productivity Dynamics (LPD) indicator (UNCCD) for both Forests and Rangelands (Annex 2, Feasibility Study; Chapter 3.1.4). The LPD indicator measures Vulnerability, Resistance and DRIN in Target Areas (TAs) (Annexes 6.a., Annex 6.b.) and Annex E.8., Atlas Report (Section 5.1.1 for TAs).

The degradation classes are based on LPD classification developed by the European Commission Joint Research Centre (JRC) and the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO). This corresponds to LPD "declining/decline" class. Moderately degraded combines the two LPD classes “Early signs of decline” and “Moderate decline”. The moderately degraded class is a transitional state, but it can be “increasing” or “decreasing”. The identified classes are a qualitative combined measure of the intensity and persistence of negative or positive trends together with changes of the photosynthetically active vegetation cover over time (e.g., increase or decrease in canopy density).

Addendum 3. Additional information related to risk factors and mitigation measures for Section G.2

### Risk Factor: 1

Component 1 puts a significant emphasis on the strengthening of the coordination between relevant government bodies and increased participation of local communities (Pastures Users Unions and other local member organizations) in the management of natural resources, including through joint action and common experiences. For example, in 2013, there was an attempt to build cooperation between the SAEPF (responsible for the implementation of the National Forest Policy) and the Pasture Department. The cooperation aimed to tackle the problem of pasture ecosystems management in an integrated manner. The management of natural resources is a complex issue that requires time to be addressed. In 2013, the FAO took three for five years; while this was an improvement, the initiative did not produce all expected results as it lacked follow-up support and implementation on the ground (details in Annexes 9, WP “Climate change and ecosystems”).

Moreover, in 2017, a group of national NGOs and international organizations prepared some amendments to The Law No. 38/2009 on Pastures and Forests. These amendments were submitted for discussion by Pasture Committees and Forest institutions. The draft was submitted to the Government Office for review but it needs to be followed up. At the time of Project design (2017-2018), this represented a substantial improvement over the previous situation where the NGOs and international organizations had their representatives to start harmonizing the discrepant regulations of State Forest Fund (which includes also land use and management) with the Law No. 38/2009 on Pastures and Forests, and to provide inputs through evidence based policy dialogue under Component 1.

Additionally, the Government Office for Regulatory Support programme, also implemented by FAO, the NDA in consultation with relevant stakeholders, including local communities, and other government institutions is raising awareness and conducting discussions at different levels on the role of the CGI Fund. The CGI Fund has been allocated a total amount of $20 million. This is the first readiness grant to ensure coordination and country ownership of CGI investments. Additionally, FAO has allocated $15 million for the CGI Fund in FY 2019-2020. These funds are expected to be implemented to prepare the ground for successful implementation of these large-scale investments.

Coordination amongst all the key stakeholders will be ensured through the Project Steering Committee, which includes representatives from the Central Finance Committee (CFC), ARB, the Kyrgyz Development Fund (KDF), the Ministry of Agriculture, Food Industries and MeatProcessing (MAFP), the Pasture Department (under MAFP), State Agency for Local Self Government and Interethnic Relations (SALSGER), the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources Protection (MENRP), the National Forestry Agency, and the FAO Representation in Kyrgyzstan. (cf. Annex 2 Chapter 5, Section A.1. Implementation principles). The Project Steering Committee will ensure that all stakeholders are involved in coordination and transversal sharing of information (already agreed during the national engagement process) (cf. Annex 5, ESMP). This is also a key element to ensure that the CGI Fund investments are aligned with efforts aimed to ensure participation of local communities and local institutions in the management of NRM. This approach has already been provided elsewhere by both Kyrgyzstan (cf. Annex 2, Feasibility Study; Chapter 3, Section II. Recent policy developments) and other countries such as Armenia and Lebanon where FAO is promoting such approach with positive results.

The Pasture Law of 2009 transformed the management of pastures from the State Land Fund (SLF) to the local governments, which in turn can further delegate management to the Pastures Users' Unions (PULU).

# ANNEXES

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 48 OF 81)

So far, all f/1 Ayi Okmotu (local government of the Apai Akmola) transitioned pasture management to established PULs (Annex 9: Working Paper "WP - NRM Policy and Governance"). The Pasture Law covers the following components: defining and implementing grazing rules, monitoring of pasture conditions, defining and collecting pasture fees, pasture-use-related conflict management, and managing funds collected from pasture use for pasture improvement and management.

The support provided under Component 1 will be in three areas: (i) strengthening community mobilization (Activity 1.3.1 under Component 1). The Project will also develop and promote implementation of harmonized regulatory frameworks across access to pasture areas (in SFLs and grazing areas on forest land under the Forest Law), as well as supporting the development of a Pasture Management Plan for Apai Akmola (Component 2 – all activities) (Annex 9: Working Paper "WP - NRM Policy and Governance" for detailed analysis, Annex 1 Chapter 7 on previous and ongoing projects on pasture law implementation).

## Risk Factor 2:

The problem of overstocking in Kyrgyzstan is significant, and (as noted in Annex 2, Feasibility Study, Chapter 1, Section VI. Sectoral economic performance (page 11)) the number of livestock has systematically increased since 2005, with a corresponding decrease in pasture productivity per animal for farmers to increase income. To this end, the Project takes this risk in high consideration, and has envisaged measures to address it under Component 3.

Regarding rules and regulations: in developing the INRMCPs under Component 1, the project will support the enforcement of existing regulations (Pasture Law, 2009, adopted by the Pasture Department of MAF [M]) for monitoring pasture conditions and defining grazing rules. The Project will also develop and specifying stocking rates in pasture management plans (details also in Annex 9: WP on Pasture Management Plans). This will be done through a participatory process with stakeholders (see Activity 1.3.2 of the PULs). Better planning of grazing in terms of spatial management, but also time and animal numbers will prevent overstocking.

Additional pastures are available also in State Forest Fund (under management of SAPEF). Under the aegis of the Project, the SAPEF will be encouraged to develop grazing land-use protocols and social mechanisms for sustainable use of these areas. This will be done through the project's Technical Committee system (cf. Annex 2, Chapter 4, Section Institutional aspects and implementation arrangements (page 133)).

Component 2 will address the problem of low productive livestock by promoting breed-improvements for cattle and small ruminants, improved feeding as well improved animal husbandry. Increased productivity per head will lead to increased income per household, which in turn will increase the overall level of household income.

Component 3 will provide significant alternatives to unproductive and unsustainable grazing and livestock production systems by promoting investments in climate sensitive value chains, which as ultimate effects will also contribute to income diversification and more sustainable grazing pressure in the Project Area. The stakeholders' consultation process (cf. Annex 5: ESMP; Section V: Consultation Processes) throughout the consultation process (cf. Annex 5: ESMP; Section V: Consultation Processes (Page 73- 75)) is designed to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the project design and design confirm that poor quality of pastures, danger of natural disasters such as landslides, absence of clean water, high number and low quality of livestock, overdependence on livestock production" represent major problems for the Project Area. The consultation process also confirmed that the Project Area provides support to the identification of an appropriate number of livestock on pastures, and support the transition from quantity to quality of livestock.

### Addendum 4: Description of monitoring parameters and M&E framework

The Project management Unit (PMU) will include an M&E unit, which will be in charge of the monitoring/measurement/collection of identified parameters during the eight years of execution. This will happen through a series of indicators and means of verification (MVs). The project management team will use the same procedure identified by the project. The M&E unit will be supported in this task by FAO technical assistance, which will provide training to the M&E Unit staff on the use of the tools and pass the hand over of knowledge and expertise and continuation after project completion (under Activity 12.1). Additionally, calculations will be verified at mid-term and termination by an independent team of evaluators. Details are provided in Annex 3.

The PMU will host a M&E Unit, which will conduct data collection and monitoring of Execution Performances, coordinates and supervises community monitoring and ground truthing, provide training and technical advice, and provides support to the project design and planning. M&E Unit will provide monitoring results of indicators for interim and terminal evaluations.

### Expected Results

#### A1.0 Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable communities and regions

GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL [PAGE AS OF:81]

FAO confirms that the default parameters will be monitored during the project and reported annually.

Georeferencing of activities and geospatial analysis (Earth Map, introduced in Chapter 6 of the Feasibility Study, and Forest Monitoring System (FMS) as described in Annex 5.2 of the FS, and presented in details in Annex 6.b of the submission package) will provide the project with data on investments' distribution and improvements against baseline (e.g., LPD, NDVI, Hansen Forest Cover Change). The results from the georeferencing activities and analysis of forest cover change will be presented on an annual basis and will be included in the Project Annual Report.

Georeferenced archive will also provide a robust framework for continuous monitoring (jointly with stakeholders) would allow identifying impacts and additions. The georeferenced archive will provide project partners with a tool to monitor progress, identify gaps and opportunities between the attributes identified by the baseline (qualitative analysis). This approach will allow the project and the FAO to follow up via ground-based surveys and remote sensing analysis the evolution of the default parameters.

The two main relevant institutions for long-term sustainability of carbon accounting and of other geospatial tools are the SAEFF (GCF NDA), the Pasture Department in the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Industry (MAFI). The project will work with these institutions to ensure that they start from the initial stage of the project unit full handover at the end of the project. The SAEFF, also covering the national forest monitoring system, is a key institution for long-term sustainability as it represents a significant institutional incentive to embed carbon accounting within its functions. FAO has already provided stakeholders with a tailored Russian version of both EX-ACT and GLEAM+ to ensure full ownership of these tools by the project partners. The SAEFF will be provided with continuous exposure and training to EX-ACT and training. Refreshers trainings are budgeted within Activity 12.1 through the provision of travel costs for the FAO representative to attend the annual national project implementation. Additionally in addition to the work done during the national engagement process, additional support is planned from the FAO representative to provide continuous exposure on GLEAM+, and a draft Russian version of the tool has been prepared specifically for this project.

Additional opportunity for long-term adoption of the relevant monitoring tools and techniques (including remote sensing) is provided by the signature in 2016 of the Kyrgyzstan National Spatial Infrastructure Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) between the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Industry (MAFI), signed among others by SAEFF and MAFI, aims at the standardization and sharing of national spatial data, including forest cover change monitoring. This MoU will provide a strong basis for long-term demand and planning. These signature institutions will be trained (as part of Activity 12.1) to use the appropriate remote sensing tools and technologies. The momentum generated by the signature of the memorandum will also allow for further engagement with other relevant stakeholders among national institutions and other development actors, such as the WB, GIZ, IFAD and FAO.

Deforestation/illegal loggings

According to different sources, including 2017 Collect Earth assessment within the framework of the FAO implemented project "National Forest Monitoring and Information Systems for a transparent and truthful" (RESIN) (https://www.resinclimate-initiative.com/en/deals/project3324), and the University of Maryland, the Hansen forest cover change data (Hansen et al., 2013) show that deforestation rate for Kyrgyzstan ranges from 0 to 0.38% at national level and 0.35% in target areas.

Each of the areas identified for forest investments is georeferenced and accurately analysed in the baseline (Baseline Atlas, Annex 6.b). The project will constantly monitor the state of forests in target


---

ANNEXES

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (PAGE 97 OF 81)

areas via dedicated remote sensing analysis and involvement of communities in the monitoring processes as described in Annex 2 Chapter 6 as well as in the georeferencing strategy working paper (Annex 3). The proposed procedure will allow for a more efficient and effective monitoring system to better quantify and follow up on project's impacts. The identified procedure will allow the project and FAO to constantly monitor activities against the baseline and to provide objective and tangible data for carbon calculation and reporting.

Given the nature and scope of this project and as part of the project's aim to support the country in creating the enabling environment for an innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning process, it is important that the project will be implemented in a way that maximizes its impact on target areas. Furthermore, the tools developed by FAO (e.g., Collect Earth, Earth Map, EX-ACT, GLEAM-) and practices (e.g., georeferencing, geospatial analysis and community monitoring) have already been introduced through the MEPPF and are now being scaled up in the project's implementation process as well as scaling up of this approach at the national level. Finally, local administrations and communities will be involved not only in the monitoring of forested areas but also in the using the tools and practices introduced by the projects.

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project «Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR)» in Kyrgyzstan as included in the funding proposal submitted by Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) to us on June 1, 2018.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the State Agency on Environment Protection and Forestry (SAEPF), the National Designated Authority of the Kyrgyz Republic.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-object to the project «Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR)» in Kyrgyzstan as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-object, it is implied that:

(a) The Government of the Kyrgyz Republic has no objection to the project «Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS FOR)» in Kyrgyzstan as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the Kyrgyz Republic's national policies, strategies and plans;
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project «Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP116

## Proposal name: Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands in Kyrgyzstan (CS-FOR)

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Country: Kyrgyzstan

Project size: Small

### I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| GCF grant financing will be used to mobilize further financing in forest and rangeland sectors creating opportunities for scaling up. | Coordination and ownership among the different partners will be critical for successful implementation and ensuring long-term sustainability. |
| An enabling environment for climate-smart green investments will be strengthened and capacity-building for relevant stakeholders provided. | |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out in the annexure, term sheet and addendum XVII, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

### II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

#### 2.1 Project background

Kyrgyzstan is a low-income food-deficit country. Livestock is the most important source of income for rural households, and the financial safety net for the rural poor. Cropping is limited due to a lack of irrigation infrastructure and worsening climatic conditions - increasing temperatures and reducing the amount and duration of rainfall. Climate change combined with land degradation has led to desertification, soil erosion, overgrazing by large groups of unproductive livestock, and to increasing forest degradation and unauthorized logging.

4. The objective of the proposal is to enhance carbon sequestration and climate resilience of ecosystems and livelihoods. The project will store 19.0 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) in forests over eight years through afforestation and reforestation, and 7,920,000 tCO2eq over the eight years of project implementation. The total expected reduction

5. Aside from the activities at national scale, the project sites are in selected rural municipalities represented by the four contiguous districts of Ak-Talaa, Toguz-Torn, Suzak and Irgen.

The project seeks a GCF grant of approximately USD 30 million, or 60 per cent of the total project cost. The project is classified as ESG category B. The project management unit costs USD 2.4 million (or 5 per cent of the project costs). This covers staff time, operational costs, and travels.

## Component-by-component analysis

### Component 1: Evidence-based strengthening of natural resource management governance (total cost: USD 5.86 GCF cost: USD 3.16 or 9.2 per cent)

This component aims to enable an enabling environment to support investments in forest-related activities at landscape scale. This will be achieved through the following:

- Harmonization of relevant procedures and regulations for “climate-change-sensitive” natural resource management, especially those related to land use planning, forest management, and climate change adaptation.
- Strengthening of governance structures by promoting community-based forest management and georeferenced monitoring and evaluation measures for natural resource and forest-rangeland ecosystems. This will be achieved through the following:
  - Promoting public-private partnerships to promote technically sound integrated natural resource management in Kyrgyzstan’s forest landscapes, including the development of a national forest landscape partnership.
  - Creating a national expert group for necessary consultations, providing technical support to pasture users unions and Lashkhoos (local forest institutions) to update and enhance the needed data to implement the project activities at landscape scale.

### Component 2: Strengthening forest ecosystems through the prevention of overgrazing and overharvesting of the forest/vegetative cover will be more effectively managed with the standardization of different legislation and full stakeholder inclusion. Through policy harmonization, stakeholders will be incentivized to adopt climate-resilient practices to incorporate climate risks in current management systems in forest-range-land ecosystems.

### Component 3: Community resilience for forest and range-land rehabilitation (total cost: USD 22.46; GCF cost: USD 10.38 or 31 per cent)

This component aims to contribute community resilience through capacity development, the adoption of climate-resilient practices, and the promotion of sustainable practices like rotational grazing, to enhance monitoring capacities, and to develop and implement a forest-range-land rehabilitation plan (see section 2.1). This is combined with investments to restore and improve forest-range-lands (output 2.2) at the project sites through key interventions: afforestation, reforestation, forest enrichment, pasture rehabilitation, and land use planning.

### Component 4: Sustainable solar value chain development (total cost: USD 81.01; GCF cost: USD 38.6 or 15.6 per cent)

This component aims to incentivize sustainable investments in carbon sequestration. Activities include market assessments, certifications, awareness campaigns on marketing

### Overall technical assessment

12. In summary, the project’s mitigation and adaptation goals are focused on integrated rangeland and forestry management guided by the prevention of natural resource degradation and enhancement of ecosystem services. The project will be supported in its implementation on communities and the private sector; promotion of bottom-up activities with local communities; and the development of a national framework for sustainable forest and rangeland management practices that sequester and preserve stored carbon. All of which will be supported by the creation of an enabling environment, baseline data sets, and capacity combined with the strengthening of key national and local institutions and planning processes, which overall will improve governance of forest and rangeland in Kyrgyzstan.

13. These goals are achieved through three components. Components 1 and 2 provide for the creation of an enabling environment, baseline data sets, and capacity combined with the strengthening of key national and local institutions and planning processes to ensure the scale and sustainability by promoting investments in the natural resources sector in Kyrgyzstan. The project’s paradigm shift could be adapted and tested in other districts and neighbouring countries.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

14. Under business as usual (substantial degradation) level, the level of forest degradation would generate an estimated net loss of 1.5 MtCO2eq in 20 years. The project will be able to avoid this by implementing a range of activities that will sequester and preserve stored carbon: 1.3 MtCO2eq from rangeland and livestock rehabilitation, 2.9 MtCO2eq from forest activities and 0.2 MtCO2eq from the promotion of alternative livelihoods.

15. The project will support the reduction of emissions and enhance carbon storage of 4.8 MtCO2eq through: (i) the creation of legal and enabling environments supported by an innovative financing mechanism; (ii) the development of a national framework for sustainable forest management, allocation of land use rights, and the promotion of alternative livelihoods to avoid further degradation; (iii) community-based investments in natural forest regeneration, sustainable forest management, allocation of land use rights, and the promotion of alternative livelihoods to avoid further degradation; (iv) the diversification of options for community livelihoods (i.e. income generation); and (v) the reduction of emission intensity per unit of animal protein (meat and milk).

16. Sequestration of 19.8 MtCO2eq in 20 years represents 7.6 per cent of the country’s total emissions, or 22 per cent of the agricultural sector’s emissions. The project’s cost is USD 25.5.

17. Average annual temperatures have increased during the period 1960–2013 by 0.8°C (0.025 °C/decade), with a higher rate of increase in the period 1990-2013 (0.07°C/year) with the greatest rate of warming occurring during the winter months. Annual precipitation has shown a slight decrease over this time, but there is evidence that climate change is causing more frequent changes in temperature and rainfall affecting ecosystems and ecosystem services, and thus community livelihoods, which depend on natural resources for their living.

## The climate resilience of 432,450 individuals (190,000 households), including 246,877 women in Lethbridge, national parks, local self-government bodies, community organizations, the private sector in the high-risk area will be improved, representing 9.8 per cent of the country's population.

### As part of a resilience impact measurement analysis methodology, developed by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO), the resilience capacity index has been calculated for each household. The results show that the main drivers of adaptive capacity are the diversification of income portfolios and the main variables of adaptive capacity. This finding confirms the importance of diversified livelihoods in enhancing resilience to climate change, increasing the resilience of beneficiaries by reducing their dependency on direct uses of natural resources, by improving ecosystem functions, and by diversifying livelihoods and income sources.

### An estimated 300,000 ha of dry, high-altitude deforested or severely degraded forest lands will be reforested. This includes 152,648 ha of degraded forests in the project area and 64,000 ha of rangelands will be under improved management. An additional 20 per cent of producers will use carbon sequestration techniques and practices.

### Analysis of ecosystem service dynamics via the FAO Earth Map will allow the assessment of project's positive impacts on ecosystems.

## Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: High

The project aims to contribute to shifting national agricultural production from a predominantly unsustainable subsistence livestock production impacting on the carbon storage of forests and rangelands to a diversified and climate-sensitive value chain business-oriented ecosystem-based approach. This will lead to improved livelihoods, food security, and community benefits by providing ecosystem services (e.g., improved climate regulation, flood regulation, water purification).

The major constraint on the increase of carbon sequestration in forests and rangelands has been the traditional extensive grazing practice and expansion of pasture areas. The project aims to contribute to shifting national agricultural production from a predominantly unsustainable subsistence livestock production impacting on the carbon storage of forests and rangelands to a diversified and climate-sensitive value chain business-oriented ecosystem-based approach.

As diversification of income sources is demonstrated as the main driver of adaptive capacity, the project will reinforce policies, legal frameworks and strategies enabling diversified livelihoods. The project will also contribute to improving governance structures by reinforcing among these core planning frameworks and governance structures. The more conducive policy environment will be a key factor in facilitating replication and scale up of future investments.

Integrated natural resources climate resilient management plans will be developed with local producers, community-based organizations and the private sector. Improved governance is important for private sector investments into afforestation and reforestation.

The project will also contribute to improving knowledge generation, dissemination, and sharing through workshops, training sessions, awareness campaigns, media outreach, and knowledge-sharing events will also strengthen knowledge generation and dissemination of good practices throughout the country, including young people. Moreover, the project will ensure that the best practices are taken into account in future decision-making by the communities, public institutions and private sector. A dedicated monitoring and knowledge management protocol has been designed for the project implementation team.


---
**Page 113**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential

* Scale: High

Community mobilization and training sessions will be undertaken in 50 municipalities and will include social mobilization, gender training, institutional support, and the establishment of a community-based forest management system. The project will take part in the selection of the project-supported value chains and in the capacity-building activities. Access to credit facilities for small-scale farmers and other stakeholders will be provided to ensure equitable distribution of benefits. The investments under the project will generate up to 3,000 new jobs.

In addition to contributing to carbon sequestration, afforestation/reforestation and forest-enrichment activities will improve soil fertility and stability, reduce land degradation, improve water quality and quantity, and increase biodiversity in degraded areas. This is together with pasture rotation. The expected carbon sequestration will increase the availability of denitrifying bacteria and thus enhance nitrogen fixation by leguminous plants and grasses. Moreover, climate change mitigation and biodiversity issues will be harmonized across strategies and legislation through the planned policy dialogue.

In terms of main outputs, there will be a 10 per cent rise of livestock production by at least 10 per cent (under the assumption of an increasing share of cows and a decreasing livestock mortality rate). The expected economic benefits are estimated to range from the selected value chains (internal rates of return between 22 and 39 per cent) and net present value benefit estimates ranging from US$6,000 to $12,400 per ha), but the highest benefits are when economic benefits are accounted for in ecosystem services. Also, forest users will benefit from collecting non-timber forest products.

### Needs of the recipient

* Scale: High

Most of the population lives in the valleys and foothills of the mountains, where vulnerability to climate events is particularly high and where around 50-80 per cent of agricultural land is affected by droughts. The most common causes are linked with climate-related shocks, including floods and mudslides. Thirteen per cent of children suffer from malnutrition, while 46 per cent of children and women suffer from anemia due to micronutrient deficiency.

Few investments are in the forest sector. Biodiversity management has received more attention than other aspects such as sustainable use of forests and increasing carbon sequestration, which makes a significant contribution to reducing GHG emissions in the country, as explained in the country's nationally determined contribution.

### Country ownership

* Scale: High

The Government of Kyrgyzstan includes ecosystem-based adaptation in its "Climate change mitigation and adaptation strategy for 2017-2035" (hereinafter referred to as the forestry sector". Mitigation and adaptation actions in the sector are included in the nationally determined contribution. FAO will contribute to the national adaptation planning process by

Stakeholder consultations with various stakeholders at both national and local levels have been conducted during project formulation. Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP116

Proposal name: Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS-FOR) in the Kyrgyz Republic

Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

Project/programme size: Small


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential Scale: High

#### 1.1 General background

Kyrgyzstan has been classified as a low-income, food-deficit country. Two-thirds of the country’s population live in rural areas of the country. Agriculture is the main source of livelihood, with livestock being the leading source of income and primary sources of food; it also provides employment for 70% of the population. Crop production has been threatened in the country due to a combination of inadequate irrigation infrastructure and the negative impact of climate change on crop yields. The independent Technical Advisory Panel is evaluating this proposal and some of the areas, poor management of resources and negative climate change impacts are highlighted as key challenges. These include: desertification, soil erosion, overgrazing, increasing forest degradation and unsustainable harvesting of timber from mountain slopes.

The interactions between changing weather patterns, the impact of these changes on communities’ livelihoods and the vulnerability of rural populations to extreme climatic events and human activities has led to an increase in damage from extreme weather events and a growing threat to the lives and livelihoods of people living in rural areas. The Government recognizes the need to take action to halt this trend, as is evident from the key interventions for investment identified by the country’s national climate change strategy and its country’s intended nationally determined contribution (INDC). The interventions will commit the country to reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing carbon sequestration through investments in international assistance. The proposed project has been designed to assist the country in meeting these commitments. The sequencing objective, which is well entrenched in the proposed project, is to ensure that the project components are sequenced for climate change and disaster risk reduction and is expected to be achieved through the following project components:

(a) Component 1: evidence-based strengthening of natural resources management (NRM) governance. This component will support the Government’s ongoing efforts to harmonize NRM policies with international best practices, including policy development, monitoring and evaluation systems.

(b) Component 2: integrated forest and rangeland rehabilitation. This component will introduce a process of local integrated rangeland and forestry resource planning built around forest, rangeland and livelihood management practices. This will subsequently lead to the creation of sustainable land use systems that are adapted to the climate and enable the diversification of household income earning activities; and

(c) Component 3: climate-sensitive value chain development. This will facilitate market opportunities that provide the financial incentives, funded by the Russian Kyrgyz


---
**Page 123**
---

Development Fund (DF), for resources users to adopt and maintain management practices that conserve and preserve mixed grasslands.

2. The targeted project areas were selected due to their high level of exposure and sensitivity to climate change stressors, mitigation potential, human poverty and dependency on natural resources.

3. The total fund proposed to implement the project is USD 49.99 million. This total investment is expected to be provided as follows:

- Grant from GCF - USD 29.99 million;
- Subsidy from the government - USD 15.00 million;
- In-kind contribution from Ministry of Agriculture, Food Industry and Melioration (MAFM) - USD 8.317 million;
- In-kind contribution from State Agency for Environment Protection and Forestry (GAFZ) - USD 2.496 million;
- In-kind contribution from Community Development and Investment Agency (ARIS) - USD 0.542 million;
- In-kind contribution from the beneficiaries - USD 3.602 million; and
- In-kind contribution from the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) - USD 0.600 million.

### Climate change element

Both the climate change mitigation and adaptation needs of the country are adequately justified by the projected increase in forest cover, reducing GHG emissions in all countries is a very important contribution to the contribution to fulfilling the goals of the Paris Agreement Kyrgyzstan has committed itself to reduce its greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from 35.76 per cent by 2018 through international assistance. Although this document did not produce clear strategies to achieve the envisaged GHG emission reductions, it emphasized that the country should focus on developing and implementing relevant sectoral targets in the country to secure both adaptation and mitigation targets. In order to structure the project proposal for the implementation of the national climate change management of Kyrgyzstan, began the process of developing a proposal for the project "Carbon Sequestration through Forest Plantations and Sustainable Management of Forest Resources". Through this project, FAO ensured that the climate change mitigation needs of the country were adequately addressed. The project was developed in accordance with the national climate change and climate rationale developed earlier for the country. Many of the inputs came from documents prepared by the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), given that the inputs for the design of this project were taken from the relevant sector planning documents, including those prepared as part of the national climate change mitigation strategy. The project was designed to contribute to the achievement of the objective set out in the UNFCCC Kyoto Protocol. This conclusion is also supported by the report included as annex 9 of the funding proposal, which is titled "Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation Strategy for Kyrgyzstan". In this document it was stated that the goal of the project is to contribute to the development of a low carbon emission economy in Kyrgyzstan's economy. The project objectives, which were clearly elucidated in this report, include:

- To intervene in key hot spots of target areas with adapted forest and pasture inventories;
- To clearly transform management of pasture and forest resources at the national and local levels to ecosystem-based sustainable NRM by enhancing an integrated and

## Key expected targets and coverage of this project intervention include carbon sequestration through investment in rangeland and forest management.

The achievement of the carbon targets is expected to have multiple co-benefits for the country, including adaptation to climate change and disaster risk reduction by supporting the Government's ongoing efforts to harmonize land use planning with climate change adaptation strategies. The project intervention will also contribute to improved land and water management systems. Carbon sequestration will be achieved through the introduction of this project intervention of a process involving local integrated rangeland and forest resource planning and monitoring system.

### Project Intervention

The project intervention is designed to be responsive to observed changes in the climate and is expected to diversify livelihoods, improve food security, and reduce poverty. The project intervention will also engender market opportunities that will provide financial incentives for resource users to adopt and maintain sustainable land management practices. Financial incentives for rangeland and forest conservation activities will be funded by a co-financing senior loan from KBFDF. Achievement of the project targets is expected to be measured against the following key performance measurement framework indicators: impact potential, country ownership, and needs of the recipients/beneficiaries.

### Alignment with Other Metrics

The alignment with other metrics, including paradigm shift, sustainability, and cost-effectiveness, will be assessed in this document. The assessment will be based on the extent to which the project intervention is aligned with the following criteria: (a) creation of legal and management enabling environments supported by an innovative evidence-based climate and natural resource planning and monitoring system; (b) community-based investments in natural forest regeneration, sustainable forest management, and agroforestry; (c) rehabilitation of rangelands and prevention of further degradation; (d) diversification of options for community livelihoods; and (e) reduction of emission intensity per unit of animal protein.

### Carbon Sequestration

The carbon emissions reductions that will be delivered by the sequestration activities listed above are estimated to be 19.0 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent over a period of 25 years, as presented in annex X(a) to the funding proposal titled "CSF-FOE: Carbon Accounting." It is also estimated that this intervention will have direct and indirect beneficiaries.

### Technical Advisory Panel

The approach used for this estimation, which is based on the MRV methodology, is described in detail in annex X(b). The number of direct and indirect beneficiaries was determined on the basis of the estimation of direct and indirect beneficiaries. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) therefore concludes that the number of beneficiaries as a one indicator of impacts is not sufficient to determine the scale of impact.

### Climate Change Vulnerability

With regard to climate change vulnerability, the project design actually focused on a carefully selected set of districts in the country, which are described in detail and in annex I(a) of the feasibility study. Analysis of the study area, consisting of four districts of the country, related to the extent and status of rangeland and forest resources as well as the identified climate change vulnerability factors is presented in annex X(b) to the funding proposal titled "Project Atlas." The four target districts are said to be the most vulnerable areas for climate change impacts. They will also benefit from green investment interventions for forest and rangeland rehabilitation and livestock management
