## Time frame of results

9. The critical results expected to be delivered by this project will include: carbon sequestration from improved forest and rangeland management; livelihoods of people in forest production systems; and increased resilience of forests and rangelands. Capacity strengthening is also expected to be delivered by component 1 of this project, which will strengthen the institutional capacity of the Kyrgyzstan Forest Service through policy and legal frameworks as well as strengthening its planning and evaluation systems.

All these critical results will be achieved through different levels of impacts during the period of project implementation. The most immediate impact is expected from the capacity strengthening activities to start manifesting during the early stages of the intervention, carbon sequestration and livelihoods improvements are expected to start manifesting in 2018 at the time of implementation due to the longer time needed for forested vegetation growth to occur.

### Potential mitigation/adaptation impacts and degree of lock-in infrastructure

10. This project intervention covers both mitigation and adaptation responses to climate change in Kyrgyzstan, which are consistent with the national priorities as articulated in the INDC of Kyrgyzstan that was submitted to the UNFCCC secretariat in 2015. The country's INDC emphasized the importance of mitigation and adaptation as priority areas for REDD+ activities in Kyrgyzstan, including forest management, land use planning, the environment, national economy, and achieving wider Sustainable Development Goals. National action plans have been developed to address climate change impacts on various sectors of the country’s economy, including: emergency situations; biodiversity and forestry; water resources; agriculture; energy; transport; urban development; and other national responses to international policy, including the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification (UNCCD) and the Kyoto Protocol.

The national action plan and its activity frameworks for implementing the UNCCD in the Kyrgyzstan for 2015–2020 have many highly relevant actions on land degradation that are directly linked to climate change, including: forest management; soil conservation; and sustainable use of land resources. The national action plan highlights strengthening the capacity for state forest services and other institutions responsible for managing forests and other natural resources of Kyrgyzstan. It states that a principal condition is to maintain the ecological functions of forests and rangelands as well as their productive functions of soil. While most of these policy documents stress adaptation intervention, mitigation activities are also included in some of them. For example, the document “Another national document, the ‘Third National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan’, a key emphasis of this project. Thus the information provided in the funding proposal and the feasibility study utilized for this project is consistent with these policy documents. This provides further evidence that the interventions foreseen by this project are the most feasible and suitable mitigation and adaptation responses.

11. The project is expected to yield an average carbon emission reduction through carbon sequestration of about 987,560 tCO2eq, which translates to about 14,371,354 tCO2eq over the 20-year period of implementation.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

### 1.2.3 Comprehensiveness

14. In the absence of this project intervention, unsustainable grazing will continue, causing expansion of pasture areas and, consequently by the continued use of traditional extensive grazing practices, degradation of the rangeland ecosystem. This has been identified as a major constraint to the increase of carbon sequestration and storage in forests and rangelands. The current situation is estimated to be affecting approximately 10 million Kenyan people, and it is not surprising that over the years there has been an increase in the country’s livestock population from about 25 million heads in 1986 to more than 37 million heads today.

15. This project intervention will address this problem through the introduction of an integrated, locally determined, ecosystem management approach for rangeland and forest resources that will enhance maintenance of existing forests and increase tree cover while enhancing the livelihoods of beneficiaries at the same time. The measures that will be delivered by this project will enhance maintenance, increase forest cover, and improve the ecological status of rangelands in the target areas. This will contribute to the achievement of national carbon sequestration objectives and result in a more diversified and profitable agriculture for beneficiaries.

16. The harmonization of national policies and strategies relating to forest and grassland management, which will be undertaken in component 1 of the proposed project, will deliver an enabling environment that will facilitate the implementation of this project intervention and also create a conducive policy and institutional environment that will greatly enhance the replicability of the project.

17. The project design process will bring together key governmental agencies and coordination bodies and will ensure identification of clear roles by the stakeholders themselves. This process will also provide an opportunity to identify lessons learned, provide a sound basis for expanded use of the new approach to local resource management, and lead to greater community involvement in forest and grassland management.

18. A key component of the intervention that involves capacity-building for the implementing entity (II) is the incorporation of project activities into the regular annual workplan of the II. This will normalize these activities as part of the implementing agency’s


---
**Page 127**
---

vision and approach to resource management nationwide, and thus establish a solid operational capacity that will be invaluable when the interventions are replicated in the other regions of the country.

19. Given the fact that the assessment carried out to identify target areas for this project was nationwide, it is expected that the lessons learned from the intervention will be replicable elsewhere.
20. The IE thus has a rich database of sites where the resource management/carbon sequestration approaches delivered by this project can be replicated.

The project will also benefit other beneficiaries who will engage in key value chains, specifically those for nuts and dried fruits as an agricultural business. The fact that the project will provide new markets to these beneficiaries will also enhance their interest in opening up new markets following the intervention.
21. It can therefore be concluded that the objectives of the project are well aligned and respond to the needs of the key value chains articulated in the funding proposal.

### Potential for knowledge and learning

22. Knowledge management and capacity building for key stakeholders represent innovative solutions that will be delivered by the proposed project. Project stakeholders will not only gain new capacities and knowledge about the different interventions, but they will also gain tools to apply these innovations into their daily activities, including decision-making processes and livelihood strategies. The project will therefore deliver a strong platform for knowledge sharing and learning.

23. The interventions are innovative for Kyrgyzstan, especially when the national efforts to mainstream climate change into the project’s key intervention is considered. The project will deliver an enabling environment that will allow Kyrgyzstan to integrate climate change considerations into their normal activities, development of market mechanisms for their natural resources, and other related policies. As such, it can be concluded that the type of investment proposed will support innovative interventions in Kyrgyzstan.

### Enabling environment/sustainability of outcomes

24. The plan described in the funding proposal is well structured, covering identification of the regions and sites where the chosen interventions will be carried out; development of an enabling environment for the governance and management of the selected interventions; and finally, a financing strategy that will ensure sustainability of these interventions after the outcome. The feasibility study (annex 2 of the funding proposal) also describes these features of the project in detail.

The proposed financing mechanism, which is based on co-financing in the form of a senior loan, will not only provide funds for the private sector and other beneficiaries but will also ensure that the enabling environment will be enhanced, but it can also offer a very robust financial exit strategy.

25. As presented in the funding proposal and its annexes, the project is planned to bring about very significant improvements in governance, conservation, and management of forests. Component 1 has been designed to strengthen the Government’s ongoing efforts to harmonize relevant policies and regulations with international best practices for forest resource management systems. This strategy is based on the awareness that a key measure in preserving the natural resources is to ensure their sustainable use through improved governance and better tools related to their management. Improved management will prevent livestock overgrazing, overharvesting of firewood and timber, will create an enabling environment that stimulates innovative practices and investments, and conserve and regenerate these resources.


---
**Page 128**
---

28. It has been recognized that to achieve these goals there will need to be a change in the outlook of institutions and management and the regulation of forests and rangelands. The funding proposal explained that the intervention will create a conducive enabling environment at existing institutions by supporting and harmonizing the regulatory framework for proper management of forests and rangelands. This will enable the capacity of forest and rangeland managing institutions to be repositioned to utilize modern NRM principles, but the capacity of their staff to undertake these activities is still weak. The project design therefore intends to build the institutional capacity and capability to monitor and regulate the use of forest and rangeland resources. The project design is therefore intended to address the fragmented nature of existing policy and regulatory frameworks.

29. The project design will also aim to bring about behavioral changes within community groups and at the individual level through training and capacity-building activities. Some of these activities are expected to be implemented in collaboration with relevant stakeholders, which may include a gender and social development specialist, and the terms of reference for each member of the expert group will be defined by the project team. The composition of each expert group will be built and strengthened by the project will include: preparing communication materials and organizing information awareness campaigns to mobilize national stakeholders; organizing training sessions and workshops on forest and rangeland tenure issues, land use planning, sustainable management practices, and developing recommendations for enforcement of sustainable management use and of forest and rangeland resources; facilitating stakeholder engagement in the development of the national stakeholder involvement process; organizing a national stakeholders’ platform to facilitate dialogue between government agencies, civil society organizations, and facilitating thematic workshops, and submitting the recommendation document to parliament.

30. The core outputs of monitoring groups at the central level of the State Agency for Environmental Protection and Forestry (SAPEF) and ARIS (the executing entities (EEs)) for this project will be the development of a capacity-building manual and training materials. Key outputs of the capacity-building activities included in component 1 of this project will include enhanced capacities on climate change risks and natural resource assessment, land use planning, forest and rangeland tenure issues, land use planning, monitoring and evaluation tools and methodologies; facilitating linkages across evidence-based approaches to policy development and implementation, ecosystem planning processes; and using a geo-referencing and evidence-based approach.

30. The TEAP can therefore conclude that behavioral changes in institutions, communities, and individuals were fully considered and transparently presented in the funding proposal and its annexes.


12.4 Replicability and scalability/knowledge and learning

The harmonization of national policies and strategies, which will be carried out as part of component 1 of the project, will not only benefit sound project delivery in the target

areas but will also deliver a more robust policy and strategy framework at a national level which will create a much more conducive policy environment for continued scaling leading to more effective replication and scalability of the successful project activities and strategies. This will greatly improve the efficiency with which expanded actions can be undertaken.

(b) A key achievement of the project implementation strategy for CS-FOR is to bring together all relevant stakeholders in Kyrgyzstan, including those involved in the management of forests, rangelands and animal husbandry in Kyrgyzstan. This achievement will enable clear delineation of the roles of agencies in the ecosystem management process, which will not only improve the efficiency with which actions are undertaken but also have a positive impact on its replication and scalability in the future in other regions. This will provide a strong foundation for the development of an effective institutional approach to local resource management leading to greater carbon sequestration;

There is ample evidence in the description of the project design, given in the funding proposal and implementation strategy document, that the project team has taken into account the outcomes but also on their comprehensive documentation using various tools, including workshops, training sessions, etc. This will provide a clear framework for knowledge that can be shared by others within and outside Kyrgyzstan who may want to replicate the idea.

Through trainings, workshops and other knowledge-sharing activities built into the project design and delivery mechanism, and the monitoring, reporting and verification protocols, the project team has ensured that the project implementation strategy provides a clear framework to share knowledge and contribute to replication and/or scaling up.

The project design and implementation strategies as presented in the funding proposal and corresponding implementation strategy document, including the involvement of government agencies and especially its policy and regulatory framework will participate in the delivery of the project. The fact that the project team has taken into account the country’s policies and settings for forestry, rangeland and livestock management, will serve as the leading implementation strategy to ensure that the project is fully integrated with national efforts at mobilization of other relevant stakeholders will ensure that the climate change adaptation and mitigation measures to be delivered by this project will be mainstreamed into policies, laws, sectoral plans and strategies in Kyrgyzstan.

Given the discussions above, the ITAP is of the opinion that the CS-FOR project as presented in the funding proposal and implementation strategy document has a high potential to deliver a paradigm shift in the way forests, rangelands and livestock are managed in Kyrgyzstan.

### Economic/social co-benefits

#### Key economic or social benefits that will be delivered by this project can be summarized as follows:

- The income of rural dwellers in the regions covered by this project is expected to increase through the investment provided.
- Specific financial benefits will come from better herd management, contributing to an expected rise of livestock production.
- Agricultural and forest users will benefit from improved access to markets for their products, including new facilities that will participate under project funding related to ecosystem services that include tree fruits/nut plantations, supplying fruits and/or nuts and other commercial crops covering 10% of the total area in the project regions.

- Women and youth engagement in business opportunities in the project-supported value chain will be promoted through business-related capacity-building activities.
- High priority will be given to women’s participation in the value chain, especially when selecting the value chain; and
- The project will also provide a platform for women to access improved natural resources (forests and rangelands) more directly, thus ensuring equitable distribution of benefits.

## Needs of the recipient Scale: High

### Economic and social needs and climate vulnerability

According to information presented in the funding proposal and its annexes and from open literature sources, it is clear that the geography and hydrography of Kyrgyzstan render it highly susceptible to hazards and disasters, which are becoming more frequent due to climate change. These include landslides, avalanches, snow avalanches, floods, droughts, strong winds, downpours, ice and frost, breakouts of glacial lakes, floods, river erosion, and earthquakes. The most vulnerable areas in Kyrgyzstan are the mountainous regions, especially the foothills of the mountains, where vulnerability to these events is particularly high and losses are extremely high.

### Financial needs

Kyrgyzstan is a low-income food deficit country with a population of nearly six million, of which two-thirds live in rural areas. Agriculture is one of the key economic sectors in Kyrgyzstan, contributing 15 per cent of gross domestic product. As a result, the country does not have the national financial resources to implement projects on its own and relies heavily on external funding for project from its own revenues due to the limited nature of national and local resources.

The kinds of interventions required cannot attract funding from the very weak domestic private sector in Kyrgyzstan because the interest rates charged by banks are high, while returns are low and the payback period is indefinite for such investment in many cases, especially where there is a lack of infrastructure. The GCF will provide grants to help Kyrgyzstan overcome these barriers. The grant funding from GCF will also strengthen the funds from KFDF, a private sector entity that will provide its contributions as wholesale credits to local


---
**Page 132**
---

### Institutional needs

#### 1.4.3.2. Institutional needs

45. According to information presented in the funding proposal, institutions in Kyrgyzstan involved with management, regulation and policy setting for forests, rangelands and livestock lack the capacity to effectively implement ecosystem-based adaptation interventions. This is due to a lack of institutional coordination and fragmented governance partnerships in integrated natural resources management. Consequently, the capacities of institutions involved in forest and rangeland management are not sufficient to deliver an intervention. For government institutions, areas that will be strengthened will include: policy and strategic planning; resource mobilization and budgeting; project design and implementation; monitoring, reporting and verification capabilities for ecosystem-based interventions; low-carbon production systems in forestry and agriculture, including biocentric sites, of the Kyrgyzstan Forest Service; and capacity building. The proposed project will also aim to build on ecosystem-based entrepreneurial activities, especially those that will deliver carbon sequestration services and climate resilient livelihoods, as the financing of such activities.

46. The IEP ranked the potential for this proposed project intervention to meet the needs of the recipients to be high.

The participation level of non-national actors in the delivery of this project will come from FAO and RKDF; FAO, as the overall coordinator of the project and an AE at GEF, will ensure that the resources allocated for the project are used effectively. ### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Score: High**

#### 1.6.1 Financial structure

63. Of the total funds needed to implement this project (USD 49-99 million), USD 29-99 million has been requested from GCF as grant funding. This funding request for GCF has been justified in the funding proposal according to the following reasons:

(a) Grant funding requested is justified given the substantial expected impact in contributing towards the achievement of the Sustainable Land Management and rangeland investment goals.

(b) In the absence of GCF funding, joint investment in rangeland rehabilitation such as planned in this project will result in limited or negative financial return of investment in forests. This will be due to the high cost of funds from other sources, when and if they can be mobilized, and the associated transaction costs and opportunity costs, often associated with mobilizing different stakeholders for joint investments in rangeland rehabilitation.

64. The project as conceptualized and presented in the funding proposal and its relevant annexes does not entail the risk of crowding-out private and other public investments. Success will instead be contingent on the assumption that a conducive environment exists and should catalyse further private sector investments.

#### 1.6.2 Co-financing/leveraging

According to the information included in the funding proposal, with a contribution of USD 29-99 million from GCF as grant funding, the total project cost is USD 49-99 million. The financing will total USD 20 million, of which: 77 per cent will be from RISDP; 10 per cent from beneficiaries; and about 2 per cent each from SAEFP, MAfSM and ARS. MAfSM will also contribute about 400,000 USD in kind.

Based on this information, it can be concluded that the project will leverage an adequate level of co-financing as determined by the GCF Co-Financing Policy.

#### 1.6.3 Financial viability/best practices

As stated in paragraph 64 above, the success of this project will provide a conducive environment in Kyrgyzstan that will catalyse further investment, particularly from the private sector, to achieve the desired results in future.


---
**Page 137**
---

Analysis(*) provided the following metrics for the expected financial performance of the project for a 20-year analysis:

- Estimated internal rate of return = 71.3 per cent;
- Estimated net present value (E-NPV) = USD 533.7 million.

Robust sensitivity analyses covering parameters such as increased cost of the project due to inflation, exchange rate fluctuations and other unforeseen events were carried out. The result of the analysis showed that variations of these parameters between 20 and 30 per cent will still yield acceptable results with the highest decrease in the estimated net present value being about USD 15 million (a decrease of approximately 3 per cent) while knowledge impacts decrease by about 30 per cent. Even for this scenario, the estimated net present value will be USD 498.7 million and the knowledge impact will still be above 60 per cent.

From the review of the plans reported in the funding proposal and its annexes, it can be concluded that the project incorporated best practices and available technologies in its design. Multiple options for implementation were considered and reviewed before finalisation of the design, which has now been presented for implementation.

Based on the above analysis and assessment, the ITAP concludes that the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed CS-FOR project are "high".

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP therefore recommends that the Board approve this funding proposal.

# Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS-FOR) in Kyrgyzstan

### Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

1. A Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) was established to implement the gender strategy and to ensure that women's needs are addressed (e.g., single-headed households and youth). Below table presents specific actions in each project component with indicative budget as envisaged at the project level. The total budget for the project is 10,500,000 USD. The majority of this work and budget at the country and regional levels is allocated to women-specific targeted interventions.* Feminine gender roles are being addressed through training and capacity development activities with female groups to be targeted in the project activities. All trainings will have post-training evaluation questionnaires to track the impact of the training on the beneficiaries. The majority of the technical and professional skills being adopted that were acquired in trainings, how knowledge and equipment was used by women to improve their livelihoods.

2. The budget for the Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan (GAP) was calculated according to the underlying principles:

   a. For activities related to capacity development / training / awareness campaigns: 30% of the total budget is considered gender sensitive, considering the estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.
   
   b. For activities involving women in decision-making frameworks: 50% of the budget is considered gender sensitive. This is due to the estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.

   c. For activities involving investment in rangeland and forestry (comp 2 - excluding training): 10% of the budget is considered gender sensitive, considering the estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.
   
   d. For activities involving women's access to productive value chains (comp 3 - excluding training): 10% of the budget is considered gender sensitive, considering the estimates on beneficiaries of project interventions.

3. On the basis of the final GAP, the Gender and Social Development Specialist each year prepares an annual work plan and budget of GAP for submission to the Project Director.

4. The GAP is in line with the overall project implementation plan and timeline, thus all activities are incorporated into the project's work plan.

5. Capacity development activities will be carried out not only by providing trainings for beneficiaries (both women and men) but also ensuring access of women and female headed households to decision-making processes at different levels. This is done through capacity building in gender equality, stakeholder collaboration with and promotion of gender sensitization among the local government and core district-level institutions. The project will ensure that women's needs are addressed when participating in land use planning (e.g., water, pastures, and other resources). The project will ensure a specific approach for women when participating in land use planning (e.g., water, pastures, and other resources) through the VCDs, supporting the latter one VCD. Through these activities it is anticipated to work on sustainability of the project interventions.

# Carbon Sequestration through Climate Investment in Forests and Rangelands (CS-FOR) in Kyrgyzstan

## Gender Assessment

### Overview of gender inequalities in rural areas of Kyrgyzstan

1. FAO conducted a gender assessment in 2016, titled 'National gender profile of agricultural and rural livelihoods: Kyrgyz Republic.' The assessment has informed the formulation of this project, including this section.
2. Prior to its independence in 1991, about three-quarters of women of Kyrgyzstan were in the official labour force, including those who worked in the state and collective farms. During the period of transition to the market economy, women's participation in the labour force declined by half. The decline was steeper for rural women than urban ones. The overall female employment rate for women decreased by half. The period also saw weakening and disappearance of public services such as water supply, sanitation, electricity, roads, schools, health facilities, kindergartens, extended maternity leave, and access to basic healthcare. The loss of such services meant that women had less time available for paid work.
3. The Kyrgyz Republic ranked 90 out of 159 countries on Gender Inequality Index (GII). GII reflects gender-based inequalities in three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment, and economic activity. The Kyrgyz Republic has the highest maternal mortality rate among the selected ECA countries. With a low fertility rate, the country's high GII is due to its low female labour force participation and low education attainment; it lags behind the two countries on maternal mortality ratio and adolescent birth rate (see Table 3).

| Country | GII value | Maternal mortality ratio | Adolescent birth rate (%) | Population with no access to safe drinking water (%) | Labour force participation of women |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kyrgyzstan | 0.384 | 95 | 16.2 | 10.0 | 49.4 |
| Tajikistan | 0.377 | 100 | 19.2 | 10.0 | 47.1 |
| Uzbekistan | 0.287 | 57 | 17.6 | 10.4 | 59.1 |
| ECA average | 0.322 | 26.4 | 16.4 | 10.0 | 56.7 |

Source: Human Development Report 2016, UNDP

### Kyrgyzstan's legal framework guarantees women’s equality in its constitution and family codes guarantee equal rights for men and women. In 2008 the Law on State Guarantees of Equal Rights and Opportunities was adopted to ensure that all public institutions are free from gender discrimination. Civil, penal, labour and family codes guarantee equal rights for men and women. In 2006 the Law on State Guarantees of Equal Rights and Opportunities was adopted to ensure that all public institutions are free from gender discrimination. The Government has also addressed gender equality in key policy measures such as the National Action Plan on Gender Equality 2015-2017, which is a follow-up to the 'Gender Equality Strategy to 2030.' The strategy was translated into the National Action Plan on Gender Equality for 2015–2017, which was approved in December 2015 and the law as of 2018.


---
**Page 150**
---

# CS-FOR Gender Assessment

That is responsible for the gender mainstreaming and monitoring the implementation of the National Action Plan on Gender Equality (GE MAP). The gender strategy and action plan highlight: (i) women's economic empowerment; (ii) functional education for girls and women; (iii) improving women's access to justice and eliminating discrimination, and (iv) gender parity in decision making and exposing women's issues. The government also regulates and ensures the implementation of laws by the state agencies and the standardization of national gender statistics. The strategy and action plan pay special attention to the role of women in agriculture, including the use of information and communication technology with a view to facilitating their better access to employment and business opportunities. Women's participation is also emphasized in the areas of education, health, and women in nutrition education and agrotechnology.

### 5. Development of the Gender Framework and Government’s Proclamations of gender equality

Researchers and development practitioners point out that Kyrgyzstan faces high gender inequalities. Deep-rooted cultural norms and practices, such as the preference for sons over daughters within the family and society serve to perpetuate gender inequality. While women's councils exist at the rural municipality level to address gender issues and support women, many are not strong. Some argue that gender equality is a priority in Kyrgyzstan but that progress has been slow. Women's participation is lagging behind as barriers to their equal employment opportunities and entrepreneurship development continue to be high. In 2014, the proportion of women in parliament was only 35%, while women (14,743 sons for men, compared to 11,125 sons for women). While both men and women take agricultural land into account when making decisions about household income generation, women are more likely to borrow as a group. A recent study revealed that men are usually the primary decision-makers in households with regard to the purchase of food, clothing, and other goods; the sale of crops and the sale of agriculture and livestock products while women have more agency over non-cash assets such as land, livestock, and savings. Women also play a significant role in the care and influence (i.e. kitchen garden).

### 6. Violence against women is widespread, and there are reported cases of domestic violence, bride kidnapping, early marriages, and physical abuse. In rural areas, in particular, bride kidnapping and early marriages remain a matter of great concern. In its 2014 review of Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, the Kyrgyz Republic noted that 35% of women aged between 15-49 years old in mixed-ethnic areas are accomplished through bride kidnapping, of which two thirds are performed without the consent of the girl's family. The practice is most common among ethnic Uzbeks. More than 12% of women in Kyrgyzstan get married before the minimum marriage age of 18. The incidence of such marriages is highest (30%) for women aged between 15-19 years old, and this is more common among poor households (16.5% than in rich families). Although more updated information is not available, the proportion of early marriages among women aged 20–24 in Kyrgyzstan rose from 17–18 in 2006 to 7.7 children in 2012 – may indicate that early marriages are on the rise.

According to a population and health survey in 2012, 27% of all households are headed by a woman (see Table 3 below). Similarly, according to the National Socioeconomic Survey (NSEC) data for 2014, 25.8% of households were headed by women. The NSEC data suggests that in single-parent families with children under five years old and where there is no other adult present or living in the household, the headship of the household is not a strong factor to influence the poverty level of the family. The NSEC data suggests that in two-parent families with children under five years old and where there is another adult present or living in the household, the headship of the household has no significant effect on the poverty level of the family. According to data from the Annual Report of the National Bank of the Kyrgyz Republic (2015), quoted in FAO (2016).

### Women in Agriculture and Natural Resource Management

9. Agriculture underpins the lives of almost all adults engaging in some work related to crop and livestock production. Rural women work in crop cultivation, livestock production, feed processing and marketing. In terms of formal labour, agriculture is the largest sector of employment for both women and men. The share of women employed in agriculture has increased from 40% in 2013, according to the statistics[1]. It must be noted that the share of women employed in agriculture has increased by almost 5 percentage points since 2012 (from 35% to 40%). The share of men working in agriculture was about 40% during the decade leading to 2012, it increased to 46.5% in 2014[2]. This may have been due to a combination of factors such as an increase in the number of male-headed households and migration. It could be also possible that this reflects women’s limited employment opportunities in other sectors.

10. Employment patterns between women and men in agriculture differ: Women are more likely to be self-employed, while men are more often engaged as hired (contracted) workers. According to the NSC data[3], 75% of self-employed women worked in agriculture in 2014, compared to 58.4% of all self-employed men.

11. Women’s equal rights to properties, including agricultural land, are legally protected. The Land Code (1996) and the Law on Land Use and Land Code of Kyrgyz Republic (1993) are the main documents

[1] NKC cited in FAO (2014).

[2] International Federation for Human Rights, Women and children from Kyrgyzstan affected by migration: An exacerbated vulnerability. Data from the Russian Federal Migration Service, quoted in FAO (2016). Total number of Kyrgyz citizens among the official population of Russia is 357,890.

[3] Data from J-Nails Assessment of Women Migrant Workers: Central Asia and Russia, UN Women, 2009, cited in FAO (2016).

[4] Migration data.

CS-FOR  
Gender Assessment  

regulating land relations and establishing the grounds for the commencement procedure for the exercise and termination of land titles. The Law on Agricultural Land Management (2006) evolved systems to validate and register individual rights to lands shares by dividing land plots in lots into individual parcels for independent ownership. The law recognizes women's right to inherit land, whereas they have no clear right to inherit land. Only 3 percent of rural women have land titles, whereas there are only 22 percent of rural men who own land. About 60 percent of rural women do not own any land[1].

### 2. Recognition and protection of women's rights in Kyrgyzstan

Women's rights to land and other natural resources is a challenge for women due to the prevailing notion that males are natural heads of the household. Land is usually owned by men, and women have no legal right to inherit or own land. The formal law in Kyrgyzstan has not in most cases been successful in protecting and improving women's rights, including rights to access and own land[2]. For example, although women who leave their birth families are entitled to receive a share of the family property upon divorce, this is rarely enforced as such a demand is seen as shameful[3]. Women who are divorcing have similar challenges in claiming their land shares. In Kyrgyzstan, women's rights to land and other natural resources remain weak due to challenge patriarchal traditions about property rights.

### 3. A participatory approach for gender equality

A participatory approach that involves the participation of women in the project area, including those with women and women's groups are not considered as separate groups that have special needs, for which special attention is required. A major obstacle to this approach has been the lack of awareness among men about the importance of the participation of women in the management of natural resources. In communities, the public role of women is reduced due to their heavy domestic responsibilities and their limited access to education related to the upbringing of children and motherhood. Often women are not informed about meetings held on pasture and livestock issues[4].

### 4. In spite of this...

In spite of this, the participants of the discussion note that in recent years there has been opportunities for women's participation in the management of natural resources at all levels. Despite the fact that there are no women in the forestry and pasture committee, especially in the management body, women's committees often become involved in the discussion of problems in the use and management of pastures.

### 5. Livestock Women's engagement in animal husbandry is significant. Women are particularly active in raising livestock such as sheep, goats, and horses. They also participate actively in managing livestock which graze animals in distant pastures during summer would travel with the husbands and children, maintaining them and selling their products[5]. Women play a major role in the management of dairy in animal raising and other economic and livelihood activities of the family[6]. However, women's participation in the livestock sector is limited to the production of milk and meat. The majority of women who keep livestock production primarily a male-led activity. Official data on individual ownership of livestock are unreliable because women often do not have access to land or credit for livestock production in mixed households (56%), but there is no significant difference in terms of the number of livestock owned by each type of household[7].

### 6. Women's involvement in pasture management is also limited.

A recent study on gender and pasture management points out that traditionally women's rights to pastures are secured through their male kin members, who have the right to use them for grazing animals or other purposes. Women's participation in the executive committees (Pasture Committees or PC) of Pasture Users Unions (PUUs) is very low[8].

# CS-Forester Assessment

Of 454 PILAs in total in 2018, only 11 (2.4%) had PIs shared by a woman**. The aforementioned study argues that gender investment levels to reflect more of men’s values, which are related to ownership of the grazing areas and focuses on infrastructure, such as roads and bridges. On the other hand, services and goods primarily for women during the families’ stay at the pasture land, such as electricity, drinking water, childcare support and health care.

## Forests: The National Action Plan on Forestry Development for 2006 to 2010** addressed gender issues in forestry development. However, the plan did not achieve its gender-related goals (goal 2.4) due to weak implementation of the action plan. Gender-related goal (goal 2.4) was not achieved because the National Action Plan on Forestry Development for 2006-2010 did not achieve its gender-related goals and AE can use this case as an opportunity to learn in order to improve the gender mainstreaming process of the project.

### First of all, the National Action Plan on Forestry Development for 2006-2010 does not address the following two facts that do not attract youth and women. Secondly, the responsibilities for the implementation of gender-responsive policies was left on international projects (not specified solely), without the involvement of the state authorities and local communities. Thirdly, the National Action Plan on Forestry Development for 2006-2010 does not provide clear mechanisms and tools. Fourth reason, lack of monitoring and control over the implementation of gender-responsive policies. Fifth reason, the absence of a separate budget line for the Gender and Social Inclusion Plan; assign clear responsibilities in ToRs of EG and PILA staff. A fifth reason is the lack of coordination between the Ministry of Agriculture (MoA), Food Industry and Meatpacking (FIM) and its relevant departments. State Agency for Environment Protection, Forestry and Nature Reserves Councils, communities who support the project and/or in-planning monitoring and implementation phases.

### Studies found typical leaseholders of forest lands are older males; and women and young men have limited access to forest resources. The study also found that women and young men lack sufficient information, as well as participating in meetings organized by EFS or community authorities due to their domestic responsibilities and the fact that they do not have a say in land use and management**.

Another gender bias was identified in collaborative forest management, which was designed for small-scale users. The study found that women were excluded from this scheme according to a study**. The model did not work due to several factors, such as small plot size and labour requirements. Women were also excluded because they were seen as being incapable of handling labour requirements or lacking sufficient resources to carry out the task.

### Non-timber forest products (NTFPs) are important sources of income and household consumption among families living near the forest land; particularly for FPHs and poor households. FPHs gather firewood, fruits, nuts, mushrooms, honey, and other NTFPs from the forest land**. However, in Kyrgyzstan, many women access forest land resources without any agreement or only with informal agreements. Poor tenure security is a major barrier to women’s access to forest lands. Women are allowed by leases to collect leftover nuts and fruits for free under a system called mahsalt.

21. It is argued that, generally speaking, women are disproportionately affected by impacts of climate change and related weather events due to existing discriminatory norms and practices that impede equal access to resources and decision-making power. The World Bank report (World Bank, 2014) states: "The report also points out that gender-gap effects on life expectancy tend to be higher in severe disasters, and climate-sensitive health issues are more prevalent among women than men."

22. Women's unequal participation in the decision making processes also constrains engendering of the planning and implementation of adaptation strategies. This is because women have a critical role particularly in the resilience and mitigation actions by contributing to resilience building and livelihood improvement.

23. A 2013 publication on gender and climate change in Kyrgyzstan points out a substantial institutional gap between the climate and gender discourses, and a subsequent lack of gender mainstreaming in climate policies and programs. This is because women are not adequately represented in discussions about climate change effects and other aspects of the ecological context leads to a lack of a clear picture of the distribution of risks for different groups of people. In addition, the absence of gender-sensitive data collection and analysis and representation is common at local level institutions, such as local councils, village administration. Water User Associations (WUA) are also not adequately represented in decision-making processes.

24. The aforementioned Livelihood Study found that women put secure access to water – both in pasture and farm land – as the high priority action for climate change mitigation.


## Youth

25. Kyrgyzstan is a young country with nearly 30% of its total population in Kyrgyzstan under the age of 25. Youth (aged between 15 and 29) account for 27%. According to an ILO survey, Kyrgyzstan has the highest youth labour underutilization rate (55.3%) among six countries in the region[4]. Female youth labor underutilization is even higher than male youth labor underutilization. The proportion of female youth is highest in the region (79.5%), and as many as 46.6% of young workers in the country are engaged in the agricultural sector. Younger children, having longer working hours than young men, are also more likely to be employed in agriculture[5]. This is a picture of typical Kyrgyz rural youth working on family farm as informal labour force with little prospect for gaining formal employment and access to social services.

Stakeholder Engagement

27. Stakeholder engagement is a process that must be undertaken during the project formulation as well as throughout the project implementation phase. The first stakeholder engagement events took place during the field mission which were held in Kyrgyzstan from March 2017 – April 2018. Different formats for stakeholder engagement process (workshops, meetings, structured consultations) were used and (co-)organized by CS-FOR with the support of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry, local government representatives, NGOs, CSOs, donors, private sector). The main objectives of these events were to: identify gaps in the current situation; identify gender gaps within the agricultural sector at the country level; identify gaps and lessons learned; and set priorities for the formulation of a project proposal. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) Gender Action Plan (GAP) was used as a reference document during Project Formulation that was held in Bishkek on March 29-30, 2017. The State Agency on Environmental Protection and Forestry (SAEFP) under the Government of the Kyrgyz Republic (KR) jointly with the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) were responsible for working on preparation of a project proposal for the GCF identified gender gaps and actions to fulfill the gaps[10].

Gender gaps:

- Management of households by women from vulnerable groups of the population.
- Lack of women on senior positions.
- Low representation in decision-making bodies.
- Mismatched in the profiles of specialists and in public authorities.
- Gender gap in the agricultural sector.
- Mismatched profile specialties (forestry faculty and other specialties).

Actions (approaches) to fulfill the gaps:

- Capacity building of specialists. Evidence-based training, Program monitoring and evaluation.
- Work with decision-makers (parliament, ministries).
- Implementation of gender equality measures in the promotion of investments in agriculture.
- Data collection (Data disaggregated by sex) methodology.
- Inclusion of women in agricultural extension services, etc.
- Strengthening of the value chains for processing products.

Based on work of the second day, it was decided to include the results of the working groups in the project proposal. The gender gap is formed as it was highlighted in the previous paragraph is strongly related to gender stereotypes that leads to imbalance in the positions of specialists and in decreasing positions (very low level of women's involvement) in such sectors as agriculture, forestry, WUAs, PUsIs.

28. At the district-level consultations participants suggested including members of women council or female representatives should be members of the Community Landscape Management Group (CLMG). One of the groups stressed that at least 30% of the membership should be women[11]. Moreover, it was noted that there is a gender gap in the positions of specialists and in decreasing gender imbalance that is quite representative and illustrated the gender misbalanced situation within the country. The gender gap in the agricultural sector is about 25%, while in forestry it is about 10%. Gender gap is formed as it was highlighted in the previous paragraph is strongly related to gender stereotypes that leads to imbalance in the positions of specialists and in decreasing positions (very low level of women's involvement) in such sectors as agriculture, forestry, WUAs, PUsIs.

Target area:

29. Vulnerability to climate change was the most important parameter for the target area selection. The design team collected a large number of data sets at national and sub-national scale and using the Earth Map tool, performed a series of vulnerability analysis to identify project's core target areas[56]. Target areas have been selected based on:

a. Exposure of ecosystems and communities to natural hazards triggered by climate change;

b. Vulnerability of ecosystems and communities to climate change;

c. Mitigation potential in terms of forest and pasture rehabilitation;

d. Human vulnerability, including access to water resources and food security; and

e. Socio-economic vulnerability of communities.

30. The core intervention area of the CS-FOR project will be located in selected rural municipalities (ayl yamak) in four contiguous districts of Ak-Talas, Toguz-Toro, Suzuk and Uzgen. Ayl yamaks are local self-government bodies that consist of several villages. In total there are 10 ayl yamaks within the project area[57]. The municipalities are organized into three administrative levels: municipal (ayl kengesh), which are represented only by one (8%). Each rural municipality has several villages. Suzuk is the largest ayl yamak with 24 villages, followed by Toguz-Toro and Uzgen with 19 villages each in Suzuk; 22% in Uzgen), as well as small percentages of Turkistan (1.9% in Suzuk; 3.1% in Uzgen). Uzbek households are the largest group within the project area, followed by Kyrgyz and Tajik.

31. Livelihoods. Rural households in the target area have several sources of income, most important of which are livestock production and crop farming. According to the livelihood study conducted during the project preparation phase, 90% of the households in the project area own livestock (on average 1.5 – 1.56 sheep/cattle per household) and 42% have cultivated agricultural land[58]. Among the households with irrigated agricultural land, those in Uzgen-Talas have smallest plots (37 ha) compared to those in Toguz-Toro (90 ha), Suzuk (100 ha) and Ak-Talas (142 ha).

Most of the households have homesteaded gardens (often called kitchen garden) in the size of 0.5 – 0.17 ha, where they grow vegetables and fruits for their own consumption[59]. The average area cultivated is among the households in the project area is 5.0 ha[60]. Two-thirds of the households who lease forest land have livestock grazing rights on these lands.

32. Livestock owning households have on average 3 – 4 cattle and about 20 sheep[61]. Among the households with livestock, 78% own cattle and 95% own sheep. The majority (80%) of the households in State Land Fund (SLF)[62] during summer in contrast to 11.1% who answered that their animals graze in State Forest Fund (SFF)[63]. A great majority of the livestock owners who send their animals to summer pastures (74.5%) rely on shepherds, while 15.2% answered they graze animals by themselves.

The data and analysis that identified the characteristics of the projected target areas were organized in form of an atlas which presents the potential for project implementation[64]. The atlas includes a series of maps showing the distribution of livestock (sheep/cattle), the presence of irrigated ecosystems (forests and pastures, and communities). The atlas provides key information such as climate variables, land use/land cover, soil types, water resources, etc. The atlas also contains data on the socio-economic characteristics of the target area. It should be noted that while the validity of the paradigm will not be objectively demonstrated. The atlas does include also available historical data on livestock production and crop farming in the project areas.

Livelihood Study for CS-FOR collected data from 907 respondents, 650 of which were in the project area (Uzgen, Toguz-Toro, Suzuk and Ak-Talas). The study was conducted during June-July 2013. The results are based on interviews with households and focus group discussions.

The figures are shown on median values of those from own agricultural land, disaggregated at district level.

Results of focus group discussions (FGDs) in the Livelihood Study revealed that forest resources are primarily used by households in the Susat and Ujung districts as the main source or an additional source of income[1]. The use of forest resources in comparison with the management of agriculture is of greater interest to these resource-poor households because it provides a more important part of their income, especially for women. Collecting such forest resources (e.g., nuts, apples, mushrooms) can be an important part of their income, especially for women. 34. Vulnerable households. The Livelihood Study and the survey conducted by the surveyed households by the economic activity on which the household's welfare depends livestock production, crop farming forest resource collection, fishing, or a combination of these activities (e.g., livestock production and agriculture). In addition, qualitative information was obtained through interviews and focus group discussions. The results indicates that households whose welfare depends on the use of forest resources are vulnerable to climate change and natural disasters. This is because they have limited access to other sources of income. Most of the household data found a relatively higher percentage of vulnerable households (households with low income, unemployed women, and young people) in the target area compared to the rest of the country. Those that receive seasonal income are particularly at risk. Less vulnerable households are those in which at least one member has an alternative source of income.

35. Disadvantaged groups. The same study found that unemployed people (both women and men), low-income households, young people, and women who have no land or livestock were in a disadvantaged position in the rural communities due to their weak social and economic standing. It was highlighted such disadvantaged groups may further weaken their social and economic situation if their access to natural resources decreases or natural disasters intensify.

Target Groups

36. The primary target group of the CS-FOR are all the die-faco users (both women and men) of pasture and forest resources (both formal and informal). In this area, the target group for the intervention will be the poor households who live in the pastures and forests. These include members of poor families, women and young women and men (for specific project actions to ensure their inclusion and participation), and other vulnerable groups such as unemployed people. The project aims to benefit about 90,000 rural families in the target area will directly benefit from the project as the primary target group.

37. In addition, the project will benefit the following categories of people:

* **The Ministry of Environment and Forestry (MENAFOR),** the State Agency for Environmental Protection and Foresty (SAEPF), the Ministry of Agriculture, Food Industry and Mechanization (MAFM), the Ministry of Energy and Mineral Resources (MEMR), the Ministry of Public Works and Transportation (MPPTK), the Ministry of Social Affairs and Family Relations (ALSIGR), the Climate Finance Center (CFCC), the Ministry of Economy (ME), the Ministry of Education and Culture (MEC), the National Forestry Agency (ANF), national and local government institutions and NGOs, with the focus on those, specializing in rural women's empowerment. Institutions at local level: Leskhotes (Forest Enterprises), National Parks, self-government bodies (districts, villages), research, women's councils, Pasteur Units, Unions and other natural resources users groups.

# Gender Strategy

## CS-FOR: Gender Assessment

### Direct beneficiaries of the project include 432,450 individuals (7% of the country's population) who will be women in the Project Area. By reducing the exposure of rural communities to natural disasters such as landslides, mudslides and floods, the project will also include 540,563 (8%) of the country's population. Individuals of whom 280,121 are women, an indirect beneficiaries in the Project Area, who will benefit from the reduction of exposure to natural disasters and other hazards.

### CS-FOR, while promoting positive shifts in the natural resource management through policy instruments, capacity building and investments for restoration on the ground, presents a major opportunity to address gender equality. The project is informed by Gender Assessment (Section 8) which recognizes that rural women in Kyrgyzstan play a key role in forest and land use activities. They are responsible for household food security, income generation and adaptation/mitigation measures. It also reflects the understanding that women's equal participation in decision-making processes can be achieved through a set of specific measures, including those related to leadership and decision-making skills.

### The gender strategy is also framed by the FAO Policy on Gender Equality (2015), the FAO Environmental and Social Management Guidelines (2014) and the National Forest Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests in the Context of National Food Security (2016).

### Underlying principles and key features of the gender strategy of CS-FOR are as follows:

- Women's equal participation in decision-making processes will be achieved through a set of specific measures.
- For this purpose, pro-active measures will be taken to address existing barriers that could prevent women from opportunities.

#### References:
1. FAO (2015) National Forest Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests in the Context of National Food Security. Available at: [http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4413e.pdf](http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4413e.pdf)
2. FAO (2015) Environmental and social management guidelines. Available at: [http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4413e.pdf](http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4413e.pdf)
3. FAO and UNDP (2016) National Forest Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests in the Context of national food security. Available at: [http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4413e.pdf](http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4413e.pdf)

CS-FOR

Gender Segment

Limit women’s opportunities to benefit from the project, and where considered necessary a revised women’s segment will be set.

- the project, through the inclusive participation of stakeholders will support the strengthening of a national policy on gender equality, in collaboration with MLSD, targeting such areas as livelihood adaptation, natural resource management and disaster prevention and warning – both at community level and at household level.
- project will target female headed households and women to support the opportunities to participate at decision-making levels: in the executive committees (Pasture Committees or PCs) of Pastoral communities.
- women’s informed engagement in decision making processes on related matters (e.g. livelihood adaptation, natural resource management and disaster prevention and warning) – both at community level and at household level.
- opportunities for women’s social and economic empowerment, as well their leadership and decision-making opportunities will be supported.
- needs for women’s capacity enhancement on relevant topics will be addressed and acted upon, and all trainings will take gender issues into consideration in the modules, selection of participants and content delivery. The project will also address other related gender issues (including transportation needs, timing, baby-sitting services etc). Needs assessment will be part of the baseline survey to be conducted at community level.
- all project stakeholders will be sensitized and trained on the importance of gender mainstreaming under the project and of specific GAP actions:
    - gender equality and mainstreaming are adequately introduced to the target communities, project staff and other relevant stakeholders. The project will ensure that all project documents consider aspects and use gender-sensitive languages.
    - each unit or sub-unit of the project will include a qualified personnel (Gender and Social Development Specialist) who oversees gender mainstreaming in the project and GAP implementation.
- knowledge management of the project mainstreams gender, and the project will monitor and evaluate gender-differentiated outputs and outcomes through sex-disaggregated M&E indicators and other tools.

43. Inclusion of the poor, youth and other disadvantaged groups: The Livelihood Study and stakeholder consultations informed that certain groups in the rural community are socially disadvantaged, particularly women and young people. These groups have limited access to resources due to their weak social and economic standings[1]. The project therefore incorporates special actions to ensure their equal participation in all aspects of the project. This will be achieved by ensuring that project information and benefits, particular attention will be paid to promote their engagement in business related opportunities and supported value chains by proactively including them in business related capacity building activities.

Similar to the gender strategy above, the project will raise awareness among stakeholders on the risks of exclusion and marginalization of these groups. The project will also take steps to ensure their informed participation through a set of specific actions. The project manager will take the full responsibility for ensuring that all project staff are aware of this issue and are fully committed to its implementation. All members of the project team will be held accountable for gender mainstreaming and social inclusion, and will be technically assisted by the Gender and Social Development Specialist (part of the PMU).

[1] Stakeholder consultation meeting for Uzgen and Suzak districts involved Uzbek community leaders.
